You receive ALL core documents of ONE jurisdiction, each page-delimited by lines `=== DOC n PAGE p ===`, with a document list (doc_id, type, year, staff-research flag). Work only from the texts; no outside knowledge of the project. Fill the matrix strictly under the contract: absence of a finding = value n/d, evidence_state unknown; a later decision of the competent body supersedes earlier claims on the same matter (name the superseded finding); staff research never establishes adopted/operational; 'the central bank will not use/see the data' is not 'cannot link'; F2 may be inferred only if each of the contract's three premises is documented for the same participant, mode and design version; `confidence` high|medium|low; unused qualifier fields 'n/d'; `superseded` = 'doc n p. m' or ''.\n\nCELLS AND ALLOWED VALUES (in order):\n- F1 CB_LINK_ONLINE: yes / no / n/d\n- F2 PSP_LINK_ONLINE: yes / no / n/d\n- F3 LAWFUL_ACCESS_ROUTE: via_PSP / via_CB / via_both / none_stated / n/d\n- F4 PRIVATE_OFFLINE_AVAILABLE: yes / no / n/d\n- F7 LOW_ID_ROUTE: yes / no / n/d\n- F8 ONBOARDING_ID: yes / no / n/d\n- F9 TARGETED_REIDENTIFICATION: yes / no / n/d\n- F10 PROGRAMMABLE_RESTRICTIONS: yes / no / n/d\n- F11 payment_modernisation: yes / no / n/d\n- F11 financial_inclusion: yes / no / n/d\n- F11 monetary_sovereignty_transmission: yes / no / n/d\n- F11 cash_substitute_resilience: yes / no / n/d\n- F11 aml_cft_tax_enforcement: yes / no / n/d\n- F11 innovation_competitiveness: yes / no / n/d\n- F11 primary_status: one_primary / several_joint / unranked / conflict / n/d\n- F11 primary_objectives: free text\n- F12 LEDGER_TECHNOLOGY: conventional_centralised / permissioned_DLT / permissionless_DLT / hybrid / undecided / n/d\n\nTHE CONTRACT (binding definitions):\n# MEASUREMENT CONTRACT v1.1 — CBDC privacy dataset / paper v11\n\nStatus: **development version 1.1, dated 18 October 2026 (evening)**; supersedes v1 of the same day after the first LLM fills of Norway and the UK and the authors' review (changes in §12). v1 was adopted after four rounds of independent review (12 Oct, 17 Oct ×2, 18 Oct 2026). It is frozen only after the worked profiles (Norway, United Kingdom) and the contrasting development cases have been coded independently by both authors and the definitions below have been corrected where the two codings diverged. Until then every rule may change; every change is logged in §12. Authors: Martin Bok, Dominik Stroukal. Technical collaborator: Claude.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Construct and unit\n\n**Construct.** Publicly documented, current institutional commitments on the privacy and identifiability architecture of a retail central bank digital currency (CBDC), as of the **as-of date 2026-10-18**.\n\n**Adopted commitment** = a concrete design choice announced by the body competent to decide for the project (central bank governing body, ministry, legislature, or a common authority of a monetary union). It need not be implemented or legislated. An option under consideration is not a commitment. A statement conditional on issuance (\"if a CBDC is issued, the central bank will not see…\") *is* a commitment about the design, unless the document presents it as one option among several.\n\n**Unit of analysis** = project under one decision authority. The euro area (Eurosystem digital euro), the ECCU (ECCB) and WAEMU (BCEAO) are one project each; national documents of member states are sources for that project, never separate observations. All other jurisdictions in core_v1 are separate projects unless a document shows a common authority.\n\n**Scope of evidence** = `core_v1` (frozen 2026-10-18; 109 documents, 56 jurisdictions; `CORE_V1_FREEZE.json`). Layers: official (default); `staff_research = Y` (author-views disclaimer or staff series: never establishes an adopted commitment; its factual description of a pilot may corroborate a cell); wholesale-only documents (flagged, outside the primary retail analysis); background legal layer (still-effective law published before 2018, usable only for F3/F5 legal basis, flagged `background_layer = true`).\n\n**Time.** Current-state matrix with a change log. A later decision of the competent body on the same matter supersedes an earlier one; a later general speech does not supersede an earlier specific rule. Ended projects keep a historical profile marked `historical = true`.\n\n## 2. Retrieval labels (not measurements)\n\nPRIV / TRACE / DESIGN / OWN_PROJECT from the encoder rounds remain **retrieval and description labels**. They say where to look. They never set a cell value.\n\n## 3. Atomic claim schema\n\nOne paragraph yields zero or more claims. Fields:\n\n| field | status | content |\n|---|---|---|\n| `project_id` | inherited | from registry |\n| `document_id`, `paragraph_id`, `page` | inherited | locator |\n| `authority` | inherited, overridable | issuer and its competence (central bank board / staff / ministry / parliament / consortium); `staff_research` inherited from registry |\n| `publication_date` | inherited | from registry; `effective_date` optional |\n| `feature_id` | core | F1 … F12 (F3 is a single descriptive cell in v1.1; F5/F6 reserved) |\n| `asserted_value` | core | per feature (§4) |\n| `claim_status` | core | `option` (considered) / `adopted` (commitment) / `tested` (capability in pilot/PoC) / `operational` (live arrangement) |\n| `quote` | core | verbatim span that establishes value **and** status; source language, with English gloss if needed |\n| `actor` | conditional | CB / operator / PSP / public authority (which) / user; **mandatory** where the feature distinguishes actors (F1, F2, F3, F9); payer vs. payee stated where the source distinguishes them |\n| `transaction_scope` | conditional | online / offline / wallet tier / limit band; **mandatory** where the value depends on mode (F1, F2, F4, F7) |\n| `limits` | conditional | amounts, counts, time windows; **mandatory** for F4, F7 when the claim is tiered |\n| `modality` | conditional | unconditional / conditional (state the condition); **mandatory** when a condition changes the meaning (F1, F3, F9) |\n| `derivation` | core | `explicit` / `inferred` (list premises and the rule from §6) |\n| `provenance` | core | `human:MB` / `human:DS` / `model:<name>` / `model+human` |\n| `legal_state` | conditional | `in_force` / `adopted_not_effective` / `intended` / `n/d`; **mandatory only** when a value for F3 or F5 is derived from a legal instrument |\n| `history` | derived | per cell, the dated sequence of (publication year, document, asserted value, claim_status) over all documents of the project; derived automatically from the claims, never coded by hand (§6.8) |\n\nRule: **every qualifier needed to derive the cell value must be recorded, unambiguously inherited, or marked `n/d`.** A claim missing a decisive qualifier can only support evidence state `partial`.\n\n## 4. Feature definitions (cells)\n\nEach cell = `value` · `evidence_state` (documented / partial / unknown / conflict / n.a.) · `claim_status` (highest status among supporting claims) · `derivation` · `provenance`. **Absence of mention = unknown, never \"no\".**\n\n| ID | question | values | decisive qualifiers | traps |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| **F1 CB_LINK_ONLINE** | Can the issuing central bank / core-system operator, in the standard online mode, directly or through a routinely available link attribute a transaction to a person? | yes / no | actor = CB or operator; scope = standard online mode | \"CB will not *use* the data\" ≠ cannot link. Extraordinary re-identification → F9, not here. Pseudonymisation at the CB counts as *no* only if the CB holds no routine key to identity. |\n| **F2 PSP_LINK_ONLINE** | Can the user's payment service provider routinely attribute an online transaction to a person? | yes / no | actor = PSP; scope; payer vs payee | KYC at onboarding does **not** imply F2 = yes (§6). Bearer/token designs where the PSP sees no transaction record → no. |\n| **F3 LAWFUL_ACCESS_ROUTE** (descriptive, v1.1) | Does the institution document a route by which a public authority can obtain person-attributable transaction data, and through whom? | via_PSP / via_CB / via_both / none_stated / n/d (+ named authority in `actor`, process in `modality`) | actor = which authority; modality = legal process named; legal_state when derived from law | Descriptive only: no channel split, no aggregate, no outcome. \"Same rules as for bank accounts\" or \"lawful basis under existing law\" is a route via_PSP only if the PSP is named as the holder; otherwise n/d with the sentence quoted. Suspicion-based STRs are a route (via_PSP). Never coded ROUTINE/TARGETED: that distinction depends on law outside the corpus (v1 experience, NO and UK). |\n| **F4 PRIVATE_OFFLINE_AVAILABLE** | Is an offline mode an adopted commitment in which individual transfer details are not routinely transmitted to or linked with a person by the CB or PSP, including at later synchronisation? | yes / no | limits; synchronisation behaviour; claim_status | Offline offered but privacy undescribed → unknown. Tested capability ≠ adopted. An adopted decision *not* to offer offline → no. |\n| **F5 INDEPENDENT_ACCESS_GATE** | Does targeted public-authority access require prior authorisation by an independent body under an applicable adopted rule? | yes / no / n.a. | legal_state | **reserved in v1** (schema only) |\n| **F6 NO_COMMERCIAL_REUSE_WITHOUT_CONSENT** | Is commercial use of PSP transaction data without separate user consent prohibited by an adopted rule? | yes / no | — | **reserved in v1** |\n| **F7 LOW_ID_ROUTE** | Is there an adopted retail mode in which an eligible user can hold/pay up to a stated limit without verified personal identity (including verification via another account)? | yes / no | limits; onboarding; link to an identified account | KYC-lite that still verifies identity ≠ no identity. Never labelled \"anonymity\". |\n| **F8 ONBOARDING_ID** | Does opening a standard wallet require verified personal identity? | yes / no | wallet tier | Descriptive only; **not** a premise for F2. |\n| **F9 TARGETED_REIDENTIFICATION** | Can an online transaction be attributed to a person afterwards under defined extraordinary conditions? By whom, under what condition? | yes / no + actor + condition | actor; modality | Prevents reading F1 = no as absolute. Descriptive only. |\n| **F10 PROGRAMMABLE_RESTRICTIONS** | Does the adopted design allow issuer/state-imposed restrictions on use (purpose, expiry, blocking) beyond AML? | yes / no / unknown | — | Must distinguish state-imposed restrictions from user-requested conditional payments. Lower priority; dropped if the pilot shows it overloads coders. |\n| **F11 STATED_OBJECTIVES** | Which objectives does the institution declare, and did it explicitly rank one as main? | `stated_objectives` = set of flags {payment_modernisation, financial_inclusion, monetary_sovereignty_transmission, cash_substitute_resilience, aml_cft_tax_enforcement, innovation_competitiveness}, each with a quote; `primary_status` = one_primary / several_joint / unranked / conflict / n/d; `primary_objectives` only when ranking is documented | — | \"CBDC will comply with AML rules\" is a design requirement, not an objective. A zero flag means \"not documented in the checked sources\", not absence of motive. Word frequencies may go in an appendix as \"linguistic emphasis\"; they never set the flags. |\n| **F12 LEDGER_TECHNOLOGY** (descriptive, v1.1) | Which ledger technology does the design use or has it chosen? | conventional_centralised / permissioned_DLT / permissionless_DLT / hybrid / undecided / n/d; `platform` free text (e.g. Hyperledger Besu, Corda, custom); `claim_status` decides whether the value is a test or a choice | claim_status; transaction_scope (online core vs offline component) | Tested platforms in a PoC are `tested`, never adopted. \"Technology-neutral\" statements → undecided. Permissionless is expected to be empty among adopted designs (stated in advance). Descriptive, outside the type and Y; external criterion: comparable fields in the BIS (Auer–Cornelli–Frost) and Atlantic Council trackers are used for a concurrent-validity check of the extraction, never to set the value. |\n\n## 5. Typology (Layer 1, architecture) — deterministic\n\nOnline axis: **A** = F1 yes; **B** = F1 no & F2 yes; **C** = F1 no & F2 no. Offline axis: **0** = F4 no; **1** = F4 yes. Six cells A0 … C1; **C0/C1 are expected to be empty** among adopted designs (stated in advance). Missing input → partial assignment (e.g., `B?` = {B0, B1}); F1 unknown → `?`. The type is nominal; it is never coded 1–6 and never correlated. F3, F7–F12 do not enter the type.\n\n## 6. Aggregation and derivation rules\n\n1. Only `adopted` / `operational` claims from official-layer documents set an adopted value; `option` and `tested` claims set the value only with `claim_status` carried along and are never reported as commitments. Staff-research claims corroborate but never establish.\n2. Supersession: later decision of the competent body on the same matter wins; a general speech never overrides a specific rule; store the superseded claim with `superseded_by`.\n3. Conflict between two adopted claims of equal standing → `evidence_state = conflict`; both stored; adjudication by the authors (not by a model); result and reasoning logged.\n4. Citation counts are not votes.\n5. **Inference is allowed only as a necessary conclusion from documented premises, with premises and rule attached.** F2 may be inferred = yes only when all three hold and are documented: (i) the PSP holds an identifier → person mapping, (ii) the PSP routinely sees the relevant transaction record, (iii) the PSP can link the two in the same mode for the same participant. \"Intermediated model + KYC + customer relationship\" alone is insufficient (counter-example: Project Tourbillon). Every inferred cell is flagged; the report shows which types change when only explicit cells are used.\n6. *(v1.1)* F3 has no aggregate. The route value is set by the most specific documented statement; conflicting routes across documents → `conflict`.\n6a. *(v1.1)* **F7 inference rule.** F7 = no (`derivation = inferred`) when F8 = yes is documented for the standard wallet and no document of the project mentions any other tier, channel or route with lower identification (premises: the F8 claim + the absence statement, both recorded). Otherwise absence of mention = unknown.\n7. Missing decisive qualifier → partial.\n8. *(v1.1)* **History.** For every cell the pipeline derives `history` = the dated sequence of (year, document, asserted value, claim_status) from all claims of the project, in publication order; the current value is the last adopted/operational claim under the supersession rule, and the sequence is reported with claim_status so that \"tested 2023 → undecided 2026\" is not read as a reversal. History is a derived output, never hand-coded, and is reported descriptively (change map) only for projects with ≥ 3 dated documents.\n\n## 7. Outcomes (Layer 1 → Y) and purpose layer (Layer 2)\n\n- **Y₁** (primary empirical illustration, conditional): Y₁ = 1 ⇔ F1 = no (adopted); 0 ⇔ F1 = yes; else undetermined. Used as primary only if the pilot meets the orientation rule: ≥ 20 determinable projects and minority class ≥ 6. It measures separation of the central bank from transaction identity, **never** unavailability of data to the state.\n- **Y₂ dropped in v1.1.** F3 is descriptive only; no outcome and no hypothesis is built on public-authority access (reason: §12).\n- **Y₃**: Y₃ = 1 ⇔ F7 = yes; 0 ⇔ F7 = no (explicit, or inferred under §6.6a and flagged); else undetermined. Results are reported with and without inferred zeros.\n- Layer 2 (F11) is descriptive; a pre-specified cross-tabulation architecture × declared objective and one pre-specified co-occurrence (share of F7 = yes among projects declaring financial inclusion) are descriptive results, never causal.\n\n## 8. Naming\n\nNo country-level label such as \"state control\". `aml_cft_tax_enforcement` only when explicitly declared as an objective. F3 is reported as \"documented lawful-access route\", never as a level of access. Types are A0–C1; Y are binary features. No \"explains / drives / effect of\"; \"is associated with\", \"co-occurs with\".\n\n## 9. Coder protocol (humans)\n\n*(v1.1)* **Development projects** are filled first by the API pipeline (§10) and by an independent second-provider run; both authors then verify every cell against the cited page in a review workbook (value, evidence state, claim status, decisive quote, justification, counter-evidence, `quote_verified`) and record whether a disagreement is a model error or a definition problem. Development verdicts are development material, never the human reference. **Blind test projects** are coded by both authors **independently from the PDFs**, without sight of any model output, filling one matrix workbook each (one row per cell: value, evidence state, claim status, derivation, document, page, quote, qualifiers, note). Original independent codings are preserved before adjudication. Per project the workload is: F1, F2, F4, F7, F8, F10 (yes/no with unknown/partial/conflict allowed), F3 (route), F9, F11, F12. F5/F6 reserved. The keyword finder shipped with the workbook lists page references only, never values.\n\n## 10. Machine protocol (API extraction, later)\n\nTwo LLMs from different providers, called through the API with no chat history, receive this contract (§3–§6) as the instruction and return claims in the schema of §3 as JSON, with `provenance = model:<name>`. *(v1.1)* The pipeline is `scripts/extraction/extract_api.py`; both its modes are logged and compared on the development projects: `two-stage` (per-document findings → synthesis from findings only) and `single` (all documents of the project in one request, where the provider's context allows). The mode used for the frozen instrument is fixed before the freeze on the basis of the development comparison, not of the test split. Development on a dev split of human-coded projects; at most five prompt versions, each a dated file with its dev metrics; the test split is scored once with the final prompt. Model–model disagreement → human adjudication; agreements and empty outputs are audited too. Whether extraction runs on all core paragraphs or on encoder-retrieved paragraphs is decided by cost before the freeze; bge-m3 is retained for sample selection and the language ablation.\n\n## 11. Validation (summary; details in PREDICTIONS_v2.1 and the audit plan)\n\nDevelopment: worked profiles (Norway, UK) → contrasting cases (euro area, Canada staff-heavy, Chile/Brazil non-English, Hungary discontinued) → definitions corrected → freeze → OSF registration with decision record. Confirmation: one final audit of the frozen pipeline (stratified, weighted; family-specific recall; feature/type/Y error against the human reference), audit of DROP documents and ingestion, independent full-source reading of all project profiles by both authors.\n\n## 12. Change log\n\n- 2026-10-18 **v1.1** (development), after the authors' review of the Norway and UK LLM fills: (1) **F3 reduced to one descriptive cell** `LAWFUL_ACCESS_ROUTE`; channels and the aggregate removed; **Y₂ dropped** as an outcome, together with the H1/H2 tests on Y₂ in PREDICTIONS — reason: in both worked profiles the aggregate was `partial` and both fills showed that the ROUTINE/TARGETED distinction rests on law outside the corpus (NO: \"AML rules the same as for other payment services\"; UK: \"lawful basis\" under existing data-protection law). (2) **F7 inference rule** §6.6a (F8 = yes and no lower-identification route mentioned → F7 = no, inferred, flagged). (3) **F12 LEDGER_TECHNOLOGY** added as a descriptive cell with an external concurrent-validity criterion (BIS and Atlantic Council trackers); permissionless kept as a value, not a feature, because it is expected to be empty. (4) **`history`** derived per cell (§3, §6.8) for the change map. (5) §10: single vs two-stage API mode compared on development projects. Cell count: 20 → 15 coded cells (F1, F2, F3, F4, F7, F8, F9, F10, F11 ×8, F12) plus computed TYPE/Y.\n\n- 2026-10-18 v1 (development): created from the reviewer's specification of 17 Oct and the authors' amendments A1–A8 (18 Oct), with the two corrections of the fourth review (F2 no-inference rule; F3 partial rule) and the purpose layer as non-exclusive flags with `primary_status`.\n\n### 12.1 Addendum 18 Oct 2026 (after the first LLM fills of NO and UK)\n\n- **Development fills are not instrument output.** Fills produced by interactive agents (session-bound, tool-driven reading, no reproducible request record) are development material for revising definitions. Instrument output is only what `scripts/extraction/extract_api.py` produces: two-stage API extraction (per-document findings → synthesis from findings only), with model id, contract/prompt/script SHA-256, raw request/response and token usage logged per run.\n- **Temperature.** The Anthropic API rejects `temperature` for current models; sampling is provider-controlled. Reproducibility is recorded as model id + prompt hashes + raw response, not as a temperature setting. The same is recorded for any second provider.\n- **Mechanical citation check.** Every quote in instrument output is checked against the cited page of the page-delimited text (`verify_quotes.py`); the `quote_verified` column is part of the output. A quote not found on the cited page (±1) voids the claim for audit purposes.\n- **Development vs. blind test.** Jurisdictions whose LLM fills the authors have seen (NO, UK, and any later development jurisdiction) are excluded from the sealed blind test set; the test set is drawn from the remaining core jurisdictions and coded by the humans without sight of any model output.\n"

A CBDC and the Settlement Finality Directive .............................................47\nReferences ....................................................................................................................48\nAnnex: Monetary policy and demand for CBDCs .......................................................52\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                                                                        3\n\f\n=== DOC 1 PAGE 4 ===\nPreface                                                                                     NORGES BANK PAPERS\n                                                                                            NO 1 | 2018\n\n                                                                                            CENTRAL BANK\n                                                                                            DIGITAL CURRENCIES\nA number of central banks are considering whether they might in the future issue a\ncentral bank digital currency (CBDC) for general purpose users. A CBDC of this kind\nis a central bank liability denominated in the official unit of account and will be a\nsupplement to cash.\n\nTechnological advances have brought this issue to the fore. A decline in cash usage\nhas prompted us to think about whether at some future date a number of new attributes\nthat are important for ensuring an efficient and robust payment system and confidence\nin the monetary system will be needed. If the answer is yes, a CBDC may be an\nappropriate measure for remedying weaknesses that may otherwise arise.\n\nThis report, prepared by a Norges Bank working group, provides an overview of\naspects that should be given weight in assessing whether Norges Bank should issue a\nCBDC. A CBDC can be designed in various ways, depending on the desired aims.\nThe working group points in particular to three possible purposes that merit further\nconsideration:\n\n    -   To ensure a public and credit risk-free alternative to deposits in private banks,\n        in addition to cash.\n\n    -   To function as an independent back-up solution for the ordinary electronic\n        payment systems.\n\n    -   To ensure the existence of suitable legal tender as a supplement to cash.\n\nNorges Bank will continue to issue cash as long as there is demand for it. But when\ncash usage declines, a CBDC can be an alternative to deposit money. The primary\npurpose of a CBDC is to ensure confidence in money and the monetary system.\n\nIt is not Norges Bank’s ambition to take over credit provision from banks. A premise\nunderlying this work is that the existence and scope of a CBDC must not impair the\nability of banks and other financial institutions to provide credit.\n\nA CBDC raises complex issues. There is virtually no international experience to draw\non. Further analysis is needed to assess the purposes of a CBDC, the types of solutions\nthat best achieve these purposes and the benefits measured against financial and other\ncosts. This is a long-term undertaking. The aim of publishing the working group’s\nreport is to inform the public about its work, disseminate knowledge and initiate a\ndialogue with stakeholders.\n\n\n\nØystein Olsen\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                            4\n\f\n=== DOC 1 PAGE 5 ===\n1. Introduction and summary                                                                                                  NORGES BANK PAPERS\n                                                                                                                             NO 1 | 2018\n\n                                                                                                                             CENTRAL BANK\n                                                                                                                             DIGITAL CURRENCIES\nA central bank digital currency (CBDC) is a digital form of central bank money\ndenominated in the official unit of account for general purpose users. A CBDC can\ntake several forms with different properties, depending on its purpose.\n\nA Norges Bank working group1 has completed an initial phase of a study of CBDCs.\nThe working group’s report provides an overview of the issues that it regards as\nrelevant in an assessment of whether to introduce a CBDC in Norway.\n\nA CBDC raises substantial and complex issues. CBDCs have not been introduced in\nany country with a fully developed financial system, and there is thus little experience\nto draw on. A number of central banks, including Sveriges Riksbank, are now\nconsidering whether a CBDC is necessary and desirable.2 Technological advances and\nreduced cash usage are two forces that have made this issue currently relevant. From a\npurely technological standpoint, however, it was possible to introduce a CBDC earlier.\n\nCBDC – what and for whom?\nIntroducing a CBDC in Norway – as a supplement to cash – would entail offering a\ndigital liability on Norges Bank for use as a means of payment and store of value. A\nCBDC would also entail the creation of dedicated payment solutions that Norges Bank\nwould have full or partial responsibility for, but would not necessarily operate and\nmaintain.\n\nA CBDC does not mean that Norges Bank will provide credit to the general public.\nThat role will still be played by banks and other financial institutions. Depending on\nits design, a CBDC may entail a more active role for the central bank in providing\ncredit to banks and in some situations in helping to fund bank lending.\n\nThe primary target group for any CBDC will be the general public in Norway: private\nindividuals, associations, organisations, non-financial enterprises and government\nauthorities.\n\nWhy a CBDC?\nAny decision for Norges Bank to take the initiative in introducing a CBDC must be\nbased on a socio-economic cost-benefit analysis. Important elements in such an\nanalysis will be the impacts on the payment system, financial stability and monetary\npolicy.\n\nInternationally, a number of arguments have been made for introducing CBDCs. The\nworking group finds that the relevant question for Norges Bank will primarily be\nwhether a CBDC is necessary and desirable for ensuring an efficient and robust\npayment system and confidence in the monetary system. Confidence in the monetary\nsystem means that we trust that the value of money will remain stable over time. This\nconfidence is supported by low and stable inflation, the ability to make payments\nsafely and efficiently and the certainty that money is genuine and its issuers are\nsolvent and will honour their commitments.\n\n\n1\n  The working group comprised Knut Sandal (chair), Farooq Akram, Ragna Alstadheim, Lasse Ask, Tom Bernhardsen,\nKathrine Stang Ottesen, Ylva Søvik, Leif Veggum, Peder Østbye, Terje Åmås and Steinar Årdal. Kjetil Heltne\nparticipated in an early phase. The project’s steering group comprised Jon Nicolaisen (chair), Ida Wolden Bache, Olav\nBø, Torbjørn Hægeland and Marius Ryel. Kristin Gulbrandsen participated in an early phase.\n2\n  See Skingsley (2016), Sveriges Riksbank (2017) and Ingves (2017) regarding work at the Riksbank. See CPMI\n(2018) for an overview of the international discussion of CBDCs. In general, countries differ in their financial structure\nand thus to some extent also differ in their approach to the issue of CBDCs.\n\n                                                                                                                             5\n\f\n=== DOC 1 PAGE 6 ===\nThe following questions must be asked:                                                        NORGES BANK PAPERS\n                                                                                              NO 1 | 2018\n\n    -   What characteristics do we want the payment system to have in the future?             CENTRAL BANK\n                                                                                              DIGITAL CURRENCIES\n\n    -   Is there a risk that important characteristics will disappear and confidence in\n        the monetary system will weaken, unless Norges Bank and/or other authorities\n        take action?\n\n    -   If yes, is a CBDC the best instrument for ensuring desired characteristics?\n\n    -   Will a CBDC have other, undesirable consequences?\n\nAn important trend in the payment system is a gradual decline in cash usage. Cash\nwill be with us for many years to come. However, it is possible that, at some point,\ncash usage will be so low that it can no longer be considered a generally available\nmeans of payment. It is not Norges Bank’s aim to eliminate cash, either with the aid of\na CBDC or in some other manner. The use of cash should be determined by demand.\n\nCash has a number of important attributes:\n\n    -   It is a credit risk-free alternative to deposit money (bank deposits). Bank\n        deposits can readily be converted into physical cash, which in and of itself\n        may sustain public confidence in bank deposits and the monetary system.\n        Cash also helps sustain competition among means of payment. Credit risk-free\n        does not mean that cash is free from risk or other costs.\n\n    -   It is an independent back-up solution if electronic systems fail. Cash is not\n        dependent on technology or a third party at the time of payment.\n\n    -   It is legal tender that can be used by anyone.\n\n    -   The use of cash is not traceable.\n\nIf cash is marginalised as a generally available means of payment, bank deposits will\nnot be fully capable of ensuring that these attributes continue. Bank deposits are now\nthe predominant means of payment and must be assumed to be virtually the only\nalternative if cash usage becomes very low. E-money and crypto-assets are hardly\nviable alternatives in the near and medium term.\n\nIt is the opinion of the working group that attributes such as credit risk-free alternative\nto deposit money, independent back-up solution and legal tender are the most\nimportant ones that may be lost. However, further analysis is needed to assess the\nusefulness of introducing a CBDC with all or at least some of these attributes.\n\nWhat these attributes have in common – perhaps to some extent with the exception of\nback-up solution – is that users barely take note of them when choosing a means of\npayment. Nevertheless, these attributes may be important to society.\n\nCash entails some “frictions” in the form of logistics and the risk of loss and/or theft\nfor the person holding physical cash. Thus, it not a fully satisfactory alternative for\npersons who wish to withdraw bank deposits on a large scale. An important question\nis whether it is regarded as desirable to offer a more “frictionless” alternative to\ndeposit money and what consequences this may have.\n\n\n\n                                                                                              6\n\f\n=== DOC 1 PAGE 7 ===\nWhile the discussion of the other attributes generally concerns matters of principle, the                     NORGES BANK PAPERS\nquestion of an independent back-up solution is more of a practical issue. Two                                 NO 1 | 2018\ntechnically wholly independent payment systems based on bank deposits can\n                                                                                                              CENTRAL BANK\nconceivably serve as contingency arrangements for each other. The question is                                 DIGITAL CURRENCIES\nwhether regulation of private financial market infrastructure (FMI) owners will ensure\nthe existence of independent contingency arrangements and, more generally, what\nstrategy is most appropriate for achieving this.\n\nThe need for a CBDC can also be assessed on the basis of other areas important to\nNorges Bank and to Norway. These include monetary policy space, the possibility of\ncurrency substitution (the use of currencies in Norway other than the Norwegian\nkrone) and the impacts on seigniorage (a source of income for central banks and/or the\npublic sector because cash is a “cheap loan”). The working group has not found\narguments in these areas that provide an essential justification for introducing a\nCBDC. The impact in these areas of any CBDC should nevertheless be included in a\ncost-benefit analysis.\n\nPrimary models for CBDCs\nThe work on models for CBDCs has made little headway. The assessments below\nshould therefore be considered preliminary and can serve as a starting point for further\nwork. In the assessment of the working group, there are primarily two relevant models\nfor organising a CBDC system:\n\n        -    In an account-based model, both value storage and transaction processing are\n             centralised. Money is thus held in accounts and moves from one account to\n             another in the system.\n\n        -    In a value-based model, value storage and processing are decentralised.\n             Money is thus stored locally in a payment instrument, typically a card or\n             smart phone app. Transfers take place directly between parties, without the\n             intermediation of a central third party.\n\nHybrid variants that combine elements of both primary models are also possible. An\nexample is a model where money is stored locally, but transactions need to be verified\nby a third party with a register of all transactions and holdings.3\n\nA third model, which can also be considered a hybrid variant, is based on distributed\nledger technology (DLT). In a DLT system, the transaction register is distributed\namong all network participants, with transactions taking place directly between end\nusers. DLT has potential, including for contingency purposes because the technology\nis independent and robust to the loss of individual elements. However, the working\ngroup does not consider DLT to be a relevant technology for a CBDC in the near and\nmedium term. The technology is generally immature. This suggests proceeding with\ncaution. The DLT systems examined so far have far lower processing capacity and\nspeed than modern centralised payment systems. This may improve in new versions of\nthe technology. The central bank must be able to control the CBDC system, so that if\nDLT is adopted, adjustments would have to be made to a purely decentralised system.\n\nThe two primary models have different strengths and weaknesses. Within each model\nthere are a number of properties and functionalities that need to be chosen. The\nimpacts of a CBDC will depend on its specific design. In general, the functionalities\n\n\n\n\n3\n    Value-based and hybrid models are often grouped together under the term “token-based”, see CPMI (2018).\n\n                                                                                                              7\n\f\n=== DOC 1 PAGE 8 ===\nof a value-based model are largely similar to those of cash, while an account-based        NORGES BANK PAPERS\nmodel resembles more closely ordinary bank deposits.                                       NO 1 | 2018\n\n                                                                                           CENTRAL BANK\nAn account-based model:                                                                    DIGITAL CURRENCIES\n\n\n    -   can be adapted to all payment methods, including those used for online\n        shopping and paying bills,\n\n    -   offers secure value storage and is suited to payments of any size,\n\n    -   has the limitation that it is dependent on contact with a central third party to\n        execute and settle payments; in isolation, this weakens its suitability for\n        contingency purposes,\n\n    -   may have adverse impacts on banks, financial stability and the\n        implementation of monetary policy. The impacts depend on the design,\n        including the interest paid on the CBDC and frictions in flows between bank\n        deposits and CBDC.\n\nA value-based model:\n\n    -   is local and thus not dependent on communication with a third party for\n        making payments. This makes the model robust and particularly well-suited\n        for contingency purposes,\n\n    -   will be able to address privacy concerns as payments are not traced or\n        recorded in a central database,\n\n    -   is not optimal for storing or paying large amounts, since the money is lost if\n        the payment instrument is lost or damaged.\n\nThe assessment of the models depends on what is deemed to be the primary purpose\nof introducing a CBDC. The working group’s preliminary conclusion is that a value-\nbased system has some advantages as a back-up solution. An account-based model has\nsome advantages in being able to offer a real risk-free alternative to deposit money as\na store of value. With regard to the purpose of providing an alternative means of\npayment to bank money and promoting competition, both models have their strengths\nand weaknesses.\n\nPrinciples for IT architecture and costs\nThe principles for IT architecture from the Agency for Public Management and\neGovernment (Difi) are common guidelines for all public sector IT work. Possible\nsolutions for a CBDC must be assessed on the basis of scalability, interoperability,\naccessibility, security and flexibility. Our preliminary assessment is that it will be\npossible to realise both primary models in line with these principles for IT\narchitecture.\n\nThe financial costs associated with both primary models would be substantial, in terms\nof both establishment and operation. Costs depend, among other things, on\ntechnology, functionality and security and on organisational matters such as\noutsourcing and system ownership. Cost estimates will not be possible until such\nmatters have been clarified.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                           8\n\f\n=== DOC 1 PAGE 9 ===\nNorges Bank’s role                                                                                                     NORGES BANK PAPERS\nNorges Bank must have control over and primary responsibility for any CBDC                                             NO 1 | 2018\nsystem. For a value-based system, it should be considered whether this responsibility\n                                                                                                                       CENTRAL BANK\ncan be defined in the same way as in the cash area currently. For an account-based                                     DIGITAL CURRENCIES\nsystem, Norges Bank would probably have to have the ultimate responsibility for the\ncore infrastructure, but not necessarily for the payment services that can be built on\ntop of it.\n\nNone of the primary models requires Norges Bank to have direct customer contact. A\nnumber of factors possibly suggest that, operationally, Norges Bank will not have to\nbe responsible for the technical development and daily operation of any CBDC\nsystem.\n\nSeveral types of service provider are relevant. There can only be one provider of the\ncore of the solution, since there may only be one account structure or register for a\nCBDC. For the services to customers, there may be a large number of providers.\n\nPublic demand for CBDC\nFor a payment system4 to be maintained it must be widely adopted and used. The\ngeneral public must be sufficiently familiar with CBDC in order to use it, for example,\non the day a contingency situation arises.\n\nUsers’ assessments of a CBDC compared with other means of payment – and\nassociated payment instruments – will determine demand for and thus the volume of\nCBDC. Users will likely give weight to confidence in means of payment, functionality\nand the total costs for users themselves. This also means that demand may change\nconsiderably if situations arise that change these assessments. Demand is also affected\nby users’ purposes for using a CBDC.\n\nFactors that can influence demand for CBDC for payment purposes:\n\n        -    What needs are met by the solution, what payment situations it can be used in\n             and how widely adopted it is.\n\n        -    How well it functions and whether it is perceived as attractive and secure.\n\n        -    How simple and inexpensive it is to convert CBDC into bank deposits and\n             vice-versa.\n\n        -    Which operators are given access.\n\nFactors that can influence demand for CBDC for value storage purposes:\n\n        -    Whether there are limitations on the amount of funds it is possible to store.\n\n        -    The relative return on stored amounts.\n\n        -    The security of storage – operational risk/risk of theft.\n\n        -    How simple and inexpensive it is to convert CBDC into bank deposits and\n             vice-versa.\n\n\n\n4\n    A payment system comprises any means, systems or instruments that can be used to execute or facilitate payments.\n\n                                                                                                                       9\n\f\n=== DOC 1 PAGE 10 ===\n    -   Which operators are given access.                                                 NORGES BANK PAPERS\n                                                                                          NO 1 | 2018\n\n    -   Which other (government-issued) assets exist that are liquid and can be easily    CENTRAL BANK\n        used for value storage.                                                           DIGITAL CURRENCIES\n\n\nConsequences for banks, financial stability and monetary policy\nTransferring bank deposits to accounts with the central bank or to a rechargeable card\nor mobile app can affect the central bank’s balance sheet and liquidity management. It\ncan also have an impact on banks’ balance sheets and funding, the structure of the\nbanking sector and monetary policy.\n\nSome possible consequences, particularly with an account-based model with few\nfrictions between bank deposits and CBDC, are:\n\n    -   Large-scale withdrawals of bank deposits can occur. Such bank runs can\n        potentially take place around the clock without limit and will be independent\n        of geographical proximity (unlike the case today with cash).\n\n    -   The introduction of a near substitute for bank deposits may motivate banks to\n        raise deposit rates and lead to a shift from deposit funding to wholesale\n        funding. This may also result in increased credit provision by non-bank\n        financial institutions.\n\n    -   A substantial reduction in the demand for deposits may reduce bank lending\n        and hence economic growth, unless other participants become important\n        providers of credit.\n\n    -   If demand for CBDC becomes very high, Norges Bank runs the risk of having\n        to fund a large proportion of banks’ assets, particularly loans.\n\n    -   The interest rate on CBDC may set a floor under all short-term market rates\n        and a CBDC may also result in runs on non-bank financial institutions.\n\n    -   The central bank may become a direct competitor to payment service\n        providers. This may have impacts on the earnings of banks and other payment\n        providers.\n\nThe bullet points above suggest choosing a model that limits the impacts on the\nfunctions performed by the banking system. These impacts can be limited if Norges\nBank chooses a value-based model or an account-based model with frictions between\nbank deposits and CBDC.\n\nLegal matters\nAn account-based CBDC solution where accounts are held in Norges Bank will\nrequire amending the provisions in the Norges Bank Act concerning which entities\nmay hold an account with the Bank. A value-based solution will probably not require\namending the Norges Bank Act, as long as the solution is within the customary or\nnormal remit of a central bank. This will be influenced by developments at other\ncentral banks and in society at large. In any case, the working group finds that a\ndecision to introduce a CBDC is so important that it will also involve other regulatory\nauthorities.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                          10\n\f\n=== DOC 1 PAGE 11 ===\nConclusion and the way forward                                                             NORGES BANK PAPERS\nIt is too early to conclude whether Norges Bank should take the initiative in              NO 1 | 2018\nintroducing a CBDC. The impacts of a CBDC – and the socio-economic cost-benefit\n                                                                                           CENTRAL BANK\nanalysis – will depend on the specific design. The design, in turn, will depend on the     DIGITAL CURRENCIES\npurpose of introducing a CBDC. The working group points in particular to three\npossible purposes, which require further analysis:\n\n    -   A credit risk-free alternative to deposit money.\n\n    -   An independent back-up solution for the ordinary electronic payment systems.\n\n    -   Legal tender, if cash can no longer be regarded as “generally” available, as\n        legal tender is normally understood to be.\n\nOn the other hand, the working group has not identified issues allowing it to conclude\nat present that introducing a CBDC can be ruled out. The working group has identified\na number of factors that suggest caution, particularly in order to avoid conversion of\nbank deposits into a CBDC that is so rapid and so extensive as to impair lending.\n\nThere is thus a need to examine the purposes of a CBDC and the most relevant\nsolutions in more detail than permitted by the scope of this study. This will also make\nit possible to elaborate on the impacts of a CBDC and the cost-benefit analysis. This is\nplanned for the project’s second phase. A premise underlying this work is that the\nexistence and scope of a CBDC must not impair the ability of banks and other\nfinancial institutions to provide credit.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                           11\n\f\n=== DOC 1 PAGE 12 ===\n2. Why a CBDC?                                                                                                  NORGES BANK PAPERS\n                                                                                                                NO 1 | 2018\n\n                                                                                                                CENTRAL BANK\n                                                                                                                DIGITAL CURRENCIES\n2.1. About money and means of payment\nMoney\nMoney has three basic functions:\n\n        -    First, money is a means of payment that can be used to transfer value\n             between parties, for example in connection with the purchase of a good or\n             service.\n\n        -    Second, money is a practical store of value.\n\n        -    Third, money is a unit of accounting that facilitates comparison of the value\n             of different goods and services. Norway’s units of accounting are the øre and\n             the krone.\n\nA CBDC must fulfil all three of these functions. The working group has proceeded on\nthe basis that a CBDC must be denominated in the Norwegian krone and have the\nsame value as other means of payment denominated in the Norwegian krone.\n\nThe money we currently use has no significant “physical” value. Rather, it represents\na receivable from the issuer. Potential issuers include central government (central\nbanks) and private enterprises. The value of money depends on public confidence that\nmoney will function as intended, i.e. that it will fulfil the basic functions. If money can\nonly be used in a small selection of stores, its function as a means of payment will be\nweakened. If there is a risk that money may lose its value, for example due to issuer\ninsolvency or high inflation, confidence in money as a store of value will be\nundermined.\n\nMeans of payment and payment instruments\nDifferent means of payment represent receivables from different operators. Means of\npayment are utilised through payment instruments. At present, the public’s primary\navailable means of payment are cash, bank deposits and E-money; see Table 1. A\nfurther means is private crypto-assets (also referred to as “cryptocurrencies”, “digital\ncurrencies” or “virtual currencies”), such as Bitcoin. These do not represent a\nreceivable from any party, and constitute separate units of account.5\n\n\n\n\n5\n    See chapter 2.5.2 for a brief discussion of how well crypto-assets function as money and payment systems.\n\n                                                                                                                12\n\f\n=== DOC 1 PAGE 13 ===\nTable 1. Most mobile payments, for example through the Vipps\n                service, involve the charging of a payment card. Recently, the Vipps\n                service has also begun offering immediate account-to-account\n                settlement.\n\n           -    Giros are used to pay bills and make transfers, and are usually actioned\n                in an online or mobile banking solution.
