Several central\nbanks have considered this in greater detail; see CPMI (2018) and Barontini and Holden (2019) for an\noverview.\n4\n  Riksbanken has also decided to develop a technical pilot for a CBDC to gather experience; see\nRiksbanken (2018).\n5\n  Mancini-Griffoli et. al. (2018), CPMI (2018) and Barontini and Holden (2019) provide an overview of the\nwork of different central banks. Sedlabanki Islands (2018) expands on the assessments conducted by\nSedlabanki.\n\n                                                                                                            10\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 11 ===\nRedundancy describes the availability of two or more solutions capable of taking over      NORGES BANK MEMO\nfor one another, for example if one solution suffers operational problems. A seamless      NO 2 | 2019\ntakeover is only possible if the solutions mirror one another and either run in parallel\nor are ready to do so immediately. For example, two identical operational locations        CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                           CURRENCIES\ncan be set up such that production can be switched between them.\n                                                                                           SECOND REPORT OF\nSeamless redundancy is beneficial in terms of accessibility, but not necessarily a good    WORKING GROUP\nback-up solution. Mirroring means that both solutions could be equally affected by an\nerror. Examples include software errors and corrupted data.\n\nMoreover, a back-up solution has to be protected against the errors to which\noperational solutions are vulnerable. Back-up solutions are typically not “hot”, do not\nrely on the same technology and other infrastructure, and are situated sufficiently far\naway in geographical terms. An alternative is to adopt multiple, entirely independent\npayment solutions to function as back-ups for one another.\n\nAccordingly, both redundancy and back-up solutions are required. Situations may\narise in which adjustments have to be made to such solutions based on a cost-benefit\nanalysis. However, the requirement of a satisfactory back-up solution can never be\ndropped in respect of critical infrastructure required to meet society’s payment needs.\n\nEffective electronic back-up solutions are vital for rapid re-establishment of the\npayment system following a stoppage. Cash is part of the overall back-up solution in\nthe event of any failure in electronic back-up systems.\n\nAs pointed out by Norges Bank in its reports on financial infrastructure, the current\nback-up solution for the payment system has deficiencies.\n\nIn general terms, the most important measures to reinforce the back-up solution for\npayments are as follows (not in priority order):\n\n    1. Regulate private payment solutions by\n            a. imposing requirements on the owners of specific payment solutions\n            b. regulating and monitoring critical payment system suppliers\n            c. requiring back-up solutions for general solutions, such as terminals.\n    2. Ensure the continued availability of cash by regulatory and other means, by\n            a. requiring banks to offer cash deposit and withdrawal functionality in\n               any contingency situation; see section 16-4 of the Regulations relating\n               to the Financial Institutions Act\n            b. as part thereof, requiring back-up solutions for ATMs\n            c. requiring banks also to offer cash services in non-contingency\n               situations\n            d. requiring or urging the public to hold some cash and a stock of basic\n               necessities (thus reducing the need to make payments); see the\n               contingency list maintained by the Norwegian Directorate for Civil\n               Protection.\n    3. Introduce a CBDC system which is operationally independent of ordinary\n       payment systems. Any CBDC system could also be an instrument for\n       ensuring national control if payment infrastructure is relocated outside\n       Norway.\n\nThe present report does not include a detailed assessment of contingency situations\nand the most appropriate measures or combinations of measures to be implemented.\n\nThe assessment as to whether introducing a CBDC is desirable for contingency\nreasons must be based on a socioeconomic cost-benefit analysis of a specific\nsolution, and include a comparison with alternative measures. Any analysis of a\nCBDC solution should include the requirements that it can function independently of\nbank payment systems and can facilitate offline payments for a certain period of time.\n\n\n                                                                                           11\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 12 ===\nAs a formality, we would note that this discussion relates primarily to technical back-        NORGES BANK MEMO\nup solutions. Back-up solutions for bank deposit-based systems will not remedy a               NO 2 | 2019\nsituation where confidence in the banking system is greatly reduced or lost.\n                                                                                               CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                               CURRENCIES\n\n2.3. Credit risk-free alternative                                                              SECOND REPORT OF\n                                                                                               WORKING GROUP\nThe Norwegian banking system can be split into two levels. The first comprises private\nbanks and the public. The second consists of private banks and Norges Bank.\n\nOn the first level, private banks provide banking services to the public in the form of\ndeposits, loans and payment services. Through their lending activities, they create\ndeposits (deposit money). This form of money is currently the dominant means of\npayment used by the public in Norway.\n\nNorges Bank is the bank of banks. On the second level of the banking system, Norges\nBank provides banking services to private banks in the form of deposit and lending\narrangements. Norges Bank is the only issuer of central bank reserves, the means of\npayment used in inter-bank settlement. By providing loans and deposit facilities for\ncentral bank reserves, Norges Bank helps alleviate some of the liquidity risk incurred\nby banks when providing banking services to the public. The public, on the other\nhand, has no access to central bank reserves.\n\nCash represents a deviation from this two-level model, in that it is the only means of\npayment issued by Norges Bank to which the public has access. The public is free to\nchoose between bank deposits and cash. Both can serve as both a means of payment\nand a store of value.\n\nHowever, cash usage is declining. Cash accounts for a steadily decreasing proportion\nof money supply, and is less used in payment transactions than previously.\nNevertheless, even if demand falls, cash may be significant with regard to public\nconfidence in the monetary system.\n\nA key question is to what extent bank deposits already meet the need for a secure\nand generally available means of payment. This currently appears to be the case in\nNorway. Regulations, deposit protection and Norges Bank’s role as a source of bank\nliquidity6 are likely to provide the public with access to a secure means of payment\neven if cash disappears. Businesses’ cash deposits will often exceed the deposit\nprotection maximum. Deposits from small and medium-sized enterprises enjoy\npreferential treatment in the context of crisis management and public administration,\nrendering credit risk negligible for these entities. On the other hand, credit risk may be\nreal for large businesses. However, in practice cash holdings are not a solution for\nbusinesses seeking to avoid credit risk.\n\nDeposit protection and crisis management of banks are not dependent on the\nexistence of cash. If a bank goes bankrupt, deposits covered by the deposit protection\nscheme will in most cases be paid into other bank accounts, not disbursed in cash.\nThis may create liquidity risk for the account holder, in that a few (but no more than\nseven) days may pass before money is disbursed. Given how rarely such situations\nhave arisen, however, such liquidity risk does not appear to threaten the position of\nbank deposits as a secure means of payment. Moreover, the continued decline in\ncash usage in Norway, even following a financial crisis, may indicate that the public\nlargely has confidence in deposit money.\n\nMost Norwegians have access to a bank account and associated technology like\npayment cards and payment apps for mobile telephones. Some people, such as the\nelderly, may feel that such technology is difficult to use in practice. However, a CBDC\n\n\n6\n    Norges Bank does not issue loans to banks which are, or are likely to become, insolvent.\n\n                                                                                               12\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 13 ===\nwould also demand technical solutions. The fact that some individuals encounter              NORGES BANK MEMO\ntechnical hindrances to the use of bank deposits as a means of payment is a stronger         NO 2 | 2019\nargument in favour of ensuring continued access to cash than in favour of introducing\na CBDC.                                                                                      CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                             CURRENCIES\n\nRetaining the current stability framework based on regulations, deposit protection and       SECOND REPORT OF\nthe central bank as a source of bank liquidity is also associated with costs. These          WORKING GROUP\ninclude both public and private administrative costs and costs resulting from\nunintended regulatory effects. One important unintended effect of deposit protection\nand central bank liquidity facilities is that banks may take on excessive risk – so-called\nmoral hazard. In addition, new systemic risks are likely to arise in future, and\nregulations and protection schemes will often lag behind developments. Nevertheless,\nbank deposits appear to be a sufficiently secure and generally available means of\npayment. Introducing a CBDC will not necessarily eliminate or reduce regulations and\nprotection schemes, or the costs associated with them.\n\n\n2.4. Competition\nAlthough demand for cash is falling, cash may influence competition in the markets for\nmeans of payment and payment instruments by offering users an alternative to\ndeposit money. The strength of this effect is difficult to determine. The markets for\nprivate means and instruments of payment are concentrated, but at present\ncompetition and regulation of these markets appear to be ensuring efficiency.\n\nLow and falling cash holdings may indicate that cash is being out-competed by\ndeposits in terms of its function as a store of value. Even though cash is most\ncompetitive against deposits when interest rates are close to zero, cash holdings have\ncontinued to decline despite historically low rates. In addition, the public has shown\nsome willingness to accept negative interest rates on deposits.\n\nThe use of cash as a payment instrument is also falling, and competition from new\nelectronic payment solutions is likely to be more important for the efficiency of this\nmarket going forward. Regulatory provisions like the revised Payment Services\nDirective (PSD2) are intended to foster competition in the payment instruments\nmarket.\n\nAt the same time, it must be concluded that the existence of cash is helping to keep\npayment service prices down. Electronic payment services are characterised by\neconomies of scale and network effects which could limit competition. This also\napplies to banking systems in Norway, and cash is in many respects the only current\nalternative. Since we have no experience of an entirely cashless system, it is difficult\nto conclude as to the size of this effect. Regulating access to cash can help maintain\ncompetition for certain types of payments, but cannot expand the scope of competition\nto include other types of payments. A CBDC, on the other hand, could achieve this.\n\nIntroducing a CBDC could potentially boost competition in the market for storage of\nmeans of payment. However, if no interest is paid on the CBDC, it will not increase\ncompetition with bank deposits at normal interest rate levels (the return on bank\nmoney makes it more attractive than the CBDC). Conversely, if interest were to be\npaid on the CBDC, competition could also be boosted in this market at normal interest\nrate levels. Increased competition for deposits would mean higher financing costs for\nbanks. Depending on its design and Norges Bank’s pricing of its share of the services\ninvolved, a CBDC could also intensify competition in the payment instruments market.\nGenerally speaking, competition would also be felt in other areas than price/interest\nrate – primarily user-friendliness and fulfilment of user needs.\n\nIt is uncertain how any future loss of cash which is not replaced by a CBDC would\nimpact on competition. At present, the effect of cash on competition appears limited,\nalthough we lack an adequate basis for a definite conclusion. This applies not only to\n\n\n                                                                                             13\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 14 ===\ncompetition linked to bank deposits, but also to the effect on competition in the market                     NORGES BANK MEMO\nfor payment services. If cash disappears, by and large only bank payment solutions                           NO 2 | 2019\nwill be left.7 Depending on market developments, there is a risk that eliminating cash\nwill undermine competition and provide scope for monopolistic/oligopolistic gains.                           CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                                             CURRENCIES\n\nThe present situation, in which there is a generally available means of payment in the                       SECOND REPORT OF\nform of bank deposits, can probably be maintained by regulatory means and inter-                             WORKING GROUP\nbank competition. The public sector has a role to play in protecting the access of\nvulnerable groups to private-sector payment services, and in ensuring that cash can\ncontinue to be used.\n\n\n2.5. Legal tender\nThere are good reasons for having a means of payment which is given the status of\nlegal tender. This provides the parties to a payment settlement with a fall-back\nsolution in case they cannot agree on a payment method. In principle, the parties are\nfree to decide how payment should occur. The rule on legal tender, see section 3-5,\nfirst paragraph, of the new Norges Bank Act,8 applies where parties have not agreed a\npayment method. At present, Norges Bank’s notes and coins are mandatory means of\npayment. This means that, in the absence of a specific agreement to the contrary,\neither party to an agreement may demand that payment be made in notes and coins\non the due date, and the other party has no right to object to this. This allows the\nparties to avoid the effects of a breach of contract. Alternatively, the payment method\nmay be regulated by law or regulation, in which case the provision on mandatory\nmeans of payment does not apply.\n\nA rule specifying a fall-back solution in case of disagreement (a fall-back rule) will\npromote efficiency by resolving disputes. In the absence of such a fall-back solution,\ndisagreement about the payment method will in practice be resolved by the creditor\nusing his or her right to issue instructions, see section 38(2) of the Financial Contracts\nAct, which provides that, “[t]he payee may give further instructions concerning the\nmethod of payment, provided this does not entail a substantial additional expense or\nother inconvenience for the payer.” The existence of a fall-back rule on legal tender\nhelps ensure that a payee cannot unilaterally impose a payment method which is\nunfavourable to the payer.\n\nThis is because the right to issue instructions is restricted by the payee not being able\nto dictate payment methods which are less favourable than using legal tender.\n\nFurther, it may be the case that a payer lacks access to the means of payment\nspecified by the payee. In the absence of a fall-back rule on legal tender, one of the\nparties – usually the payer – may be in breach after the due date.\n\nThe existence of a means of payment which the public knows can be used in most\nsettlements is advantageous in itself. If it were to be left to the parties to a payment\nsettlement to agree the means of settlement in all cases, the stronger party to the\nsettlement would be able to force, systematically, the weaker party to accept the\npayment method which is to the stronger party’s advantage. The existence of a\ngenerally accepted means of payment which the parties agree may be used unless\nspecified otherwise gives the assumed weaker party to a payment settlement some\nprotection against enforcement of a disadvantageous payment method. This\nconsideration primarily applies in the consumer context, where it must be assumed\nthat negotiating power is asymmetrical. The rule on legal tender thus holds some\nrelevance for consumer protection. In Norwegian legislation, this is apparent\n\n\n7\n  New, non-bank payment providers are entering the market and may boost competition, at least in the short\nterm. However, network effects and economies of scale may reduce competition in the longer term; see\nalso the discussion in chapter 2.6.\n8\n  See discussion of the new Norges Bank Act in chapter 5.\n\n                                                                                                             14\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 15 ===\nparticularly from the provision in section 38(3) of the Financial Contracts Act, which        NORGES BANK MEMO\nstates that consumers have an unconditional right “to effect settlement with the              NO 2 | 2019\nrecipient of the payment in legal tender”.\n                                                                                              CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                              CURRENCIES\nThe working group has concluded that there are still good reasons for having a rule on\nlegal tender. Under the new Norges Bank Act, cash is retained as legal tender; see            SECOND REPORT OF\nsection 3-5, first paragraph. If a CBDC were to be introduced, it could be given status       WORKING GROUP\nas legal tender alongside cash. A CBDC has all the characteristics legal tender should\nhave. Further, a CBDC could expand the area of application of legal tender, as a\nCBDC can be used for distance payments and can be traced. However, we do not see\nthat the consideration of legal tender alone justifies the introduction of a CBDC at this\njuncture.\n\n\n2.6. Effects of a CBDC and the precautionary\n     approach\nNo complete cost-benefit analysis has been conducted of the introduction of a CBDC.\nNevertheless, we would point out a number of relevant circumstances and competing\nconsiderations.\n\nOn the one hand, we see that a CBDC could:\n\n           function as an independent back-up solution in case confidence in the\n            banking system declines materially or electronic back-up solutions prove\n            insufficient. The consideration of back-up capacity may increase in\n            importance if payment infrastructure becomes more international\n           strengthen competition in the payment market\n           safeguard all the characteristics of legal tender and, if desirable, expand\n            the area of application\n\nOn the other hand, as pointed out in Norges Bank (2018), the introduction of a CBDC\nis associated with financial costs and other potential disadvantages and risks.\n\nConsideration of potential negative consequences\nAn important balancing exercise to be performed by Norges Bank when considering\nthe introduction of a CBDC is between the benefits to the public of being able to hold a\ncredit risk-free means of payment and the potential negative consequences for\navailability of credit in the real economy and management of the central bank balance\nsheet. This balancing exercise is the same regardless of whether the public wishes to\nhold cash, a CBDC or deposits in full-reserve banks. This is described further in\nNorges Bank (2018). At the same time, Norges Bank has differing opportunities to\ninfluence the alternatives.\n\nDemand for such holdings may vary. At present, demand for cash is low, and probably\nfairly interest-inelastic. We do not know how large a premium the public is willing to\npay for an electronic alternative, or how the premium will vary in line with interest rates\nand uncertainty in the financial system. Since the existing full-reserve bank in Norway\nhas not received notable deposits, there are indications that the premium on such\nassets is low, but this may change. While uncertainty also attaches to how banks will\nadapt to lower demand for deposits, deposit rates and the proportion of market\nfinancing appear likely to increase. Although it is not given that such a change will\nreduce the availability of credit in the economy, this possibility cannot be excluded.\n\nThe precautionary approach\nA cost-benefit analysis will not necessarily encompass the precautionary approach.\nBeing well-prepared for the introduction of a CBDC may well be a sound strategy in\ncase the payment system and means of payment develop in an unforeseen direction.\nFor example, a material change could occur in the market structure and stakeholder\n\n\n                                                                                              15\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 16 ===\nprofile as a result of which deposits in private banks in Norwegian kroner and a                NORGES BANK MEMO\nNorwegian-controlled payment system are no longer dominant.                                     NO 2 | 2019\n\n                                                                                                CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\nTwo possible substantial structural changes are:                                                CURRENCIES\n\n\n             relocation of bank payment infrastructure abroad                                  SECOND REPORT OF\n                                                                                                WORKING GROUP\n             stronger competition for banks from international “big-tech” companies.\n\nA relevant example of the first type of change is the P27 initiative focused on a joint\nNordic payment infrastructure. Norwegian banks have recently decided not to\nparticipate. Other initiatives may emerge. If central payment infrastructure operates\noutside Norway’s borders, a new look must be taken at back-up solutions located in\nNorway. Both location and national control will be important considerations in this\nregard.\n\nA further potential development is that major international “big-techs”/platform\ncompanies may become important stakeholders in trading and payments by the\nNorwegian public, in addition to their prominent role in communications and social life.\nThe advantages in terms of networks, access to information and economies of scale\nmay further strengthen the market position of these companies over time. Barriers to\nentry may increase as ever greater investment is required to be able to compete, thus\nreinforcing the market power of the biggest companies.\n\nThe current payment strategy of such companies is to offer a user interface “on top” of\nthe traditional payment system. However, it is entirely possible that companies will\npenetrate ever lower levels of the payment system in the longer term, offering\ncustomers the possibility of depositing money with them and making payments\nthrough the system. One example is Alipay’s business in China. On 18 June 2019,\nFacebook announced plans to launch, in cooperation with selected partners, a\nproprietary monetary unit called Libra, as well as a DLT-based payment system. Libra\nis an example of a so-called \"stablecoin\" solution, where value stability is sought by\nlinking the monetary unit with a basket of currencies. Questions arising related to the\ndevelopment of solutions outside the traditional payment system include whether\npayments will be denominated in Norwegian kroner, how large a proportion of their\nwealth Norwegian customers will want to hold in such solutions and what influence\nNorwegian banks will have over the payment system and the economy.\n\nIn a payment universe dominated by international platform companies, challenges\nmay thus arise with regard to competition, data protection and back-up solutions.\nWhat options are there for disciplining big-tech companies if other private (and\nNorway-based) stakeholders are squeezed out?9 Further, how can we ensure that the\nNorwegian public is always able to make payments, if payment systems are located\nand managed from abroad?\n\nA CBDC can function as a safety valve in the event of developments in the payment\nsystem which could undermine Norges Bank’s ability to perform its tasks. This\ndevelopment is not expected, but is possible. It may therefore be a sound strategy to\ngather knowledge about the potential design of a CBDC system. Moreover, it may well\nprove more difficult to introduce a CBDC if we wait until the market has made\nsignificantly steps in the direction outlined above.\n\n\n\n\n9\n Another type of challenge is that some instruments for managing the Norwegian economy may be\nweakened. This is not discussed further in the present report.\n\n                                                                                                16\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 17 ===\n3. Alternative solutions for central bank                                                                          NORGES BANK MEMO\n                                                                                                                   NO 2 | 2019\n\n   digital currencies                                                                                              CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                                                   CURRENCIES\n\n\n                                                                                                                   SECOND REPORT OF\nIn this chapter, we describe necessary and desirable characteristics of a CBDC in                                  WORKING GROUP\ngreater detail (chapter 3.1). We then consider different technical solutions (chapters\n3.2–3.4). Finally, based on the necessary and desirable characteristics, we\nrecommend one main solution and one alternative solution for further study (chapter\n3.5).\n\n\n3.1. Necessary and desirable characteristics\nNecessary characteristics\nMoney in a Norwegian CBDC system must take the form of a claim on Norges Bank.\n\nAny potential CBDC must be designed such that the CBDC has value parity with\nbank money and cash. Accordingly, supply- and/or demand-side mechanisms must\nbe incorporated which ensure this.10\n\nThe CBDC must be customer oriented. In other words, it must be accessible to a\nbroad public, and an infrastructure must be in place which renders the CBDC suitable\nfor customer payments, including in terms of user-friendliness and security.\n\nA customer focus contrasts with a so-called “wholesale” CBDC, where money is only\naccessible to banks and other financial institutions, much like central bank reserves at\npresent. Internationally, there is discussion of whether a wholesale CBDC which is not\naccount-based could offer advantages within the financial system, such as the\npossibility to effect quicker and more secure settlement between financial institutions.\nSuch benefits are not discussed separately in the present report, although account is\ntaken of the fact that some customer-oriented CBDCs could also play a role in\nsettlement between financial institutions.\n\nSteps should be taken to prevent a CBDC from contributing to financial instability, for\nexample by intensifying a bank run. It will be necessary to integrate frictions into\ntransfers between the CBDC and bank money, for example through interest rates,\nfees or quantitative limits. When necessary, formal and informal barriers can be used\nto limit the risk of rapid, large-scale substitution of bank money with a CBDC.\n\nThe CBDC must be controlled by Norges Bank. Irrespective of the operational\nmodel chosen, Norges Bank must be able to issue instructions on all aspects of the\nsystem.\n\nThe CBDC must be capable of functioning as legal tender. This requirement does\nnot mean that the CBDC must necessarily be legal tender, but rather that its design\nmust not include any inherent barriers to its potential functioning as legal tender.\n\nThe CBDC must be compliant with obligations under EEA law.\n\nNational legislation may have to be amended to permit the introduction of the CBDC.\nHowever, the system’s design should not be such that unrealistic legislative changes\n\n\n10\n   Although there is currently value parity between bank money and cash, the possibility cannot be excluded\nthat value parity may be broken in extreme situations, for example in the event of a bank run. In such\ncircumstances, a CBDC could appear more attractive than bank money, and thus have a higher market\nvalue. However, we do not consider it necessary to give the consideration of value parity greater protection\nthan is currently granted in the case of cash. Otherwise, the solution would eliminate all credit risk linked to\nbank money.\n\n                                                                                                                   17\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 18 ===\nare required, for example heavy restrictions on data protection and consumer                NORGES BANK MEMO\nprotection. A further necessary characteristics is that payments in the CBDC must           NO 2 | 2019\nbe settled with immediate and final effect (finality).\n                                                                                            CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                            CURRENCIES\nThe CBDC’s design must be consistent with sound IT architecture principles. At\nthe general level, this means requirements relating to scalability, accessibility,          SECOND REPORT OF\nsecurity, technological neutrality and adaptability. Guidance can be taken from the         WORKING GROUP\noverarching IT architecture principles published by the Agency for Public Management\nand eGovernment (Difi).\n\nA further requirement is that the CBDC must be designed to satisfy requirements\nrelating to technical autonomy. The level of technical autonomy will depend on the\nneed for the CBDC to function as an independent back-up solution. We have\nassumed that the CBDC must be independent of banks’ electronic payment solutions.\n\nIn Norges Bank (2018), it was deemed sensible for customer communications and\ndue diligence to be undertaken by third parties. Depending on the chosen design,\nthese parties may be ones with which Norges Bank has concluded agreements or\nwhich have some other regulatory basis for performing the tasks. It is uncertain\nwhether Norges Bank can delegate its responsibilities under other regulatory\nprovisions, including financial regulations and data protection rules. Accordingly, it\nappears unrealistic to require third parties to assume full responsibility for customers.\nNevertheless, it is desirable for such responsibility to be assigned to the third parties\nmandated to perform the tasks, wherever possible.\n\nThe necessary characteristics are therefore:\n\n           claim on Norges Bank\n           value parity with bank money and cash\n           customer orientation\n           adequate frictions in transfers between the CBDC and bank money\n           controlled by Norges Bank\n           capable of functioning as legal tender\n           compliant with obligations under EEA law\n           CBDC payments are immediate and final\n           compliant with sound IT architecture principles\n           satisfy requirements relating to technical autonomy\n           customer communications and due diligence undertaken by third parties\n\nDesirable characteristics\nIn addition to the necessary characteristics a CBDC must have, there may be various\nother characteristics a CBDC should have or which are desirable for a CBDC to have.\nA non-exhaustive list review of desirable characteristics follows below.\n\nA CBDC should offer sufficient flexibility to provide the desired degree of data\nprotection. Such data protection must, as a minimum, satisfy the requirements\nimposed by EEA law – a necessary characteristic. We do not consider it necessary for\na CBDC to offer the same degree of anonymity as cash.\n\nOffline payment functionality is desirable. Depending on the technical autonomy\nrequired, this may be a necessary characteristic, although the option to make offline\npayments is considered desirable in any event. Offline payment functionality can be\nimplemented in various ways. It may be integrated into the solution, or the system can\nbe designed to allow third-party stakeholders to deliver offline payment solutions on\ntop of the CBDC.\n\nIt is desirable that a CBDC is capable of providing a platform for third-party\nproviders. The objective is that third-party stakeholders should be able to innovate\n\n\n\n                                                                                            18\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 19 ===\nand build services on top of the CBDC. These could include, for example, payment             NORGES BANK MEMO\napps, solutions based on so-called smart contracts and offline solutions.                    NO 2 | 2019\n\n                                                                                             CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\nIf it is possible to charge interest on a CBDC, the CBDC could serve as a monetary           CURRENCIES\npolicy instrument.\n                                                                                             SECOND REPORT OF\nFurther, CBDC transactions can provide information relevant to Norges Bank’s                 WORKING GROUP\n\nmacroeconomic monitoring.\n\nIt is the characteristics of a CBDC which will determine the choice of technology, and\nit is not given that distributed ledger technology (DLT) will be the obvious choice.\nHowever, DLT may develop, and it may be sensible for the CBDC design to be\nsufficiently robust to allow this technology to be incorporated, i.e. for the CBDC to be\nDLT compatible. This could be ensured in several ways. One is for the CBDC system\nto be designed so that several “nodes” can be assigned functions, for example\ntransaction validation, whilst keeping Norges Bank as the only current node. This\ncould be termed “latent” DLT. Under this approach, decentralisation would have to be\neffected at the network level. Norges Bank would control the system rules (protocol\nlevel). A different approach is for the CBDC to be designed to permit third parties to\nadd their own DLTs on top of the CBDC, or to permit integration with the DLTs of\nother stakeholders.\n\nIt is desirable for a CBDC to be in continuous use, at least to some degree. One way\nof achieving this is to make the CBDC an attractive niche solution for certain types\nof payment. Offering innovative payment solutions is not Norges Bank’s primary task.\nA niche solution could be linked to Norges Bank’s core operations, for example the\nprovision of legal tender as a fall-back rule. Certain public payments could also be\nmade using a CBDC. If the CBDC becomes an attractive platform for third-party\nproviders, the market could foster the development of such niche solutions.\n\nSome desirable characteristics are therefore:\n\n           provision of the desired degree of data protection (beyond the\n            requirements imposed by EEA law)\n           offline payment functionality\n           platform for third-party providers\n           monetary policy instrument\n           provision of information relevant to Norges Bank’s macroeconomic\n            monitoring\n           DLT compatibility\n           attractive niche solution\n\n\n3.2. Token-based and account-based solutions\nIn Norges Bank (2018), a distinction was made between account-based solutions,\nvalue-based solutions and hybrid solutions. Value-based solutions entail local storage\nof money, for example on payment cards or in mobile apps. A hybrid solution allows\nvalue to be linked to a register without the registered information necessarily being\nassociated with a name.\n\nIn CPMI (2018), a distinction was made between token-based money and account-\nbased money. Token-based money includes value-based solutions and hybrid\nsolutions. CPMI (2018) states:\n\n“A key distinction between token- and account-based money is the form of verification\nneeded when it is exchanged (Kahn and Roberds (2009)). Token-based money (or\npayment systems) rely critically on the ability of the payee to verify the validity of the\npayment object. With cash the worry is counterfeiting, while in the digital world the\n\n\n                                                                                             19\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 20 ===\nworry is whether the token or “coin” is genuine or not (electronic counterfeiting) and    NORGES BANK MEMO\nwhether it has already been spent. By contrast, systems based on account money            NO 2 | 2019\ndepend fundamentally on the ability to verify the identity of the account holder. A key\nconcern is identity theft, which allows perpetrators to transfer or withdraw money from   CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                          CURRENCIES\naccounts without permission. Identification is needed to correctly link payers and\npayees and to ascertain their respective account histories.”                              SECOND REPORT OF\n                                                                                          WORKING GROUP\nIn the case of token-based money, value is represented directly by the token. Cash is\na good example of token-based money, as it is the genuineness of cash, not the\nidentity of the payer, which is critical for the payee. Payment with token-based money\nrequires the payee to check that received money is genuine. Any electronic alternative\nmust include security mechanisms which block the making of multiple payments with\nthe same money at the same time (no double-spending).\n\nIn the case of account-based money, value is linked to the credit balance of an\naccount belonging to an identifiable account holder. When payments are made with\naccount-based money, the holder must be identified and it must be verified that the\npayer has the money which is to be used (coverage) and transferred to the payee; see\nFigure 1.\n\nFigure 1. Account-based and token-based CBDCs. Fundamental mechanisms\n\n\n\n\nSource: IMF (2018)\n\nIn the present report, we have applied the distinction made between token-based and\naccount-based money in CPMI (2018), and classify different solutions as token-based\nand account-based solutions.\n\nBelow, various token-based and account-based solutions are presented in general\nterms. We start with existing, tested solutions, and thereafter consider opportunities\nfor broader use of the technology beyond existing solutions.\n\n\n3.3. Token-based solutions\nWe have considered two types of token-based solution:\n\n       Storage on physical devices generally entails the storage of money locally,\n        on a physical device (card, mobile telephone or other mobile device with\n        internet access).\n       Register-based solutions safeguard the integrity of money by means of an\n        underlying register, meaning that money does not need to be linked to a\n        specific physical device.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                          20\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 21 ===\nStorage on physical devices                                                                   NORGES BANK MEMO\nIn a token-based model involving storage on a physical device, value is stored locally        NO 2 | 2019\non an electronic device, for example a pre-paid card or a mobile telephone (either on\nthe sim card or in the actual mobile telephone). Payments are effected through a              CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                              CURRENCIES\ntransfer of funds between the payer and payee’s electronic devices, without\nverification of the payment by a third party. The integrity of the value is ensured by        SECOND REPORT OF\ncryptographic technology integrated into the user equipment.                                  WORKING GROUP\n\n\nIn such a system, card and mobile payments are made by contact with or physical\nproximity to a terminal or card reader. These payments require proximity between the\npayer and payee.\n\nPayments in systems based on storage on physical devices are settled immediately,\nand are final. As payments are received, a payee will accrue receivables from the\nsystem owner until the payments are forwarded for settlement and crediting in an\naccount-based system (operated by a central bank or private bank).\n\nPayers do not have to identify themselves when making payments through such a\nsystem, but all individual payments can potentially be registered to the cards or chips\nused and be consolidated into a register in connection with topping-up and\nwithdrawals. A greater degree of anonymity can be secured by making topping-up\npayments and withdrawals in cash.\n\nIn similar systems which are currently in use, topping-up and withdrawals are made\nusing a mobile telephone or at user locations. Funds can be transferred from a bank\naccount into an e-money account administered by the system owner. E-money can\nthen be used for online shopping or to top-up the chip in the mobile telephone or a\nphysical card.\n\nIf the electronic device is lost or destroyed, the money on the device will in principle be\nlost. Accordingly, it is likely that there will be limits to how much value users wish to\nstore on such devices. Moreover, individual system owners may set quantitative limits\non value storage on individual devices. When value is only stored locally, it will not be\npossible to pay interest on it. These characteristics may limit the volume of value\ntransferred from bank deposits to the CBDC.\n\nRegister-based solutions\nA challenge presented by electronically stored token-based money is that money can\nbe copied and used multiple times (double-spending). As stated above, when value is\nstored on a device, the security of the physical equipment (hardware) hinders such\ndouble use.\n\nAn alternative is to establish a register which continually records transactions and\nprevents double use of the same money. This would entail checking each transaction\nagainst a register to ensure that the money in question has not been used previously.\nUnlike a bank account, this register would not be associated with a particular identity,\nbut rather with cryptographic codes and keys.\n\nUsers could control such a CBDC via a user interface which communicates with the\nregister, for example a mobile app. Since the money would be linked to the register\nand the user interface would only be used to access the money, users who lose their\nuser equipment would not lose their money. However, the opportunity to use the\nmoney may be lost if the information required to use it is only stored on the user\nequipment.\n\nWhile this principle is integrated into most crypto-currencies, the concept of “digital\ncash” was developed as early as the 1980s, not least in the wake of innovations in the\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                              21\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 22 ===\nfield of cryptography. 11 In the literature, many authors have argued that register-based     NORGES BANK MEMO\ntoken money is suitable for CBDC use.12 The technology used in such solutions is              NO 2 | 2019\nfurther explained in the box below.\n                                                                                              CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                              CURRENCIES\n\n\n                                                                                              SECOND REPORT OF\n                                                                                              WORKING GROUP\nBox: How do register-based solutions work?\nKey-based transactions\nControl of a token is linked to cryptographic keys, i.e. lengthy digital codes. To transfer\nthe value represented by the token, access is required to a private (secret) key. Each\nprivate key can be linked with a public key. The public key can be published because\nit is considered impossible to invert the public key to reveal the private key. The token\nis a digital code/address in a register and represents a given value. The adress is\nderived from the user’s public key.13\n\nA transfer is effected through the payer’s “signature” of the transaction using his or her\nprivate key. The transaction is only valid if the digital signature matches the token\naddress. This public key can be used to verify that the token has been transferred by\nthe person in possession of the private key associated with the token. In practice,\nsuch long code keys will be protected by a password, biometrics or other\nauthentication solutions in the user interface used to make transfers.\n\nThe register validates transactions\nThe register is designed to prevent the value represented by the token from being\nused several times, i.e. the register validates valid transactions and rejects invalid\ntransactions. A token can be represented in a register in several ways:\n\n              A token can take the form of an account, where consolidated value is\n               registered against the token.14\n              An alternative is for the register to review all transactions and check\n               whether the token has been topped up with sufficient value to cover the\n               intended payment, i.e. that enough has been paid in to cover what is to be\n               paid out.15\n              A third alternative is for a token to be deleted from the system once it has\n               been used and for a new token to be created for the payee. This solution\n               is used, for example, by the DigiCash solution. 16\n\n\nTransfer by means of cryptographic keys makes it easy to supplement transaction\nexecution with programming functions. This allows a range of conditions to be\nimposed on token transfers, for example that several persons must sign using their\nprivate keys to effect a transfer (so-called “multi-signature”).\n\nSmart contracts\nA further example in this context is that different transfers can be made inter-\ndependent. If, for example, securities are recorded in the same register or a\ncompatible register, the release of money can be made conditional on simultaneous\nrelease of securities (DvP). Payments can also be made conditional on the delivery of\ngoods, provided that a mechanism exists for recording such events in the register.\nConditional transfers can be combined with a lock-in of the sum in question (“escrow”)\nand a time limit, thus ensuring that a locked-in payment will be returned to the payer if\ncertain conditions are not met by a specified time. Such conditional transactions which\n\n11\n   See Narayanan et al. (2016) and Sojli and Furche (2018).\n12\n   See for example Wandhöfer (2017).\n13\n   Technically, this will often be a “hash code” generated by the public key.\n14\n   Used by the crypto-currency Ethereum.\n15\n   Used by Bitcoin.\n16\n   See Narayanan et al. (2016).\n\n                                                                                              22\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 23 ===\nare executed automatically by a register on the basis of pre-defined instructions are                                 NORGES BANK MEMO\noften referred to as smart contracts.                                                                                 NO 2 | 2019\n\n                                                                                                                      CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\nDLT                                                                                                                   CURRENCIES\nThe traditional solution is for a register to be managed by a central administrator in\nwhom users have confidence. An innovation facilitated by crypto-currencies is that                                    SECOND REPORT OF\nregisters can be managed using decentralised technology (DLT). 17 In such systems,                                    WORKING GROUP\nregisters are not administered by a central third party in whom users have confidence.\nInstead, validation is carried out by nodes/users of the network in whom users do not\nnecessarily have confidence. Open networks like crypto-currencies often allow any\nuser to carry out validation, whereas closed systems may employ appointed validators\n(thus potentially reducing resource use considerably). 18 Incentive mechanisms are\nintended to ensure confidence in a system featuring validators in whom users do not\nnecessarily have confidence.19 It is not given that the register for a token-based\nsolution must be DLT-based.\n\nIntegration with other solutions\nThrough the use of smart contracts, register-based token money can be combined\nwith other registers which are separate from the CBDC register. For example, it could\nbe the case that some stakeholders wish to develop a DLT solution which uses a\nCBDC for transactions. To avert credit risk, the CBDC could be locked-in to the CBDC\nregister by means of a smart contract, and be replaced by a token in this register. Net\nsettlement could then be effected at regular intervals using the CBDC. The person\noperating the DLT register would never be in a position to dispose of the locked-in\nfunds. Likewise, third parties might wish to provide offline-based payment solutions\nwithout exposing users to credit risk linked to the company that offers such solutions.\nA third area of application is payments between devices connected to the internet of\nthings (IoT).\n\n\n\n\n3.4. Account-based solutions\nTwo primary alternatives appear feasible for an account-based solution:\n\n               An open account solution corresponding to payment accounts offered by\n                private banks, with the possibility of direct payment to recipients’ private\n                bank accounts.\n               A closed account solution which requires both the payer and the payee to\n                have an account with Norges Bank. The solution will have similarities with\n                proprietary account solutions offered by e-money institutions.\n\nOpen account solution\nIn a bank account-based solution, users would have a traditional bank account with\nNorges Bank or an operator supervised by Norges Bank. Payments to payees with\naccounts in other banks would be implemented in the customary manner, through the\nordinary payment system. To use deposit money for customer-focused payments,\npayment instruments would have to be issued to users, and Norges Bank would have\nto participate in relevant payment systems, including inter-bank systems and systems\nfor payment services. Depending on the organisational structure, the solution would\nhave strong similarities with a full-reserve bank.\n\n\n\n17\n   Although the focus is often primarily on DLT technology with respect to register-based solutions for token\nmoney, the means of effecting transfers are probably an equally important element; see Halaburda (2017).\n18\n   This is frequently used in private blockchain solutions, such as R3’s Corda solution.\n19\n   Such incentives typically inflict costs on the validator in connection with validation and receipt of financial\ncompensation for validation (transaction fees and newly issued units of a crypto-currency). Provided that a\nsufficiently large proportion of other validators have incentives to validate correctly, the reward will be lost in\nthe event of an invalid validation.\n\n                                                                                                                      23\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 24 ===\nClosed account solution                                                                    NORGES BANK MEMO\nA closed account solution has similarities with account solutions offered by e-money       NO 2 | 2019\ninstitutions such as PayPal. Such e-money accounts have differing interfaces with the\nbank-based payment system. Nevertheless, the key characteristic of electronic e-           CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                           CURRENCIES\nmoney solutions is that both the payer and the payee have an account with an e-\nmoney institution which contains money issued by the e-money institution. The payer        SECOND REPORT OF\nnormally tops up this account with bank money, in return for which the e-money             WORKING GROUP\ninstitution issues a corresponding amount of e-money to the customer. When the\nsolution is used to make a payment, settlement occurs through debiting and crediting\nof the payer and payee’s respective accounts in the system operated by the e-money\ninstitution. If they wish, users can exchange their e-money for bank money.\n\nA CBDC could be organised as e-money-like system in which payments require the\npayer and payee to have accounts in the system. Payments through the system would\nthen be independent of the bank payment system and bank payment instruments.\n\nA closed account solution can also be referred to as an “account-based solution with\nrestrictions”. It can be designed to be expandable into an open account solution.\n\nAs discussed above, a closed account solution based on the principles of e-money\ncan be combined with storage on physical devices.\n\n\n3.5. Solutions recommended for further consideration\nThe working group has evaluated the different solutions by reference to necessary\nand desirable characteristics.\n\nWe have not found any solutions which have to be excluded because they clearly fail\nto fulfil necessary characteristics. However, the different solutions safeguard the\nnecessary characteristics to differing degrees. Moreover, they also vary quite\nconsiderably in terms of fulfilling the desirable characteristics.\n\nIf the only purpose of a CBDC is to introduce a basic electronic supplement to cash\nwithin a reasonable period of time, a solution based on physical-device storage only\nappears the most appropriate. Relatively well-tested technology and infrastructure can\nbe used. The disadvantage is that the solution will be “too similar” to cash, and usage\nwill probably be low for several of the same reasons as are driving low and falling\ncash usage. Accordingly, even though such a solution would have many of the same\ncharacteristics as cash, it would be unlikely to ensure retention of the characteristics\nof cash. A further disadvantage is that the system would impose high administrative\ncosts on Norges Bank or the party mandated to operate the solution.\n\nRealisation of a register-based solution would require more development, and would\ntherefore take longer and entail higher development costs. Nonetheless, this appears\nto be a solution which is more robust in the face of future developments and future\nneeds. It could offer the required characteristics, and has potential to be sufficiently\nattractive to safeguard important characteristics of cash. The solution could also\nfunction as a platform for innovation through third-party solutions, would facilitate\nsmart contracts which could be a useful addition to the payment system, and could be\nused as a monetary policy instrument. The solution could be designed to function as a\ntechnically independent back-up solution.\n\nAn open account solution would have to be integrated with bank payment solutions,\nand would be a close substitute for accounts with private banks, subject to the\nexception that no credit would be on offer. With this solution, demand would have to\nbe managed through interest rates and other conditions. However, in a crisis users\nmay wish to use the account as a secure store of value. An open account solution\nwould be an integral element of bank payment solutions, and it would be difficult to\nmaintain this inter-operability while also satisfying the requirement of technical\n\n                                                                                           24\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 25 ===\nautonomy. Nevertheless, provided that independent payment instruments are                 NORGES BANK MEMO\navailable, it would be possible to transfer money between two persons with accounts       NO 2 | 2019\nin the same solution independently of bank solutions. With an open account solution, it\nwould also be possible for payees to receive money from persons with a CBDC               CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                          CURRENCIES\naccount without having a CBDC account themselves. This could undermine incentives\nto open a CBDC account, which in turn could negatively impact the back-up solution.       SECOND REPORT OF\n                                                                                          WORKING GROUP\nA closed account solution would be a weaker substitute for bank money than an\nopen account solution. In payment situations, the account could only be used for\npayments between persons who have an account with Norges Bank. In a crisis, the\nsolution could probably be used as a store of value. In a closed solution, however, it\nwould probably be easier to use instruments other than interest rates to prevent large\ntransfers out of bank accounts, for example by imposing quantitative limits on\ntransfers into e-money accounts, or on e-money account balances. The solution could\nbe designed to be independent of bank payment infrastructure in payment situations.\nThe solution could easily be combined with a solution permitting local storage on a\nphysical device, meaning that the solution would also allow decentralised, offline use.\nThe solution would be sufficiently independent of bank account systems, and thus\nprovide a platform for third-party innovation. An open account solution, on the other\nhand, would have to take greater account of EEA rules on payment accounts,\nincluding with respect to solutions granting third parties access. Freedom of design\nwould also make it easier to develop niche solutions which could ensure a certain\ndegree of use.\n\nBased on the above considerations and the findings in Norges Bank (2018), we\nrecommend that further work be done on:\n\n           A CBDC in the form of register-based token money (main solution)\n           A CBDC in the form of a closed account solution offering the option of\n            storage on physical devices (alternative solution)\n\nAccordingly, we are currently not recommending further exploration of a system based\nsolely on the storage of money on physical devices, or of an open account solution.\nHowever, in choosing the design for the alternative solution, we have emphasised that\nthe solution can be expanded into an open solution. A summary of the different\nsolutions is provided in Table 1. We would emphasise that the conclusions are\nuncertain and may change over time as payment system structures evolve and new\nknowledge is gained.\n\n\n\n\n
