Organisation and implementation of the                                                                       CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                                                CURRENCIES\n\n\n   solutions                                                                                                    SECOND REPORT OF\n                                                                                                                WORKING GROUP\n\n\n\n4.1. Organisation20\nIn the case of the main solution, Norges Bank or an entity controlled by Norges Bank\nwill in principle have to develop a protocol/rules for the register, issue money and\noperate the register. Consideration could be given to designing the solution to\nincorporate “latent” DLT, to allow other parties to perform certain register tasks in\nfuture or when otherwise necessary. This could include, for example, validation of\ntransactions. We envisage that the tokens in the register would be distributed via\nbanks, and potentially other stakeholders who contract with Norges Bank. Banks (and\npossibly other parties) who sell tokens to customers and provide user interfaces will\nbe in contact with end users. A material pre-condition for a token-based solution is\nthat Norges Bank does not maintain a register which links token ownership with\nidentity. (Otherwise, the solution would in practice be an account-based solution\noffered by Norges Bank.)\n\nThe alternative solution must be operated by Norges Bank or an entity controlled by\nNorges Bank.21 This entity could either operate the solution itself or appoint private\nstakeholders who are granted access to accounts through API 22-solutions based on\nthe same principles as PSD2. In this solution, a large proportion of customer contact\ncould be left to private enterprises. Nevertheless, it is uncertain whether Norges Bank\nor the entity operating the register could avoid all contact with individual customers in\nits capacity as account provider. A further question is whether Norges Bank should in\nany event offer a “minimum solution” in the market to avoid dependence on private\nstakeholders.\n\n\n4.2. Conditions of use\nBoth the main solution and the alternative solution can be organised so that the\nrequirements for accessing the CBDC are largely the same as those which currently\napply for accessing an account with a Norwegian bank. In the case of the main\nsolution, this could be done directly by making procurement of the CBDC conditional\non the holding of an account with a Norwegian bank. In the alternative solution, the\nbest solution would probably be to establish an undertaking controlled by Norges\nBank which grants customers access to accounts subject to the same requirements\nas apply to open an account with a Norwegian bank (know-your-customer and anti-\nmoney laundering procedures, etc.) The solutions could be designed to ensure that\ntransactions are only possible between persons who have completed the approval\nprocess described above. This would prevent the Norwegian CBDC from playing a\nsignificant role as money for persons with no links with Norway.\n\nWith respect to both the main solution and the alternative solution, consideration could\nbe given to whether it should be possible to grant limited access to persons who do\n\n\n\n\n20\n   See Lagarde (2018) for a discussion of the scope for partnerships between private parties and central\nbanks.\n21\n   It may be possible for the register underpinning this solution to be operated on a decentralised basis. In\nview of current technology, however, DLT appears to be the most relevant option for the token-based\nsolution.\n22\n   “Application Programming Interface” – a software interface.\n\n                                                                                                                27\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 28 ===\nnot satisfy the requirements mentioned above, for example tourists and other persons                          NORGES BANK MEMO\nwho are in Norway temporarily. Both solutions could permit this. 23                                           NO 2 | 2019\n\n                                                                                                              CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n4.3. Conversion into bank money                                                                               CURRENCIES\n\n\n                                                                                                              SECOND REPORT OF\nRegarding the main solution, the most practical approach would be for customers to                            WORKING GROUP\nacquire token money pursuant to the same principles as currently apply to the\nprocurement of cash, i.e. through banks. Consideration could be given to whether\nother entities, such as e-money institutions, should be assigned such a role as well. In\npractice, this could be achieved through Norges Bank selling token money to banks,\nwho could set up register addresses for this money for their own customers. This will\nbe discussed further below, in the context of choice of technology.\n\nAs regards the alternative solution, the most practical approach would be for\ncustomers to convert money into the CBDC e-money in the same way as currently\napplies to e-money, i.e. by making a transfer directly from a bank account or using a\ndebit or credit card. Settlement between banks and the CBDC provider would occur\nthrough their respective central bank accounts.\n\n\n4.4. Restricting large drops in demand for bank\n     deposits\nThe main solution may present challenges linked to the avoidance of large drops in\ndemand for bank money. Cash features an inherent limit, in that it can be lost or\notherwise nullified. A question which arises in this context is whether corresponding\nrestrictions would apply to register-based token money.\n\nIf tokens can be stored relatively securely in storage solutions, there may be fewer\nfrictions than in the case of cash. An example is storage of access codes in a secure\ndata centre run by private stakeholders (so-called “cold storage”). This storage system\nis currently used by owners of crypto-assets.\n\nStoring cryptographic codes securely in cold storage is costly. In addition to direct\ncosts and the need for confidence in the relevant private stakeholders, administrative\ncosts will be incurred in connection with the handling of inheritance cases, etc. It may\nappear rational to accept all of these costs linked to crypto-currencies when the plan is\nto hold these for a long time in the expectation that their value will rise, but doing so\nappears less attractive in the case of a CBDC with a relatively stable value. A cost-\nbenefit analysis suggests that there are probably better alternatives that could function\nas a store of value.\n\nFurther, individuals could be threatened into surrendering codes, etc. At worst, this\ncould entail the loss of one’s entire possessions, a possibility the banking system\nprotects against, at least in part. To avoid this in the case of a register-based CBDC, it\nwould be necessary to construct similarities with the banking system.\n\nIn a register-based system, it would be possible to set interest rates (including\nnegative rates) and adjust transaction fees. These measures could constitute\ninstruments for restricting dramatic changes in demand for bank money. Quantitative\nrestrictions could threaten value parity with bank money.\n\n\n\n\n23\n  For example, use of such a limited solution could be restricted to smaller sums or a limited time period,\nand be made subject to the requirement that at least one party to a transaction must have completed full\ncustomer due diligence (so that transactions are not possible between two persons with limited access).\nOne questions which arises is how such persons would access the CBDC. The solution could be to permit\nthe use of foreign deposit money/credit cards and/or cash.\n\n                                                                                                              28\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 29 ===\nThe alternative solution would be a weaker substitute for bank money than an open,          NORGES BANK MEMO\ncomprehensive, bank account-based solution. In a payment situation, the account             NO 2 | 2019\ncould only be used for payments between persons with an account in the CBDC\nsystem. In practice, a customer would have to have both a bank account and a CBDC           CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                            CURRENCIES\naccount to be able to make a full range of payments (including, for example,\npurchases from abroad), and to be able to use bank-issued debit cards.                      SECOND REPORT OF\n                                                                                            WORKING GROUP\nIn a crisis, the account solution could become an attractive store of value. In a closed\nsolution, it would be easier to use other instruments in addition to interest rates to\nblock large transfers out of banks, for example by imposing limits on the size of\ntransfers into CBDC accounts, and on balances held in CBDC accounts. Further\ninvestigation is required of whether quantitative limits on CBDC accounts could\nthreaten value parity between the CBDC and bank money. In view of the deposit\nguarantee scheme and other measures to protect bank money, it appears likely that a\nCBDC would be unattractive as a store-of-value solution, although it could be\nattractive as a back-up solution for payments while a customer is waiting for access to\nmoney protected by the deposit guarantee scheme.\n\n\n4.5. User interface and third-party access\nAs regards the main solution, we have assumed that Norges Bank would be\nresponsible for administering the register, and that it would enter into “wholesale\ncustomer contracts” with banks (or other stakeholders) authorised to sell tokens to\ntheir customers. The system could be organised so that any person or authorised\nstakeholder can create a user interface to facilitate spending of the CBDC, including\nsolutions for stores and other businesses. Further, the system could be designed to\nallow third parties to provide different solutions based on smart contracts, DLT, IoT\npayments, offline payments, etc.\n\nIn principle, the alternative solution would be a closed solution offered by Norges\nBank or an entity controlled by Norges Bank. Stores and sales outlets could sign up to\nthe solution and, if they wish, provide terminals for the use of physically stored money.\nUsing APIs compliant with PSD2 principles, third parties could develop solutions for\nconsumers and businesses, and competition linked to the supply of terminal\ninfrastructure could be facilitated. Accordingly, businesses could offer their own\nsolutions and user interfaces for using the CBDC.\n\n\n4.6. Independent back-up solution\nThe main solution would allow register-based token money to function independently\nof bank payment systems. The CBDC would comprise an independent register which\nis validated and updated without the involvement of bank solutions. However, users\nwould be dependent on banks or other third parties to acquire money. Users who use\na bank user interface to access the CBDC could be affected if banking systems were\nto fail. Nonetheless, systems could be designed to allow users to access registered\nCBDC holdings independently of specific stakeholder interfaces.\n\nAs stated above, a system of register-based token money would in principle require\nusers to have internet access (i.e. to be online), and would require Norges Bank’s\nvalidation system to be operational. However, it would be possible to develop third-\nparty solutions on top of the system, which could meet these requirements – at least\nfor some time – for example through offline solutions like DLT systems running\nalongside the CBDC register. Such solutions would allow execution of transactions\ninvolving a CBDC or CBDC-based tokens without continuous access to the register,\nand without creating credit risk for the undertaking providing the solution. This could\nbe achieved by, for example, implementing a time-limited lock-in of a specified amount\nin the register for use in the solution, and subsequent consolidation with the register.\nOnly a person able to present cryptographically valid codes could effect a transfer of\nmoney from the amount reserved in the register. Based on this principle, hardware-\n\n                                                                                            29\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 30 ===\nbased solutions or “scratch card”-like solutions could be developed to facilitate CBDC    NORGES BANK MEMO\npayments in an offline situation.                                                         NO 2 | 2019\n\n                                                                                          CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\nIn the alternative solution, storage on physical devices would permit decentralised       CURRENCIES\nsettlement, making users independent of banks in payment situations. Reliance on an\ninternet connection would also be eliminated. As in the main solution, users would be     SECOND REPORT OF\ndependent on banks or other stakeholders to acquire the CBDC.                             WORKING GROUP\n\n\n\n4.7. Available technology and development needs\nIn the case of the main solution, further consideration is required of how register\naddresses should represent money. As stated above, various alternatives are\navailable for representing money in registers, and there is no basis for making firm\nrecommendations at this time. The choice of technology will also depend on how\nnecessary or desirable characteristics are to be safeguarded, including data\nprotection, smart contracts, DLT compatibility, etc.\n\nConsiderable development work will have to be done before a register-based solution\ncan safeguard necessary and desirable characteristics. Achievement of the solution\ntherefore lies some way in the future. Norges Bank will either have to assume a\nsignificant development/promotional role or wait for the market to develop relevant\nsolutions. The register-based solution will largely be based on the achievement of\ncharacteristics through market-based third-party solutions (for example solutions for\noffline payments). There is no guarantee that the market will supply such solutions on\nthe desired scale. In such case Norges Bank may have to supplement the market, for\nexample by subsidising stakeholders wishing to develop such solutions.\n\nAs regards the alternative solution, solutions are already available in the market, for\nexample the Octopus system used in Hong Kong. It would therefore be possible to\nprocure a relatively complete solution. One disadvantage of purchasing a finished\nsolution is that it may create strong dependence on an individual supplier. It may\ntherefore be necessary to engage in some in-house development to avoid such\nreliance.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                          30\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 31 ===\n5. Legal matters                                                                                         NORGES BANK MEMO\n                                                                                                         NO 2 | 2019\n\n                                                                                                         CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                                         CURRENCIES\n5.1. Regulatory framework for Norges Bank in\n     connection with the provision of relevant services                                                  SECOND REPORT OF\n                                                                                                         WORKING GROUP\n\n\nNorges Bank (2018) contains an assessment of the rules applicable to Norges Bank in\nthe provision of a CBDC. The evaluation below is largely based on the assessments in\nthe previous report.\n\nNorges Bank’s operations are governed by the Norges Bank Act. The Storting (the\nNorwegian parliament) has adopted a new Norges Bank Act, which forms the basis for\nthe following discussion.24 Pursuant to section 1-3(1) of the new act, Norges Bank is\nto “be the executive and advisory body for monetary policy”. Pursuant to section 1-\n3(3) of the new act, the bank is to “issue banknotes and coins, facilitate the central\nsettlement system and monitor the payment system”. Both the main model and the\nalternative model have been selected with achievement of these purposes in mind.\nBoth provisions were also included in the old Norges Bank Act.\n\nIn accordance with the old Norges Bank Act, section 1-3(6) of the new Norges Bank\nAct provides that, “Norges Bank may additionally implement any measures\ncustomarily or ordinarily taken by a central bank”. In principle, introducing any of the\noutlined solutions would not be contrary to this provision. If Norges Bank were to\ndecide to issue a CBDC, this would be because it considers doing so an ordinary\ncentral bank task. Nevertheless, introducing a CBDC is a far-reaching and\ncomplicated step. While the new Norges Bank Act does not regulate CBDCs, the\nquestion of introducing a CBDC was discussed in Prop. 97 L (2018-2019), which\nstates, on page 104:\n\n“The ministry agrees with the Norges Bank Act committee and Norges Bank’s\nExecutive Board that any introduction of electronic central bank money for the public\nrequires further investigation, including of the consequences for banks and credit\nprovision, and that the Norges Bank Act should not facilitate this at the present time.”\n\nNorges Bank (2018) contained an assessment of the financial regulatory requirements\nwhich would apply to Norges Bank in connection with the supply of a CBDC. As\nregards compliance with know-your-customer and anti-money laundering rules,\nNorges Bank could largely rely on third parties to do this work. In the main solution,\ncompliance could generally be based on the procedures of banks and other\nstakeholders authorised to sell tokens to end users. Necessary compliance\nprocedures would be a prerequisite for performance of this task. In the alternative\nsolution, Norges Bank or the entity operating the solution would have less scope for\ntransferring all compliance to third parties. Even though the solution would be based\nexclusively on APIs and user interfaces supplied by third parties, Norges Bank would\nstill be the account provider.\n\nConsideration must also be given to which parts of the regulatory framework for\npayments Norges Bank will be subject to in connection with the supply of a CBDC.\nNorway’s implementation of the revised Payment Services Directive (PSD2) and e-\nmoney rules is of particular relevance. Both regulatory instruments contain wide-\nranging exemptions for central banks acting in their capacity as monetary authorities.\nSupply of central bank money can probably be regarded as such a function, meaning\nthat PSD2 and the e-money rules will not impose significant restrictions on a CBDC\nunder any of the proposed models. In the main solution, the token-based money will\n\n\n\n24\n  See Prop. 97 L (2018-2019) and Innst 338 L (2018-19). The act was approved by the King in Council on\n21 June 2019. The majority of the act enters into force on 1 January 2020.\n\n                                                                                                         31\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 32 ===\nnot constitute a payment account, making it unlikely that requirements to grant third-        NORGES BANK MEMO\nparty stakeholders access will have to comply with PSD2. The same applies to the              NO 2 | 2019\nalternative solution, as the proposed solution is a closed system with a limited area\nof application.                                                                               CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                              CURRENCIES\n\nBoth solutions will in principle fall within the legal definition of e-money in section 2-    SECOND REPORT OF\n4(2), first sentence, of the Financial Institutions Act:                                      WORKING GROUP\n\n\n“By electronic money is meant an electronically stored monetary value represented by\na claim on the issuer that is issued on receipt of funds for the purpose of making\npayment transactions and that is recognised as a means of payment by entities other\nthan the issuer.”\n\nAccordingly, if supplying a CBDC is not deemed to be an official function, a detailed\nassessment will be required of adjustments necessitated by the e-money rules.\n\nSupply of a CBDC must comply with data protection rules. Responsibility cannot be\noutsourced. In the main solution, Norges Bank or any entity which operates the\nCBDC will not have to process significant volumes of personal data. The CBDC will be\nissued to banks or other stakeholders authorised to circulate the CBDC, and it will be\nthese entities that process personal data in connection with this task. In the\nalternative solution, Norges Bank or an entity controlled by Norges Bank will\nnecessarily have to link identities with accounts, and will receive information about\ntransactions which have to be implemented. This may impose extensive\nresponsibilities pursuant to the data protection rules.\n\nIf a CBDC is introduced, it will play a fairly central role in the payment system, and one\nof its purposes will be to foster competition within the payment system. However, the\nEEA Agreement and competition rules may impose restrictions intended to prevent\nthe CBDC from limiting or unnecessarily distorting competition. Accordingly, Norge\nBank should not adopt conditions for third-party access to the system which result in\nunnecessary distortion of competition or are particularly advantageous to individual\nstakeholders.\n\n\n5.2. Necessary amendments to the Norges Bank Act\nIf the legislature considers that Norges Bank should have a duty to issue a CBDC, the\nNorges Bank Act will have to be amended accordingly. If the CBDC is to be legal\ntender the act will also have to be adjusted to facilitate this; see section 3-5 of the new\nact. If the CBDC is deemed to constitute deposits with Norges Bank, this will also\nrequirement amendment of the Norges Bank Act, see section 3-1 of the new act,\nwhich generally limits deposit rights to banks and undertakings in the financial sector.\nAt this juncture, we will only discuss the latter issue in more detail.\n\nIn the main solution, the token in the register will constitute a claim on Norges Bank.\nIn legal terms, such claims can be compared to cash, and will therefore not be\nregarded as deposits with Norges Bank. Thus, the solution will not necessitate\namendment of the Norges Bank Act’s provisions on who may deposit funds with\nNorges Bank.\n\nThe alternative solution rests on the same principles as current account-based e-\nmoney solutions. From a legal perspective, e-money entails issuance of money by an\ne-money institution which has the users as its customers. Customers control accounts\noperated by the institution. E-money payments are made to and from accounts in the\nsystem operated by the e-money institution. An e-money-based CBDC solution would\nrequire Norges Bank to act as an e-money institution, either directly or indirectly, and\nfor CBDC payments to take the form of account transfers in Norges Bank’s system.\nSuch payments/account transfers would occur outside of banks’ ordinary payment\nsystems.\n\n                                                                                              32\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 33 ===\nIn principle, such a CBDC solution would fall within the legal definition of e-money, as      NORGES BANK MEMO\ndiscussed above. The question is whether, in the alternative solution, the CBDC would         NO 2 | 2019\nconstitute a deposit pursuant to section 3-1 of the new Norges Bank Act. There is no\ngeneral legal definition of “deposit”, but a deposit must generally be regarded as a          CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                              CURRENCIES\nclaim by a depositor on the party that accepts the deposit. The size of the claim will\ntypically vary in line with withdrawals from and further deposits into the original deposit   SECOND REPORT OF\naccount. This description of a traditional deposit with a bank or other financial             WORKING GROUP\ninstitution can be distinguished from the closed account system operated by e-money\ninstitutions. Section 2-2 of the Financial Institutions Act states expressly that\n\n“Means of payment that are accepted in order to issue electronic money are not\ndeemed to be deposits from an unrestricted range of depositors. The same applies to\nmeans of payment accepted by payment institutions or electronic money institutions in\nconnection with payment service assignments.”\n\nIn other words, pursuant to the E-Money Directive, the legislation thus expressly\ndistinguishes between ordinary deposits and receipt of funds for the purpose of\nissuing e-money. Fundamentally, it is logical to apply this distinction when interpreting\nsection 3-1 of the new Norges Bank Act, i.e. to conclude that an e-money-based\nCBDC solution does not conflict with restriction of the deposit rights of undertakings in\nthe financial sector.\n\nPurpose considerations also suggest that this interpretation is correct. The situation\nwhich section 3-1 aims to regulate is where private individuals and legal persons other\nthan banks and undertakings in the financial sector wish to make deposits into\naccounts at Norges Bank, in competition with other banks. This is significantly\ndifferent from the introduction of an e-money-based CBDC solution by Norges Bank,\nwhich would be a distinct system independent of banks’ ordinary payment systems.\n\nThe conclusion is that neither the main solution (token with register) nor the alternative\nsolution (closed account solution) requires amendment of the Norges Bank Act’s\nprovisions on who may deposit money with Norges Bank. As stated in section 5.1,\nhowever, introducing a CBDC may necessitate legislative amendments due to its\npotential far-reaching effects.\n\n\n5.3. Need for other regulatory amendments\nThe introduction of a CBDC may necessitate various legislative amendments to\nensure that the CBDC functions as intended. This can be effected through a separate\nCBDC act and/or through adjustments to the current regulatory framework, particularly\nthe financial regulatory rules. Some of the need could also be met by applying\nlicensing requirements and similar conditions to stakeholders involved in CBDC\ndistribution.\n\nThis report does not include an assessment of the best legal approach to\nimplementing regulatory amendments. However, we would note that if the alternative\nsolution is chosen and a separate entity controlled by Norges Bank is established to\nsupply the CBDC to the public, a separate legal framework may be required in relation\nto this entity.\n\nIt may be necessary to make legislative amendments to ensure circulation of the\nCBDC. In this connection, it may be appropriate to require banks and, if relevant,\nother institutions to supply the CBDC to customers on the same terms as cash is\ncurrently supplied. It may also be necessary to impose additional obligations on banks\nin connection with sale of the CBDC to the public. There may be a need for\nsupplementation of the know-your-customer rules and anti-money laundering rules to\nensure adjustment to the existence of the CBDC. A further potential need is for special\nsecurity rules applicable to relevant institutions, to ensure that the CBDC is a secure\nalternative for users.\n\n\n                                                                                              33\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 34 ===\n                                                                                              NORGES BANK MEMO\n                                                                                              NO 2 | 2019\nIf a CBDC is to become legal tender, it may be appropriate to introduce provisions\n                                                                                              CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\nsimilar to those currently found in section 38(3) of the Financial Contracts Act on the       CURRENCIES\nright to make payment in cash, to ensure that customers can pay with the CBDC at all\nuser locations.                                                                               SECOND REPORT OF\n                                                                                              WORKING GROUP\n\nIt may also be necessary to regulate third-party suppliers of CBDC-related services\nother than the sale of CBDC to the public. This will typically include providers of digital\nwallets and other user interfaces. We assume that it will be attractive to supply\nsolutions which facilitate easy CBDC use and which allow customers to integrated\nCBDC use and storage with other services. Examples include Vipps, Apply Pay,\nGoogle Pay and various PSD2 stakeholders. If such entities do not offer secure CBDC\nsolutions, confidence in the CBDC may be undermined and reputational risk may\narise. Typical regulations in this context include requirements as to authentication\nmechanisms and back-up solutions. In the case of register-based solutions in which\nstakeholders offer access by password, biometrics, artificial intelligence, etc., it may\nparticularly important to impose requirements as to the security of these mechanisms,\nand requirements regarding back-up solutions.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                              34\n\f\n=== DOC 2 PAGE 35 ===\nReferences                                                                                     NORGES BANK MEMO\n                                                                                               NO 2 | 2019\n\n                                                                                               CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\nBarontini, C. and Holden, H. Introduction                                                                                              NO 2 | 2020\n\n                                                                                                             STATUS REPORT –\nA central bank digital currency (CBDC) is a digital form of central bank money                               NORGES BANK’S\ndenominated in the official unit of account for general purpose users. It is a                               CENTRAL BANK\n                                                                                                             DIGITAL CURRENCY\nclaim on the central bank, in the same way that banknotes and coins are                                      PROJECT\ntoday. By comparison, bank deposits are claims on private banks.\n\nOwing to declining cash usage and the possibility of major structural changes\nin the monetary and payment system, Norges Bank is assessing whether it is\nappropriate also to issue a digital form of central bank money to ensure that\nmembers of the public are able to pay efficiently and securely in Norwegian\nkroner also in the future.\n\nIn Norges Bank (2018), a Norges Bank working group presented an overview\nof factors that should be given weight in an assessment of whether Norges\nBank should issue a CBDC in addition to cash. In Norges Bank (2019), the\nworking group took a closer look at possible objectives of a CBDC and\nalternative designs for achieving these objectives.\n\nThe CBDC project is now in its third phase.1 The purpose of publishing this\nstatus report is to provide information about the work, disseminate knowledge\nand invite stakeholders to a dialogue.\n\n\n2. Purpose and deliveries in the project’s\n   third phase\nThe purpose of the project’s third phase is to provide a more solid foundation\nfor assessing whether Norges Bank should work to issue a CBDC and if so, in\nwhat form. Project deliveries are to include:\n\n    a) Examining relevant detailed CBDC solutions on the basis of the\n       proposed solutions in (2019) with assessments of, among other things,\n       necessary and desirable characteristics.\n    b) Performing further analyses of the motivation for and impact of a\n       CBDC where the working group deems it necessary in order to make a\n       recommendation.\n    c) Summarising and systematising international developments and any\n       new factors in the international debate on CBDCs and related issues.\n    d) During 2021 Q1, issuing a recommendation to central bank executive\n       management as to whether Norges Bank is to work further on a\n       specific CBDC solution and what a fourth phase, if any; should contain.\nThis status report will primarily consist of a briefing on work on point a).\n\n\n\n\n1\n The project working group comprises Knut Sandal (chair), Ragna Alstadheim, Tom Bernhardsen, Kjetil\nHeltne, Arne Kloster, Helge Syrstad, Ylva Søvik, Leif Veggum, Peder Østbye, Terje Åmås and Steinar Årdal.\nCarola Müller contributes to a sub-project. The steering group comprises Torbjørn Hægeland (chair), Ingrid\nSolberg (took over from Ida Wolden Bache on 1 April 2020), Olav Bø, Kasper Roszbach and Marius Ryel.\nThe working group can be contacted at knut.sandal@norges-bank.no and peder.ostbye@norges-bank.no.\n\n                                                                                                             3\n\n\n=== DOC 3 PAGE 4 ===\n3. International trends                                                              NORGES BANK PAPERS\n                                                                                     NO 2 | 2020\n\nCentral banks worldwide are working increasingly on CBDCs. Boar et al                STATUS REPORT –\n(2020) documents a survey on work on CBDCs in 2019 in which 66 central               NORGES BANK’S\n                                                                                     CENTRAL BANK\nbanks responded:                                                                     DIGITAL CURRENCY\n                                                                                     PROJECT\n\n    -   A good 80 percent of the central banks were working on a CBDC,\n        compared with a good 70 percent in 2018.\n    -   Most were working on a variant of a CBDC for general purpose users.\n        Many also looked at a CBDC for settlement between financial\n        institutions.\n    -   Only 10 percent of the central banks had technical solutions under\n        development or in pilot testing. Most were working on analyses and\n        proofs of concept that (for the time being) did not involve development\n        and testing.\nThe Riksbank is currently pilot testing a technical solution for an e-krona (see\nRiksbank (2020)). Other countries that have tested or are about to test\ntechnical solutions for a CBDC for general purpose users include China,\nUruguay, Ecuador, the Bahamas and Ukraine.\n\n\n4. Validation of solutions\nThe characteristics of a CBDC were outlined in Norges Bank (2019):\n\nNecessary characteristics                   Desirable characteristics\nClaim on Norges Bank                        Can provide the desired degree of data\n                                            protection (beyond requirements under\n                                            EEA law)\nValue parity with bank money/cash           Offline payment functionality\nCustomer orientation                        Platform for third-party providers\nAdequate frictions vis-à-vis bank money     Monetary policy instrument\nControlled by Norges Bank                   Provide information relevant to Norges\n                                            Bank’s macroeconomic monitoring\nCapable of functioning as legal tender      DLT compatibility\nCompliant with obligations under EEA law    Attractive niche solution\nSettlement of payments immediately and\nwith finality\nCompliant with sound IT architecture\nprinciples\nSatisfy requirements related to technical\nautonomy\nCustomer communication and due diligence\nundertaken by third parties\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                     4\n\n\n=== DOC 3 PAGE 5 ===\nTechnical solutions recommended in Norges Bank (2019):                                            NORGES BANK PAPERS\n                                                                                                  NO 2 | 2020\n\nSolution                             Explanation                                                  STATUS REPORT –\n                                                                                                  NORGES BANK’S\nA CBDC in the form of                The CBDC is linked to a register and is accessed via         CENTRAL BANK\nregister-based token                 cryptographic codes not associated with an identity. This work has iterated between necessary and\ndesirable characteristics listed in Norges Bank (2019) and technical solutions\nrecommended in Norges Bank (2019). This means going back and forth\nbetween characteristics and technical solutions as knowledge is gained.3 The\npurpose is to assess whether technical solutions have the necessary and\ndesired characteristics and, if applicable, whether the characteristics need to\nbe specified further.\n\n\n\n\n2\n    See Turner (2018).\n3\n    In line with the “agile development” principle.\n\n                                                                                                  5\n\n\n=== DOC 3 PAGE 6 ===\nValidation work is performed in three partly overlapping work streams:             NORGES BANK PAPERS\n                                                                                   NO 2 | 2020\n\n      Specification of characteristics                                            STATUS REPORT –\n      Meetings with outside parties                                               NORGES BANK’S\n                                                                                   CENTRAL BANK\n      Analysis of relevant technological solutions                                DIGITAL CURRENCY\n                                                                                   PROJECT\n\n\n\n\n                       Characteristics\n\n\n\n\n                                          Meetings\n          Technology                     with outside\n                                            parties\n\n\n\n\n4.1. Specification of characteristics\nExperience shows that there is a need to specify characteristics in more detail\nthan in Norges Bank (2019) in order to assess whether different technical\nsolutions can fulfil the characteristics. For example, an assessment of whether\nNorges Bank has control over a system containing elements of distributed\nledger technology (DLT) requires a specification of what is adequate control.\nFurther specification is also necessary for weighing characteristics against one\nanother, where fulfilment of one characteristic can conflict with fulfilment of\nanother.\n\n\n4.2. Meetings with outside parties\nThe working group has organised or planned information meetings with\n\n      technology providers,\n      user representatives and\n      government authorities.\nSome meetings came about after outside parties contacted Norges Bank,\nwhile others were on the initiative of Norges Bank. We are planning several\nsuch information meetings this autumn and are open to queries from parties\nwho wish to speak with us.\n\nTechnology providers we have met with include providers of CBDC-relevant\ntechnology and consultancies. They have provided information and shared\ntheir views on how necessary and desirable characteristics can best be\nrealised. Several of the technology providers offer solutions based on DLT,\nincluding blockchain technology. We would like to make it clear that Norges\n\n\n                                                                                   6\n\n\n=== DOC 3 PAGE 7 ===\nBank has not taken a position on a preferred technology for a CBDC system if                         NORGES BANK PAPERS\nimplemented.                                                                                         NO 2 | 2020\n\n                                                                                                     STATUS REPORT –\nUser representatives are typically organisations representing users and                              NORGES BANK’S\n                                                                                                     CENTRAL BANK\nrepresentatives of parties that would be affected by a CBDC. Government                              DIGITAL CURRENCY\nauthorities can be relevant both as users and as regulators.                                         PROJECT\n\n\n\nThe working group has been in contact with the Riksbank and the Central\nBank of Iceland to discuss technical solutions, among other topics.\n\nThe working group has also reported on – and received comments on and\nsuggestions for - its work on a CBDC at a number of conferences and\nseminars.\n\nThe working group is planning an external seminar in autumn 2020 about user\nneeds and technical solutions.\n\n\n4.3. Technology\nThe purpose has been to gain more insight into relevant technical solutions for\nCBDC systems and how they can be tested. The working group has reviewed\ndifferent elements of the relevant technologies and solutions chosen for pilot\ntesting in other countries.\n\n\n4.4. Some preliminary assessments\nThe work on validation has taken place in parallel with pilot testing carried out\nby a number of central banks,4 and several publications by central banks and\nother parties have come out that go into more detail on design and technical\nsolutions.5\n\nA common feature of the international work seems to be that CBDCs in the\nform of register-based token money (including the use of variants of DLT\ntechnology) appear to be a more viable alternative than previously. For\nexample, the Riksbank has chosen a technical design based on DLT to pilot\ntest (see Riksbank (2020)). Our assessment in phases 1 and 2 was that DLT\nwas not mature enough and that considerable development was necessary for\nit to be relevant for a CBDC. We assume that a number of central banks would\nhave focused more on account solutions if time had been a constraint.\n\nEven though most of the focus in validation work has so far been on DLT, the\ndesign and characteristics of some existing account systems have been\nassessed, such as the Octopus system in Hong Kong.\n\nThe impression is that DLT requires more detailed specification of\ncharacteristics than account-based solutions, in part because there may be\ncases of doubt as to whether the characteristics have been fulfilled.\nFurthermore, there are not as many market-tested DLT solutions to choose\nfrom as there are account-based solutions.\n\n\n\n\n4\n See, eg, Riksbank (2020), National Bank of Ukraine (2019) and Central Bank of the Bahamas (2019).\n5\n See, eg, Bank of England (2020), Auer and Boehme (2020), WEF (2020), Consensys (2020) and Allen\net.al (2020).\n\n                                                                                                     7\n\n\n=== DOC 3 PAGE 8 ===\nValidation work has focused on how different variants and elements of DLT –                                      NORGES BANK PAPERS\nbut also where the actual CBDC register is not necessarily based on DLT –                                        NO 2 | 2020\ncan contribute to the adequate fulfilment of necessary and desirable\n                                                                                                                 STATUS REPORT –\ncharacteristics.6                                                                                                NORGES BANK’S\n                                                                                                                 CENTRAL BANK\n                                                                                                                 DIGITAL CURRENCY\nThe final report from phase 3 will contain a recommendation for a framework                                      PROJECT\n\nfor pilot testing if a decision is made to move forward. This has so far not been\nconsidered in detail. However, a preliminary assessment is that it would make\nlittle sense to duplicate pilot tests conducted in other countries. Knowledge\nfrom other pilot projects can be acquired through the exchange of experience.\nIt must also be considered whether physical testing can be conducted as a\nform of “sandbox” or a test network where several operators can test\nsolutions, instead of a traditional pilot test where a single operator is chosen in\na tender competition.\n\n\n\n\n6\n  An important question is whether a CBDC register can benefit from external DLT overlay solutions. This\nentails designing the CBDC to be cryptographically compatible with DLT solutions offered by other\noperators, so that the CBDC can be locked into such a system for using services there, without a credit risk\nfor users. In such cases, fulfilment of the potential characteristics will be left to the market (eg offline\nsolutions and increased anonymity for payment transactions) and that the CBDC can be a platform for\ninnovation. This also means that Norges Bank can provide a relatively simple core solution, while more\nadvanced functionalities (such as smart contracts) are left to the market. This is a type of organisation that\nthe Bank of England seems to be leaning towards (see Bank of England (2020)). However, such overlay\nsolutions may entail an operational risk for users, which also may give rise to a reputational risk for Norges\nBank. This is therefore something we will study further.\n\n\n                                                                                                                 8\n\n\n=== DOC 3 PAGE 9 ===\nReferences                                                                        NORGES BANK PAPERS\n                                                                                  NO 2 | 2020\n\nAllen, S., Capkun, S., Eyal, I., Fanti, G., Ford, B., Grimmelmann, J., ... &      STATUS REPORT –\nPrasad, E. Such money is a claim on the central bank denominated in         WORKING GROUP\nthe official unit of account in the same way as cash.\n\nNorges Bank and a number of other central banks are researching the introduction of\na CBDC as a supplement to cash. For Norges Bank, the paramount question is\nwhether introducing a CBDC is an appropriate measure for promoting an efficient and\nsecure payment system and confidence in the monetary system.\n\nNorges Bank’s research into CBDCs is motivated by low and falling cash usage and\napplication of the precautionary principle. Cash provides the payment system with a\nnumber of attributes that may be relevant to retain and develop further by issuing a\nCBDC: An independent backup for payment solutions based on bank deposits, a\ncredit risk-free alternative to bank deposits that can foster competition in the payments\nmarket and legal tender that can be used by anyone.\n\nAt the same time, Norges Bank wishes to be prepared to introduce a CBDC if the\nmonetary and payment system evolves in a different direction from the one currently\nforeseen. We must take into account changes in the payment solutions on offer, with\ndifferent forms of money, structural changes in banks’ payment infrastructure and the\npossible consequences of these changes for competition, contingency preparedness\nand national governance and control of the monetary and payment system.\n\nThis report by a Norges Bank working group summarises the third phase of Norges\nBank’s research into CBDCs and is based on the working group’s previous reports,\npublished as Norges Bank Papers 1/2018 and 2/2019. The working group discusses\nthe characteristics any CBDC must have, relevant technical solutions and the impact\nof introducing a CBDC. The working group also discusses international developments\nin this area and how Norges Bank’s research into CBDCs can continue.\n\nNorges Bank has recently decided to continue this research for a fourth phase of up to\ntwo years, in line with the working group’s recommendation in this report. This phase\nis to comprise experimental testing of technical solutions and further analysis of\npurposes and consequences of introducing a CBDC.\n\nThis work is intended to provide a basis for deciding whether Norges Bank will test a\npreferred technical solution. Any decision to introduce a CBDC will require a political\ndecision. The question may also arise as to whether the introduction of a CBDC would\nrequire an amendment to the Central Bank Act.\n\nNorges Bank’s research into CBDCs has run for a good four years. Any introduction of\na CBDC will still lie some time in the future. The time spent reflects Norges Bank’s\nview that there is no immediate need to introduce a CBDC. This is a new and complex\nissue, and there is little international experience to draw on. There is therefore a need\nfor more information to be able to conclude whether introducing a CBDC is an\nappropriate measure.\n\nThe purpose of publishing the working group’s report is to provide information about\nits work, disseminate knowledge and foster dialogue among stakeholders. Norges\nBank would be grateful for views on the analysis and input for the work ahead.\n\nØystein Olsen\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                            4\n\f\n=== DOC 4 PAGE 5 ===\nIntroduction and summary                                                                                       NORGES BANK PAPERS\n                                                                                                               NO 1 | 2021\n\n                                                                                                               CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                                               CURRENCIES\nA central bank digital currency (CBDC) is a digital form of central bank money                                 THIRD REPORT FROM THE\ndenominated in the official unit of account for general purpose users. Like                                    WORKING GROUP\ncash, a CBDC is a claim on the issuing central bank. In contrast, bank\ndeposits are claims on private banks. A CBDC can take several forms with\ndifferent characteristics, depending on its purpose.\n\nNorges Bank and many other central banks are evaluating the introduction of\na CBDC. A survey by the central-bank representative organisation BIS1 has\nfound that 86% of central banks in a broad-based sample are currently\nstudying CBDCs, and that 60% are taking a closer look at technical solutions.\nSeveral central banks, including Sveriges Riksbank (the Swedish central\nbank) and the People’s Bank of China, are running pilot projects. The\nEuropean Central Bank is examining a digital euro. To date, however, only a\nfew central banks in emerging economies have actually introduced a CBDC.\n\nA Norges Bank working group2 has now completed the third phase of a study\non CBDCs, building on the reports on the first and second phases; see Norges\nBank (2018) and Norges Bank (2019).\n\nThe purpose of the study is to establish a basis for assessing whether Norges\nBank should plan to introduce a CBDC and, if so, what form the CBDC should\ntake. Important questions include: “What problems could a CBDC help to\nsolve?”; “What characteristics must a CBDC have?” and “Which solutions are\nmost suitable?”\n\nThe working group has emphasised the functions of a CBDC as a means of\npayment and payment system, rather than its function as a store of value. In\nNorges Bank (2019), the working group concluded that a CBDC could be\ndesirable to:\n\n     -    function as an independent back-up solution if ordinary electronic\n          payment solutions fail or confidence in the banking system weakens\n          substantially. The contingency perspective may become more\n          important if payment infrastructure becomes more international\n     -    maintain or strengthen competition in the payment market\n     -    deliver all the key characteristics of legal tender and, if desirable,\n          expand the area of application.\n\nIn Norges Bank (2019), the working group also pointed out that the payment\nsystem may change considerably in the years ahead, in terms of new\nstakeholders, new monetary and payment systems, and the geographical\nlocation of systems. The working group therefore concluded that the\nprecautionary principle also indicated that Norges Bank should continue its\nexamination of CBDCs.\n\n\n\n\n1\n See Boar and Wehrli (2021).\n2\n The working group comprised Knut Sandal (chair), Ragna Alstadheim, Tom Bernhardsen, Kjetil Heltne,\nArne Kloster, Helge Syrstad, Ylva Søvik, Leif Veggum, Peder Østbye, Terje Åmås and Steinar Årdal. Carola\nMüller and Magdalena D. Riiser assisted the working group with parts of the analysis. The project’s steering\ngroup comprised Torbjørn Hægeland (chair), Ole Christian Bech-Moen, Olav Bø, Kasper Roszbach, Marius\nRyel and Ingrid Solberg. Ida Wolden Bache participated in the steering group until 1 April 2020.\n\n                                                                                                               5\n\f\n=== DOC 4 PAGE 6 ===\nIn Norges Bank (2019), the working group set out a provisional assessment of       NORGES BANK PAPERS\nthe characteristics which a CBDC must or should have in order to fulfil the        NO 1 | 2021\nobjectives. In Phase 3, these characteristics were specified and assessed in\n                                                                                   CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\ndetail. While the list of characteristics remains largely unchanged, this report   CURRENCIES\n                                                                                   THIRD REPORT FROM THE\ndiscusses aspects of the characteristics which require further investigation and   WORKING GROUP\nspecification through technical testing.\n\nIn some instances, characteristics may conflict with one another. Some\nconflicts are inherent, in the sense that they are technology-independent. For\nexample, opening up the CBDC register/account system to innovative\nsolutions developed by other stakeholders would inherently conflict with the\nprinciple that Norges Bank should have ultimate control. Conflicts may also be\ntechnology-dependent, i.e. the relevant characteristics are linked with the\ntechnical solution to some degree. In other cases, the technology will be\nflexible enough to allow characteristics to be balanced in connection with\nsystem implementation or during system operation. The working group\nconsiders it expedient to select solutions which are sufficiently flexible to\npermit such trade-offs, and which are robust enough to withstand future\nrevision of an established trade-off. The working group has proposed an\napproach for balancing conflicting characteristics.\n\nThe working group has also considered how technical solutions can deliver\nthe characteristics and how they can be tested. In this context, the working\ngroup has examined solutions used in CBDC testing in other countries, in\ncryptocurrency systems and in different account-based solutions. Feedback\non solutions, testing and user needs has been gathered through meetings with\nvarious external stakeholders.\n\nPotential CBDC demand is highly uncertain. Among other things, demand will\ndepend on as-yet unknown specifics of the CBDC solution design, the CBDC’s\nareas of application and how CBDC users choose to manage their personal\nliquidity. In its analysis, Sveriges Riksbank (the Swedish central bank), has\nassumed CBDC demand of around 3% of GDP. In Norway, 3% of mainland\nGDP totalled some NOK 90 billion in 2020.\n\nA guiding principle adopted in this report is that the existence and size of any\nCBDC should not materially undermine private sector provision of credit to\nfirms and households. The working group has analysed potential effects on\nNorwegian banks of introducing a CBDC. The effect on bank financing\nstructures will depend on how demand for bank deposits changes, and is\nuncertain. There is reason to believe that some deposits will be replaced and\nthat demand for deposits will become more interest-sensitive. However, it is\npossible to limit the effect of a CBDC on bank funding and lending rates,\nprovided that the central bank makes reserves available to banks when\ndeposits are withdrawn from banks and replaced by a CBDC, and that there\nare no significant changes in banks’ funding structures otherwise or in interest\nrate spreads in banks’ wholesale funding markets. The above conclusion also\nreflects the assumption that the CBDC will not be particularly competitive as a\nstore of value, but rather will meet a transactional need, for example because\nthe interest rate on the CBDC is set at a low level.\n\nThere have been rapid developments in the digital money field over the course\nof Phase 3. One of these is the emergence of so-called cryptocurrencies\nincorporating stabilisation mechanisms intended to make the currencies more\nstable relative to national currencies or other benchmarks. One example is\n\n\n                                                                                   6\n\f\n=== DOC 4 PAGE 7 ===\nDiem, whose backers include Facebook. Although such initiatives are unlikely         NORGES BANK PAPERS\nto have material consequences for Norges Bank’s tasks and goal achievement           NO 1 | 2021\nin the short term, the introduction of new digital money illustrates the potential\n                                                                                     CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\nimpact of new technologies and new user needs on the function of money in            CURRENCIES\n                                                                                     THIRD REPORT FROM THE\nfuture, and raises questions about who should be meeting such needs and              WORKING GROUP\nfulfilling such functions. The working group has considered this question not\nleast when assessing how a CBDC could constitute a platform for service\ninnovation by third-party providers. In dialogue meetings, stakeholders\nhighlighted the need for so-called programmable money, and the working\ngroup has assessed both risks and benefits associated with programmable\nmoney as part of the validation process. In addition, various central banks\nhave intensified their CBDC-related work by launching tests and pilot projects,\noften incorporating technology which facilitates programmability and other\nfunctions. This development underlines the need to follow the precautionary\nprinciple identified in Norges Bank (2019) when studying CBDCs. The fact that\na CBDC could provide future functionality may be important to counter the risk\nthat money and payment functions could shift to new arenas and\ninfrastructures which may undermine the efficiency and security of the\npayment system. Further consideration should be given to how a CBDC could\nhelp counter this risk.\n\nThe working group has recommended extending the CBDC study for a fourth\nphase. The group has not observed any circumstances indicating that the\nstudy should be concluded. On the contrary, the findings thus far and the\ndevelopments occurring in monetary and payment systems indicate stronger\ngrounds for continuing the work. The proportion of cash payments in Norway\nhas fallen further, and is now thought to be the lowest in the world. If anything,\nthe importance of applying the precautionary principle with respect to\nunanticipated structural changes in monetary and payment systems has\nincreased. Plans for new monetary and payment systems have been\nlaunched, and the role of CBDCs in the response to this development has\nbecome clearer. Central banks and international organisations are paying\ncloser attention to CBDCs. This offers opportunities for knowledge-sharing. In\naddition, any decision by other central banks to introduce a CBDC, and the\nchosen design, may affect how Norges Bank and other central banks fulfil\ntheir mandates, and may in isolation accelerate a decision on introducing a\nCBDC.\n\nThe working group has recommended that the fourth phase of the study\nshould last up to two years and should comprise experimental technical\ntesting and further analysis of the purpose and consequences of introducing a\nCBDC.\n\nThe purpose of technical testing is to generate new knowledge on how\nsolutions can deliver the necessary and desirable characteristics of a CBDC,\nand to uncover potential unintended consequences of relevant solutions. Such\ntesting should utilise tests performed by other central banks, and cooperation\nshould be pursued with other central banks wherever expedient. Norges\nBank’s own tests will be an important factor in facilitating such cooperation.\nThe working group has concluded that experimental technical testing will both\nprovide information on technological solutions and help reveal and concretise\nrelevant analytical issues in the economic and legal/regulatory fields which\ncannot be discovered through purely analytical work.\n\nThe fourth phase should produce a basis for deciding whether Norges Bank\nshould test a preferred solution with the aim of being able to introduce a\n\n                                                                                     7\n\f\n=== DOC 4 PAGE 8 ===\nCBDC. However, continuation of the CBDC study does not constitute a              NORGES BANK PAPERS\ndecision to introduce a CBDC. Further, any decision to introduce a CBDC will     NO 1 | 2021\nrequire political support, and the requisite statutory changes will have to be\n                                                                                 CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\nconsidered and implemented.                                                      CURRENCIES\n                                                                                 THIRD REPORT FROM THE\n                                                                                 WORKING GROUP\nThe working group has discussed the organisation of technical testing, and\nhas recommended using external testing suppliers. Norges Bank should\nmaintain close ongoing contact with selected suppliers.\n\nThe purpose and consequences of introducing a CBDC should be studied\nfurther. Relevant topics include supplementation of reserves and liquidity\nmanagement using the CBDC, and consequences for Norges Bank’s balance\nsheet and monetary policy in general. Consideration should also be given to\nwhat statutory changes will be needed before a CBDC can be introduced.\nFurther, the working group has recommended further assessment of how the\nintroduction of a CBDC could counteract the risk that money and payment\nfunctions may shift to new arenas and infrastructures. More generally, the\nintroduction of a CBDC should be evaluated by reference to what can be\nachieved through regulation of privately-owned payment systems for privately\nissued money.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                 8\n\f\n=== DOC 4 PAGE 9 ===\nPART I: Characteristics and technical                                                NORGES BANK PAPERS\n                                                                                     NO 1 | 2021\n\nsolutions                                                                            CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\n                                                                                     CURRENCIES\n                                                                                     THIRD REPORT FROM THE\n                                                                                     WORKING GROUP\n\n1. Validation of technical solutions by\n   reference to characteristics\nNorges Bank (2019) identified a number of characteristics a Norwegian CBDC\nmust and should have. The working group has not come across\ncircumstances indicating that this list of characteristics needs to be materially\namended. The characteristics appear adequately flexible and robust.\nHowever, the framing of the characteristics has been expanded and adjusted\nto reflect the content of the characteristics and simplify validation of technical\nsolutions. The substance of the characteristics has not changed significantly.\nIn Norges Bank (2019), a distinction was made between necessary and\ndesirable characteristics. The working group considers that this distinction is\nunnecessary going forward, since most of the characteristics appear to be\nnecessary. In addition, some characteristics are so closely interlinked that they\ncan be dealt with jointly. For example, the requirements of technical autonomy\nand offline payment functionality both relate to contingency preparedness. In the context of         NORGES BANK PAPERS\nthe different workflows, the working group has assessed how technical testing        NO 1 | 2021\nshould be performed to ensure optimal investigation of how technical solutions\n                                                                                     CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\ndeliver the characteristics. This is summarised in Part III, which contains          CURRENCIES\n                                                                                     THIRD REPORT FROM THE\nrecommendations on further work to be done.                                          WORKING GROUP\n\n\n\n\n2. Review of characteristics\nTo validate whether different technological solutions are in line with the\nrequired characteristics of a potential CBDC, the working group has reviewed\neach individual characteristic in detail.\n\nConducting this assessment necessitated detailed specification of the\ncharacteristics, including their core features and the purpose they are intended\nto serve.\n\nThe objective was to identify\n\n   -   the number of compatible/consistent technical solutions which may\n       fulfil all the characteristics,\n   -   whether there are solutions which cannot fulfil all the characteristics\n       simultaneously.\n\nWhile many technical solutions/architecture solutions fulfil the characteristics\nto a greater or lesser degree, it may still be necessary to balance the\ncharacteristics when implementing the solution. The working group has\nidentified potential necessary trade-offs, and has given primary emphasis to\nensuring that selected solutions are flexible so that trade-offs can be made on\nan ongoing basis in accordance with applicable priorities at any given time.\nThis can be supplemented with a concrete cost-benefit analysis.\n\nThe working group has not conducted a new systematic assessment of the list\nof characteristics in Norges Bank (2019). Nevertheless, the group has\nconcluded that new CBDC priorities or specifications resulting from the\nvalidation process can be attributed to the previously identified characteristics,\neven though these factors were not necessarily assessed closely or in full at\nthe time the characteristics were formulated. For example, interoperability\nissues have been evaluated in detail. Interoperability with the CBDC solutions\nof other countries in particular has increased in relevance as other countries\nhave intensified their CBDC-related efforts. Interoperability may relate to\nseveral characteristics, including compliance with sound IT architecture\nprinciples, functioning as a platform for third party providers and DLT\ncompatibility.\n\nA summary of the working group’s review of the different characteristics listed\nin Table 1 above follows.\n\n2.1. Claim on Norges Bank\nThe characteristic of cash as a claim on Norge Bank is best expressed as an\naccounting principle. Issued cash is shown on the liability side of a central\nbank balance sheet. The liability lapses when cash is withdrawn from\ncirculation. In principle, the situation would be the same for a CBDC. The\n\n                                                                                     12\n\f\n=== DOC 4 PAGE 13 ===\nworking group has assumed that a CBDC would be issued to banks for                                             NORGES BANK PAPERS\ndistribution to customers through conversion of bank deposits. This mirrors the                                NO 1 | 2021\nfunctioning of cash. Accordingly, a CBDC would be shown – like cash – as a\n                                                                                                               CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\nseparate item on the liability side of Norges Bank’s balance sheet.4                                           CURRENCIES\n                                                                                                               THIRD REPORT FROM THE\n                                                                                                               WORKING GROUP\nNorges Bank’s balance sheet should clearly show the number of issued CBDC\nunits and, correspondingly, how many units have been withdrawn from\ncirculation – again like cash. Clear procedures should therefore be established\nfor the issue and withdrawal of CBDC units, including for the technical\nhandling of withdrawn units. For example, it will be undesirable for withdrawn\nCBDC units to be shown as an asset item in the central bank balance sheet.\n\nAny CBDC should be structured in such a manner that Norges Bank is the\nformal issuer, and issued CBDC units should be expressly specified as a\nliability item in the bank’s balance sheet. Banks and other parties should not\nbe given the right to issue CBDC units, since this could create confusion about\nwhether issued CBDC units should be recognised in the central bank balance\nsheet. In such case, it would also be unclear whether the CBDC can actually\nbe deemed to be central bank money.\n\nThese considerations apply irrespective of whether or not a CBDC is granted\nlegal tender status. If the CBDC is granted such status, it must be clarified\nwhether cash will remain legal tender and whether CBDC holders will be\nentitled to demand conversion into cash, or vice versa. See chapter 2.6 for\nfurther discussion.\n\n2.2. Parity value with cash and bank deposits\nAny CBDC must have parity value (1:1) with bank deposits and cash and\nother central bank money (central bank reserves). It is inexpedient for the\nsame good or service to have multiple prices in Norwegian kroner depending\non the legal tender used. Correspondingly, it is undesirable for conversion\nrates between different means of payment denominated in Norwegian kroner\nto fluctuate, whether in official marketplaces or unorganised markets. The\nparity value characteristic does not bar the charging of different fees for\npayments made using certain means of payment and payment instruments.\n\nCBDC-cash parity is unlikely to be challenged. This becomes a certainty if\nCBDC units and cash are freely convertible. Moreover, if banks demand\nconvertibility between the CBDC and central bank reserves, free convertibility\nin this regard will be a prerequisite for full parity between all the different types\nof central bank money. However, this will not block the setting of a lower\ninterest rate on the CBDC than on central bank reserves and cash.5 Both the\nCBDC and cash are claims on Norges Bank, and both will be legal tender; see\nchapter 2.6.\n\n\n\n4\n  Norges Bank could potentially also issue a CBDC to banks by increasing the banks’ central bank reserves,\nand in such case the CBDC would not appear as a separate liability item in the central bank balance sheet.\nHowever, this appears both inexpedient and undesirable, not least from the perspective of Norges Bank’s\nliquidity management. The CBDC could potentially also be issued directly to the general public, i.e. without\npreceding distribution to banks. However, this solution appears unrealistic given the working group’s\nconclusion that the CBDC should not be based on an open account solution. In any event, the CBDC would\nstill appear as a separate liability item in Norges Bank’s balance sheet with this solution.\n5\n  It is parity between the CBDC and other central bank liabilities which will prevent the emergence of\nmultiple units of account in the economy, or several versions of the Norwegian krone.\n\n                                                                                                               13\n\f\n=== DOC 4 PAGE 14 ===\nMoreover, breach of CBDC-bank deposit parity6 appears unlikely. However,                              NORGES BANK PAPERS\nparity may still be breached in an extreme situation where depositors are                             NO 1 | 2021\nuncertain about the solvency and liquidity of the entire private banking sector.\n                                                                                                      CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\nIn this scenario, depositors may wish to transfer their funds out of the banking                      CURRENCIES\n                                                                                                      THIRD REPORT FROM THE\nsystem and into CBCDs. CBDCs may appear more attractive than bank                                     WORKING GROUP\ndeposits, and may acquire a higher market value in the “grey market”. The\neffect on market value may intensify if CBDC supply is limited through volume\nrestrictions; see chapter 2.4. Conversion from bank deposits into CBDC units\nwill be simpler in practical terms and entail a lower risk of theft/loss than\nconversion from bank deposits into cash.\n\nHowever, there are also factors which may promote parity:\n\n    -    Banks may increase their deposit rates to compensate depositors for\n         the perceived increase in credit and liquidity risk. In a major crisis, the\n         interest spread relative to the CBDC may become substantial.\n    -    The Norwegian deposit guarantee scheme protects deposits of up to\n         NOK 2 million per depositor per bank. This sum must be made\n         available to the depositor within seven working days. Comprehensive\n         information on the guarantee scheme can help to limit depositor flight\n         to CBDCs and reduce the pressure on parity.\n\nIt appears unnecessary for the requirement of parity value between the CBDC\nand bank deposits to confer greater protection than is currently available in the\ncase of cash and bank deposits. Otherwise, the solution would eliminate credit\nrisk linked to bank deposits.\n\nThe characteristic of CBDC-bank deposit parity is difficult to test.\nNevertheless, it is possible to simulate a breach of parity, and assumptions\nregarding depositor conduct can be made based on sources such as historical\ndata on banking crises.\n\n2.3. Customer orientation\nAny CBDC must have a customer orientation. This means that the CBDC\nmust be accessible to a broad audience and that infrastructure must be in\nplace to make the CBDC suitable for customer payments, including user-\nfriendliness and security requirements.\n\nA CBDC solution will only fulfil the purposes of functioning as a contingency\nsolution and promoting competition if use exceeds the minimum level\nnecessary for the solution to be both accessible and usable in practice. To\nachieve the minimum usage level, user interfaces for payers and payees must\nbe attractive.\n\nCore infrastructure7 should be designed to be compatible with different\npayment instruments and different payment situations (such as physical trade,\nonline shopping and transfers between private persons). The objective of\n\n\n\n6\n  Central bank money (cash, CBDCs and central bank reserves) has a unique function as a unit of\nmeasurement in the economy. Banks use central bank money as a unit of measurement and thus promise\nparity between private bank deposits and central bank money; see White (2001), page 8.\n7\n  The primary core features of any CBDC system are the issuance and cancellation of CBDC units, the\nregister/accounts system IT architecture and system rules/”protocols”.\n\n                                                                                                      14\n\f\n=== DOC 4 PAGE 15 ===\ncontingency preparedness requires the CBDC to be compatible with use at               NORGES BANK PAPERS\nphysical points of sale.                                                              NO 1 | 2021\n\n                                                                                      CENTRAL BANK DIGITAL\nTo ensure a minimum level of use, it will probably be necessary to allow third        CURRENCIES\n                                                                                      THIRD REPORT FROM THE\nparties to develop attractive new user interfaces or to link the infrastructure       WORKING GROUP\nwith existing payment solutions. Any test phase should investigate whether\ninfrastructure can be linked to different payment instruments, such as mobile\ntelephones and payment cards, including existing solutions in this field. Such\ninvestigation should also cover which standards can and should be used to\nensure this.\n\nUsing third-party payment instruments and solutions may give rise to tensions\nin relation to other characteristics, for example that a CBDC should be\ncontrolled by Norges Bank and that the CBDC should be technically\nindependent of banking systems.\n\nMoreover, some parts of the population may find it difficult to use electronic\nmeans of payment, for varying reasons. Any CBDC should therefore have an\nIT architecture that allows Norges Bank or third parties to offer simple,\naccessible solutions. This is examined further in chapter 2.9 on IT\ninfrastructure and in the discussion of user interfaces in chapter 3.\n\nConsideration should be given to whether the CBDC should be available to\ntourists and others staying in Norway temporarily. However, it seems to make\nlittle sense for a Norwegian CBDC to function as a system for payments or as\na store of value abroad. This will require a technical infrastructure and a\nregulatory framework that facilitate limited intentional use by foreign nationals\nwhile simultaneously eliminating undesirable consequences of granting foreign\nnationals access. Technical testing will shed further light on this issue.\n\n2.4. Adequate frictions in transfers between the\n     CBDC and bank deposits\nThe purpose of securing scope for introducing frictions is to prevent the\nimmediate conversion of private bank deposits into CBDC units on such a\nscale as to cause or reinforce financial instability.\n\nBoth relevant literature and international CBDC discussion forums evidence\nuncertainty about the actual risk of major bank runs.
