For Norges Bank, the paramount question is whether introducing such money is\nan appropriate measure for ensuring that it will be secure, efficient and attractive to pay in Norwegian\nkroner also in future.\n\nIntroducing a CBDC can be a way to ensure access to a means of settlement trusted by all, also in new\npayment arenas, and can also facilitate responsible innovation and improved payment contingency\narrangements. At the same time, we must consider what can be achieved by the use of other measures.\n\nThis report from a Norges Bank working group summarises Phase 4 of the research project. This phase\nhas consisted of experimental testing of technical solutions, analysis of scenarios for the payment\nsystem, evaluation of consequences for liquidity management and monetary policy and a review of the\nlegislative changes necessary to permit the introduction of a CBDC.\n\nThe assessment of a CBDC raises complex issues, and the current payment system in Norway functions\nwell, which is why we should not proceed with undue haste. Nevertheless, against the backdrop of\nfalling cash usage, the emergence of new forms of money and payment platforms and work on CBDCs in\nother countries, introducing a CBDC is more relevant now than when the Bank’s research into this issue\nbegan in 2016.\n\nThe research project will therefore continue in a new phase until the end of 2025. We will analyse the\npossibilities afforded by, and the impact of, introducing a CBDC and test candidate solutions. In addition\nto researching a CBDC intended for general purpose users, we will research new forms of settlement in\ncentral bank money that can facilitate innovation related to bank deposits and financial assets in a\ntokenised format. To obtain knowledge and contribute to international standardisation and\ncooperation, we will work with other central banks and international organisations.\n\nThe purpose of publishing this report – and other material from the research project – is to provide\ninformation about this work, disseminate knowledge and foster dialogue.\n\nIda Wolden Bache\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 4 ===\n                                                                                                              4\n\n\n1. Introduction\n\nA working group 1 at Norges Bank has conducted a fourth phase of a study project on central bank digital\ncurrency (CBDC). The work builds on the reports from the first three phases (see Norges Bank (2018),\n(2019) and (2021)). This is the final report from the fourth phase. Material from some sub-projects is\nalso published (see information below).\n\nWhat is CBDC?\nCBDC is electronic money issued by the central bank in the official unit of account. Internationally, two\nmain variants of CBDC are being studied: a retail CBDC for end users such as consumers and businesses\nand wholesale CBDC for settlement between banks and other financial actors.\n\nRetail CBDC is generally accessible to the public on a par with cash and bank deposits. Banks and other\nthird parties will have the possibility to develop and offer payment services to private individuals,\ncompanies and the public sector using CBDC as their underlying means of payment. Unless otherwise\nmentioned or indicated by context, it is the retail variant of CBDC that is discussed in the report.\n\nWholesale CBDC is central bank reserves in tokenised form. 2 Wholesale CBDC would only be available to\nbanks and some other financial actors with an account in the central bank. It will be possible to use such\nCBDC for payment settlements in the same way as the current interbank settlements at Norges Bank are\nused, but in a different technological form.\n\nInternational developments\nMany central banks are exploring CBDC. A survey 3 by the Bank for International Settlements (BIS)\ntowards the end of 2022 shows that 93 percent of a wide range of central banks are studying CBDC. Of\nthese, almost three-quarters investigated both retail CBDC and wholesale CBDC, while a good quarter\nonly examined a retail CBDC. The European Central Bank (ECB)/Eurosystem, the Riksbank, the Bank of\nEngland, the Federal Reserve and the Bank of Japan are among the central banks that are investigating\nCBDC. So far, only a few central banks in other parts of the world have introduced (retail) CBDC. 4\n\nInternational organisations, such as the International Monetary Fund (IMF) and BIS, are investing\nconsiderable resources into the analysis of various CBDC-related issues. The BIS Innovation Hub (BISIH)\nhas been established to experiment with how new technology can strengthen the financial system, and\nCBDC is a central topic. See Section 3 and Norges Bank (2023) for further details on work on CBDC\ninternationally.\n\n\n\n1\n  The working group was composed of Knut Sandal (leader), Ragna Alstadheim, Tom Bernhardsen, Espen Gjøs,\nArne Kloster, Suela Kristiansen, Helge Syrstad, Kjetil Watne, Peder Østbye and Terje Åmås. At the end of the\nproject phase, the steering group was composed of Torbjørn Hægeland (chair), Ole Christian Bech-Moen, Gaute\nLangeland, Kasper Roszbach and Marius Ryel. Olav Bø and Ketil Rakkestad were members of the steering\ncommittee in parts of Phase 4. In the sub-project on experimental testing of technical solutions, Lasse Meholm\nfrom the company Finansit has participated as external project coordinator and four Norwegian IT companies have\nbeen engaged, see Section 3 and Norges Bank (2023c).\n2\n  See box with explanation of terms at the end of the report.\n3\n  The survey is documented in Kosse and Mattei (2023).\n4\n  The Bahamas, Nigeria, the Eastern Caribbean and Jamaica have introduced CBDC, while China, India, Ghana and\nUruguay have or have had pilot projects with real payments.\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 5 ===\n                                                                                                              5\n\n\nNorges Bank's research\nThe purpose of the Bank's research project is to assess whether the introduction of CBDC is appropriate\nto ensure that it is secure, efficient and attractive to pay with NOK also in the future. The project is to\nprovide a basis for decision-making and a recommendation as to whether Norges Bank should work to\nintroduce CBDC and, if so, in what form and manner its introduction can be implemented.\n\nFalling cash use, new technological opportunities, the emergence of new money and payment systems\nand work on CBDC in other countries form the background for the project. The introduction of CBDC\ncan, among other things, ensure access to a means of settlement 5 that everyone trusts, also in new\npayment arenas, and facilitate responsible innovation and improved contingency arrangements. At the\nsame time, introducing CBDC is not the only – and not necessarily the best – way this can be achieved.\nThe introduction of retail CBDC and/or wholesale CBDC must therefore be assessed in relation to the\nuse of other instruments, such as the regulation of private means of payment and systems.\n\nNorges Bank started its CBDC project in 2016. Phases 1-3 of the project have included assessments of\nthe purpose of CBDC, the characteristics of CBDC, relevant technical solutions for CBDC, schemes for\ntechnical testing and the implications of introducing CBDC for banks.\n\nThe work in Phase 4 of the project has built on the analyses from previous phases and enriched certain\naspects. The purpose of Phase 4 has been to strengthen the decision-making basis for whether CBDC\nshould be introduced by conducting experimental testing of technical solutions and analysing the\npurpose and consequences of the introduction of CBDC.\n\nThe work has been carried out in four sub-projects:\n\n    •   Discussion of the adoption of a precautionary approach as motivation for the introduction of\n        CBDC: Analysis of scenarios for the development of the payment system that may entail risks in\n        Norges Bank's areas of responsibility, and assessment of whether the introduction of CBDC or\n        other instruments is appropriate to reduce the risks.\n    •   Experimental testing and other validation of technical solutions to assess whether different\n        technologies can ensure the necessary characteristics of CBDC required to fulfil its purpose.\n    •   Assessment of the implications for Norges Bank's liquidity management regime and the impact\n        of monetary policy through the introduction of CBDC with various design elements.\n    •   Review of necessary legislative amendments to introduce CBDC. The introduction will affect a\n        number of provisions on payments and settlement pertaining to both private and statutory law.\n\nIn parallel with the work in the sub-projects, several developments have influenced the assessments of\nelements that should be included in Phase 5. This applies, for example, to work on CBDC in other central\nbanks and the BISIH, as well as developments regarding stablecoins and tokenised bank deposits. The\ninternational debate on wholesale CBDC and other forms of settlement in central bank money of\npayments and trading on tokenised platforms has also influenced the assessments.\n\n\n\n\n5\n Money has three functions: medium of exchange or means of payment/settlement, unit of account and store of\nvalue.\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 6 ===\n                                                                                                          6\n\n\nSo far, the project has mainly focused on retail CBDC. As discussed below, we recommend that the next\nproject phase include the study of wholesale CBDC and other forms of settlement in central bank money\nof payments and trading on tokenised platforms.\n\nSections 2 to 5 summarise the work, lessons drawn and assessments in the sub-projects mentioned.\nSection 6 contains a recommendation on required elements for the content of the upcoming phase 5.\n\n2. A precautionary approach motivating the introduction of CBDC – analysis of payment\n   system scenarios\nThe payment system is changing. New financial actors are emerging, new payment services are being\ndeveloped and new technology is providing fresh opportunities. This may lead to a more secure and\nefficient payment system, but some developments may also lead to a situation in which Norges Bank's\nscope for exercising its corporate social responsibility is reduced. In a sub-project, we have assessed\nsome scenarios for the development of the Norwegian payment system.\nThe purpose of the scenario analysis was to be explorative and to shed light on how adopting a\nprecautionary approach to threats from new monetary and payment systems and new types of financial\nactors may be part of the motivation for introducing CBDC. The relevant main scenarios we have\nconsidered are the following:\n    •   Increased use of crypto-assets, including stablecoins in foreign currencies and Norwegian kroner\n        (NOK).\n    •   Increased presence of global financial actors/bigtechs within the payment system.\n    •   Other central banks introduce CBDC that is available to Norwegian users.\nWe also point out that the introduction of CBDC is relevant if there is no prospect of private money in\nNOK with attractive new functionality, ensuring access to a means of settlement that everyone trusts,\nalso in new arenas. In this case, CBDC is therefore motivated by the payment system’s potential failure\nto develop in a desired direction, rather than the payment system’s potential development in an\nundesirable direction.\nWe consider the three scenarios to be plausible, but they do not necessarily represent expected\ndevelopments. We have not assessed the probability of them occurring, but are discussing factors that\nmay shed light on and influence the probability. The time perspective in the analyses is in the range of 3-\n10 years.\n\nThe scenarios entail various forms of innovation in the payment system, which may entail both benefits\nand risks. Here we have focused on risks/potential negative consequences. The two most important\nrisks in Norges Bank's areas of responsibility are linked to currency substitution away from NOK and\ndiminished national control over the payment system. The first risk can undermine the impact of\nmonetary policy and may result in our losing a common unit of calculation or measurement for the cost\nof goods and services and shall be traded in. The second risk may lower the efficiency and security of the\npayment system and financial stability.\n\nThe degree of risk depends on the extent to which Norwegian users will use means of payment in\ncurrencies other than NOK and from parties other than financial actors in the Norwegian payment\nsystem. This will depend, among other things, on whether users perceive that these alternatives inspire\ntrust and can offer attractive functionality, good conditions and network advantages. Such risk increases\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 7 ===\n                                                                                                                 7\n\n\nif changes in the payment system coincide with changes in trading patterns. Regulations, particularly the\nextent to which they permit the use of alternative means of payment, will also affect risk.\nThe literature assesses different, more macroeconomically oriented explanatory factors for currency\nsubstitution than factors in the payment system, such as instability in the value of money and limited\nconfidence in monetary policy. We consider the risk of currency substitution to be minor. The first line\nof defence against currency substitution is a well-executed monetary policy that inspires confidence,\nand an otherwise responsible macroeconomic policy. This implies that currently the risk of currency\nsubstitution in Norway is relatively limited. However, we cannot exclude the possibility that highly\nsecured stablecoins denominated in foreign currency issued by, for example, bigtechs, or CBDC from\ncountries with significant financial links to Norway, such as a digital euro, may be used by the Norwegian\npublic to a certain extent, even though holdings of such assets for payment purposes entail currency risk\nif the funds are to be used in Norway. A gateway to such means of payment may be the use of mobile\napplications when shopping online and travelling. 6\nAll three scenarios – or combinations of them, such as bigtechs offering stablecoins – entail a risk of\nweakened national control over the payment system. Bigtechs are very large companies who have their\nprimary activity in digital services, for example in social media, search engines, e-commerce and\nIT/telephony equipment. A key part of bigtechs' business model is to collect, analyse and commercially\nexploit information about users. The size of customer networks and the use of customer data also make\nit easier for them to achieve economies of scale if they step into payment and financial services. The\nextent to which this could lead to weakened national control over the payment system will – in addition\nto customer demand for services from bigtechs and regulations – depend on how deep in the payment\nchain bigtechs will be.\nCBDC can be designed to facilitate new functionality that can meet the competition from bigtechs\nand/or token-based money such as stablecoins. CBDC can contribute to interoperability and make it\neasier for customers to move money between payment platforms, and central banks can use CBDC to\nset standards for the appropriate development of new functionality in the payment system.\n\nHowever, it is not obvious that introducing retail CBDC is necessary to ensure a payment system offering\nfunctionality that makes it attractive to pay in NOK also in the future. The introduction of CBDC is\nrelevant if there is no prospect of private money with such functionality, and which can ensure access to\na means of settlement in which everyone has confidence, also in new arenas. Furthermore, the question\nof whether current private money can provide a sufficiently competitively neutral and robust\ninfrastructure for such functionality arises.\nTechnological experimentation and literature on tokenisation of bank deposits is a relevant new\ndevelopment, especially internationally. Domestic banks’ issuance of stablecoins in national currency is\na variant of this development. In both areas, developments have been fairly limited, and Norges Bank\nshould monitor further developments. If the trend is towards more tokenised bank money, this also\nraises the question of how the settlement of payments with such money should take place and, if\napplicable, can facilitate the development of such money, including whether the introduction of\nsettlement with tokenised reserves (wholesale CBDC) is appropriate.\n\n\n\n\n6\n    Alstadheim (2023) discusses the risk of currency substitution associated with the use of cryptocurrencies.\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 8 ===\n                                                                                                              8\n\n\nIn summary, our assessment so far is that with respect to the precautionary approach, introducing retail\nCBDC is not a very urgent matter. 7 The idea that the introduction of retail CBDC is the most adequate\ninstrument for managing risks in Norges Bank's areas of responsibility associated with new monetary\nand payment systems is uncertain as well. Nevertheless, we cannot exclude the possibility that highly\nsecured stablecoins in foreign currency issued by, for example, bigtechs, or CBDC from countries with\nsignificant economic relations to Norway, may be used by Norwegian audiences to a certain extent.\nNorges Bank should therefore pursue its assessment of how to ensure that payments in NOK offer\nrequired functionality demanded by end users in the future. Retail CBDC, wholesale CBDC, other\nchanges in the settlement system and regulatory instruments are all relevant in such an assessment.\n\n3. Experimental testing and other forms of validation of technical solutions\nThis sub-project has conducted experimental testing of relevant technology as part of a broader\nvalidation work. A separate report on the sub-project will be published in Norges Bank (2023c).\nThe purpose of this work has been to validate whether technology can deliver the necessary\ncharacteristics of CBDC, required to fulfil its purpose, as defined in Norges Bank (2021). Furthermore,\nthe purpose of the work was to highlight economic and regulatory factors related to the use of these\ntechnologies, as well as serve as a basis for dialogue with the banking/financial industry, other\nauthorities and other central banks. The validation work has also helped to build the expertise necessary\nto assess how the work on CBDC should be pursued.\nIn its work, the project has engaged in dialogue with a number of private financial actors (including\nNorwegian and international technology companies as well as banks established in Norway), authorities,\nother central banks and international organisations such as the BISIH. We have accounted for the\ninvestigation into CBDC at many conferences and seminars, as well as arranged two conferences and\nthree hackathons, all in collaboration with others.\nThe experimental testing was conducted by constructing a series of test cases, each validating one or\nseveral characteristics (see Figure 1). Examples of test cases are 1) issuance and destruction of CBDC, 2)\nmass payments and 3) cross-border payments.\nThe testing was mainly conducted in a test network/prototype and blockchain technology\n(private/closed Ethereum network). The prototype is a simple core infrastructure for CBDC: a\nregistry/database and functionality for issuing, distributing, confiscation and settlement of CBDC.\nThe prototype was developed by a Norwegian IT company that has been engaged to develop\napplications for testing. In addition to the prototype, we have tested other technologies on a smaller\nscale, such as OpenCBDC.\n\nAn important finding is that the characteristics from Norges Bank (2021) so far appear to be achievable\nwith the technology employed in the testing. This may indicate that it is possible to introduce CBDC with\nthe characteristics we believe are required. However, we must make the reservation that we have not\nsufficiently tested all the characteristics yet. Moreover, not all of the characteristics’ aspects are suitable\nto be verified by means of technical testing. It should also be noted that conclusions from the testing so\nfar do not imply that the technology we have tested necessarily represents the most appropriate\ntechnology for the CBDC core infrastructure.\n\n\n7\n Note that any customer-oriented CBDC may have a different/broader purpose than that of the precautionary\napproach, e.g. related to contingency arrangements for payment (see Norges Bank (2021) and Norges Bank (2023).\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 9 ===\n                                                                                                          9\n\n\nFigure 1. Conducting testing\n\n\n\n\nSource: Norges Bank\n\nSome elements of the testing have not previously been carried out by other central banks. The testing\nhas also been the starting point for useful cooperation with other central banks and other external\nfinancial actors, e.g. in the project Icebreaker discussed below.\nThe technology choices that were made, including the use of known technology based on open source,\nhave been an important factor in our investigation. If a more unknown and/or proprietary technology\nhad been chosen, the testing would unlikely have advanced as much as it has. For example, it would\nprobably have been challenging to find IT expertise to develop a prototype, develop test cases and hold\nhackathons if a little-used and/or proprietary base technology had been chosen.\nMany tests and test cases have been carried out. At the same time, continued experimental testing\nthrough the establishment of new test cases and further development of existing test cases should\nprovide further needed insight for the potential introduction of CBDC. It should also be a springboard for\nfurther cooperation with external actors. Among other things, privacy solutions and solutions for\nregulatory compliance can be further elucidated. Test cases should also be developed for performance\ntesting (e.g. number of transactions per second and time of final settlement) and security testing.\nFurthermore, testing incentive structures and new business models for third parties (e.g. banks),\nretailers and consumers is needed.\nOne lesson from testing is that different types of technology and maintenance of registries (databases of\ntransactions) offer diverse functions that are to varying degrees suitable to ensure the necessary\ncharacteristics of CBDC. Thus, to exploit the advantages and disadvantages inherent in different registry\nsolutions, it may be appropriate for CBDC to use several registries linked together through bridges. By\nway of such bridges, the relative advantages of different types of registries can be exploited. However,\nbridges raise some special challenges that have been highlighted in the experimental testing.\nIn principle, the core infrastructure (primary registry/account system, arrangements for issuing,\ndistributing and confiscation of CBDC and interface, inter alia to Norges Bank's settlement system) can\nbe developed in three ways:\n    •   In-house development.\n    •   Purchases from suppliers who offer more or less fully developed comprehensive CBDC solutions\n        or parts thereof, possibly in combination with in-house development.\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 10 ===\n                                                                                                            10\n\n\n       •   Association with CBDC infrastructure of other central banks.\nSo far, only a few central banks in other parts of the world have introduced CBDC or have pilots with real\npayments. Most of these central banks use systems from external suppliers of comprehensive solutions,\nsome of them based on token technology. So far, its use seems to be limited.\n\nMany central banks are studying CBDC. A number of central banks have – often with the help of external\nsuppliers – developed various forms of prototypes (simple test versions) in order to gain more\nknowledge about various technological solutions and how they can contribute to fulfilling the desired\npurpose. Overall, this is similar to Norges Bank’s course of action, but differences also appear, e.g. in\nterms of scope and use of resources.\n\nFurthermore, a number of tests pertaining to CBDC are carried out in collaboration with, or under the\nauspices of, the BISIH. One such project was Project Icebreaker on international payments with CBDC. 8\nThis was a collaboration between Norges Bank, Sveriges Riksbank, Bank of Israel and BISIH in autumn\nand winter 2022-23. Participation in such cooperation and participation as observers in other projects\nhave provided and will provide Norges Bank with useful insights for further work on CBDC.\n\nIn October 2023, the Governing Council of the ECB decided that work on a digital euro continues in a\n“digital euro preparation phase.” 9 In parallel, the European Union (EU) is working on necessary\nlegislative amendments to introduce a digital euro.\n\nThe digital euro project discusses, among other things, whether several central banks can use the same\ninfrastructure for CBDC in different currencies. The ECB (2023c, p. 2) states that multi-currency\npayments with CBDC can be facilitated in various ways, including:\n           \"... by following a single-system approach whereby a common technical infrastructure would\n           host multiple CBDC issued by their respective central banks. The priority for the Eurosystem is to\n           ensure the timely delivery of a digital euro that serves the needs of euro area users. If a digital\n           euro is introduced successfully in the euro area, the provision of cross-currency functionalities\n           could be supported where there are mutual interests with other monetary jurisdictions.\"\nA potential use of the Eurosystem's CBDC infrastructure is thus likely to apply not only to cross-border\npayments, but also to domestic CBDC payments in national currencies in non-euro countries.\nIn general, our assessment, based on the testing work and information obtained from suppliers of CBDC\nsolutions to other countries, is that there are currently no available fully developed solutions that can\nsatisfactorily fulfil the requirements and necessary characteristics of a Norwegian CBDC.\nOur experimental testing and analysis so far indicate that fulfilling the characteristics is possible.\nHowever, we believe it is premature to draw a conclusion about which technological solution for the\ncore infrastructure of a potential Norwegian CBDC is most appropriate. CBDC technology is developing\nrapidly because there is a strong willingness to invest. It is likely that more suitable solutions will be\ndeveloped for Norwegian CBDC (i.e. that can fulfil the requirements for characteristics even better) in\nthe coming years, both by private financial actors and as a result of development and testing work\nperformed by other central banks.\n\n\n\n8\n    See BIS Innovation Hub, Bank of Israel, Norges Bank and Sveriges Riksbank (2023).\n9\n    See ECB (2023a) and ECB (2023b).\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 11 ===\n                                                                                                         11\n\n\nAt the same time, information obtained by the BISIH shows that the suppliers offering CBDC solutions\nhave chosen very different technologies, design and security solutions. There is reason to believe that if\nCBDC becomes more widespread, there will be convergence and standardisation in solutions. A lack of\nstandardisation in a start-up phase may mean that central banks that introduce CBDC early may later\nhave to implement substantial upgrades if the solution they chose ended up failing to meet up to the\nfuture standard.\nMore generally, CBDC infrastructure developed and/or tested in other central banks may be relevant\ntechnology for Norwegian CBDC. In Phase 5, the project should therefore assess the characteristics of,\nand advantages and disadvantages of, a connection to other central banks’ CBDC infrastructure,\nincluding a potential digital euro infrastructure.\nNorges Bank’s evaluation, in the coming years, regarding the pertinence of connecting to the\nEurosystem's TARGET settlement system is an additional element in this assessment. 10 A potential\nassociation with TARGET could entail both advantages and disadvantages for a potential connection to\nthe digital euro infrastructure. If both the CBDC and the ordinary settlement system use\nECB/Eurosystem infrastructure, advantages may be gained, among other things, in links between the\nsystems and other forms of harmonisation. On the other hand, a large part of the core of the Norwegian\npayment system would then be dependent on one and the same actor. Such factors must be further\nassessed.\nDeveloping the core infrastructure for a full-scale CBDC solution is a complex task. The working group is\nof the opinion that it is probably not appropriate for Norges Bank itself to develop and maintain the core\ninfrastructure, given the resources this requires and the role Norges Bank must then take on. This\napplies both to work performed by the Bank's own employees and work performed with external\nsuppliers’ support.\nWhen Phase 4 started, the work was aimed at validating the characteristics listed in Norges Bank (2021).\nThese characteristics have been useful aids in the testing work. At the same time, experience shows that\nthe characteristics are slightly interchangeable and can be more adequately consolidated and\nsystematised. In the next phase, it is recommended that the project identifies requirements for a\nNorwegian CBDC that are better suited to differentiating various solutions and serve as a starting point\nfor a more detailed requirements specification for the Norwegian CBDC solution.\n\nFurther work on studying a full-scale solution should be agile and exploratory:\n       •   The resources should initially be invested in identifying overarching requirements for a\n           Norwegian CBDC and, based on these, preparing a requirements specification for a full-scale\n           solution.\n       •   Work on a regulatory framework for a full-scale solution should also be carried out, including\n           business models and incentive structures for the financial actors involved. We will be completely\n           dependent on banks and other payment service providers facilitating payment services for end\n           customers based on CBDC as a source of money. This can be just as extensive and demanding a\n           task – both for the private sector to implement in a good manner and for Norges Bank to follow\n           up – as to establish a core infrastructure.\n       •   Experimental testing should be pursued, because it increases our competence, matures the\n           assessment of technological, legal and economic factors, tests various crucial components and\n           provides insight into requirements for a potential Norwegian CBDC solution. Experimental\n\n10\n     See, among other documents, Norges Bank (2023a) and Norges Bank (2023b).\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 12 ===\n                                                                                                                 12\n\n\n           testing should continue to be a catalyst for cooperation with other central banks and private\n           financial actors/suppliers working to develop full-scale, performable solutions.\n\n4. Implications for Norges Bank's liquidity management and the impact of monetary policy\nDepending on how CBDC is designed, CBDC can influence Norges Bank's liquidity management and\nconduct of monetary policy. This issue has been considered in further detail in a separate sub-project.\nThe paper from the sub-project will be published in Bernhardsen and Kloster (2023).\n\nIn its liquidity management, Norges Bank aims to keep banks' deposits in the central bank overnight –\nthe central bank reserves – around NOK 35 billion. As with the public's demand for cash, CBDC will be an\nautonomous factor on the liabilities side of the central bank's balance sheet, i.e. they affect the amount\nof central bank reserves beyond the central bank's control. Norges Bank counteracts such autonomous\nfactors with market operations (F-loans and F-deposits) with a view to keeping the shortest money\nmarket rates close to the policy rate. This is how monetary policy is implemented.\n\nCBDC may present some challenges for today's financial system, depending on how CBDC is designed.\nThese challenges were also discussed in Norges Bank (2018). The potential challenges will be greater if\nCBDC becomes well suited as a store of value, so that public demand for CBDC may be high. High\ndemand for CBDC can affect banks' balance sheets, funding costs and the monetary and credit-\ngenerating process, as well as the central bank's balance sheet and risk, and the implementation of\nliquidity and monetary policy. The reason for this is that banks may lose deposit funding in the case of\ndemand for CBDC: Banks' customers may wish to exchange part of their bank deposits for CBDC. Banks\ncan meet this challenge by raising their deposit rates. The quantity of CBDC that will be demanded in a\nnew equilibrium is uncertain. To supply CBDC to their customers, banks must purchase them from the\ncentral bank and pay with central bank reserves. On banks' balance sheets, deposits from the general\npublic and deposits in Norges Bank are reduced to the same extent. If banks lack sufficient central bank\nreserves, they must borrow from the central bank.\n\nThis is entirely parallel to effects of the public requesting cash. However, if demand for CBDC in a new\nequilibrium becomes substantially higher than demand for cash, banks’ need to borrow from the central\nbank may exceed the value of their approved collateral. Ultimately, the central bank may have to accept\ncollateral with higher credit risk. At the same time, greater uncertainty may arise as to the composition\nof banks' liabilities and funding costs. Uncertainty related to funding costs and interest margins may\ninfluence banks' lending behaviour. Banks may be less willing to extend credit to the public. The overall\neffect is ambiguous and situational. 11\n\nLiterature has extensively shed light on these factors. Much of the discussion revolves around how CBDC\ncan be designed to contribute to a more efficient payment system, while avoiding or at least significantly\nreducing these financial challenges. Reducing financial challenges implies that CBDC should be designed\nto enable the management of demand. This can be done via quantitative restrictions (quotas) or\nthrough a pricing mechanism (interest rate). Quotas, in the form of a limit on how much CBDC each\nperson can hold, provide a secure limit on the amount of CBDC outstanding. However, a quota system\n\n\n11\n     Alstadheim and Søvik (2021) discuss how banks' lending rates may be affected by the introduction of CBDC.\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 13 ===\n                                                                                                         13\n\n\nmay not provide much flexibility for adapting to individual preferences, at the same time as it may also\nbring forth other challenges within the payment system. The use of interest rates does not result in a\nlimit that is as stable for the total amount of CBDC. However, it provides greater flexibility for the\nindividual and potential challenges in the payment system are eliminated. With the use of interest rates,\na two-stage system can be introduced, where the interest rate on CBDC holding within a fixed individual\namount limit is higher than the interest rate on holdings beyond the limit. The purpose of such a system\nis to support CBDC's role as an attractive means of payment, but at the same time to be able to control\ndemand for a store of value. Pros and cons of various solutions for managing the demand for CBDC are\ndiscussed in further detail in Bernhardsen and Kloster (2023).\n\nThe report also discusses a claim that is often highlighted in the literature, namely that banks as a group\ncan replace deposit funding with wholesale funding to meet the need for central bank reserves when\nthe public requests CBDC. This may be possible for an individual bank, but not for the banking system as\na whole. For the banking system as a whole, loss of deposit funding in such a situation can only be\nreplaced by central bank funding (because banks must \"settle\" in central bank reserves when they lose\ncustomer deposits). The fact that banks' loss of deposit funding can only be replaced by central bank\nfunding is also discussed in the report. If the public’s CBDC storage/holdings reach a high level in a new\nequilibrium, the central bank's balance sheet and footprint in the financial system will increase.\n\nThis discussion draws the overall conclusion that CBDC should not primarily be seen as a monetary\npolicy instrument and that liquidity management, with reasonable CBDC designs, will be manageable.\nFurther conclusions are as follows:\n\nLiquidity management\n    •   CBDC is an autonomous factor that influences banks' need for reserves. Under the current\n        system for the management of banks' reserves, the quota system, reserves in the banking\n        system are relatively scarce. In the event of increased demand for CBDC, Norges Bank may have\n        to provide banks with loans (F-loans under the current system). This may lay claim to amounts\n        of banks' collateral that are greater than today and may have an impact on banks' Liquidity\n        Coverage Ratio (LCR).\n    •   Volatile demand for CBDC is likely to make the forecast for the structural liquidity more unclear\n        and subject to unpredictable shifts.\n    •   A number of fine-tuning market operations may be required to keep reserves in the banking\n        system near the liquidity management target.\n    •   Liquidity management is likely to be more unpredictable, but is nevertheless considered\n        manageable, provided that CBDC is designed with a view to substantially reducing the financial\n        disadvantages mentioned above.\n\nMonetary policy\n    •   If CBDC is introduced, the argument in favour of this should primarily be linked to the payment\n        system and not be thought of as an additional monetary policy instrument. It cannot be\n        completely ruled out that CBDC may play a role as a monetary policy instrument in the long\n        term, but this should not be the motive for introducing CBDC in the next few years.\n    •   The introduction of CBDC can reduce monetary policy leeway by diminishing the pass-through\n        from the policy rate to bank rates and by raising the effective lower bound for the policy interest\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 14 ===\n                                                                                                         14\n\n\n           rate. The latter is due to it probably being easier for the general public to convert from bank\n           deposits to CBDC than from bank deposits to cash. 12\n       •   CBDC should be designed in a way that enables the management of demand. This can be\n           achieved by means of individual quotas or by setting interest rates on CBDC. When using\n           interest rates, the introduction of a two-stage system should be considered. The CBDC interest\n           rate cannot be used as a monetary policy instrument while simultaneously being used to\n           regulate CBDC demand.\n\n5. Necessary legislative amendments to provide for the introduction of CBDC\nThe introduction of CBDC for general public use will affect a number of provisions on payments and\nsettlement, both pertaining to private and public law. Among other things, it is necessary to clarify the\nlegal liability placed on the central bank, private banks and other payment system participants. How to\nassess the relationship between payer and payee must also be clarified, if payment is made with CBDC.\nWe have therefore considered the necessary legislative amendments to introduce CBDC, see Syrstad\n(2023). Below is a summary of the legislative amendments that must be made in order to introduce\nCBDC as a generally accessible means of payment for the public.\n\nThe issuance of CBDC will require legislative amendments to Section 3-4 of the Norges Bank Act on the\nissuance of cash as means of payment, including potential legal basis for regulating interest rates on\nCBDC. If CBDC is also to be granted legal tender status, this must be specified in Section 3-5 of the\nNorges Bank Act on legal tender. Norges Bank's responsibility for issuing CBDC should also be set out in\nSection 1-3 of the Norges Bank Act relating to central banking operations.\n\nA well-functioning solution for CBDC also requires clarifications to be made in the provisions of the\nFinancial Contracts Act relating to the method of settlement, under section 2-1, and the time and place\nof payment, under section 2-2. This is necessary if CBDC is to maintain some of the key characteristics of\ncash, which has been a basis for Norges Bank's project. The Financial Contracts Act may also specify that\npayments with CBDC shall be deemed final, and specify that the payments have legal protection against\nthe payer's creditors.\n\nSection 16-4 of the Financial Institutions Act contains provisions on banks' obligation to receive cash and\nmake deposits available to their customers in the form of cash. These provisions must be extended to\ninclude CBDC, if CBDC and cash gain equal status.\n\nVarious provisions in special legislation should be adapted to make CBDC a new, generally accessible\nmeans of payment. If CBDC is to be used for public receipts and payments, regulatory adjustments must\nbe made. This applies in particular to Section 9-1, first paragraph, of the Tax Payment Act relating to\npayment of tax claims and Section 1 relating to payments from the Norwegian Labour and Welfare\nAdministration (NAV), in the regulations relating to the implementation of Section 22-18 of the National\nInsurance Act and Section 34 of the Maintenance Collection Act on the payment method for benefits.\n\nResponsibility for customer due diligence under Section 10 (1) (a) of the Anti-Money Laundering Act will,\nas is the case today, rest with the banks. However, under Section 10(1)(b) and Section 21 it is unclear\n\n\n\n12\n     This is also discussed in Norges Bank (2018), Appendix 1.\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 15 ===\n                                                                                                           15\n\n\nwho will be responsible for transaction monitoring. This must be resolved through legislative\namendments to the Anti-Money Laundering Act.\n\nPotential interest rates on CBDC raise some special issues that should be addressed in both the Norges\nBank Act and special legislation. This applies, among other things, to questions about how public\nreceipts and payments should be made, responsibility for reporting to the tax authorities and customer\ndue diligence measures pursuant to the Anti-Money Laundering Act.\n\nA general point is that the technical design of CBDC may require various legislative amendments. Some\nnecessary and desirable characteristics of CBDC may also require special technical design. Both in\nNorges Bank's project and internationally, particular attention is placed on a solution where CBDC are\nissued and distributed on blockchain-based solutions, i.e. solutions that are wholly or partly based on\nblockchain technology. Compared to account-based solutions, blockchain-based solutions raise some\nspecial questions. This applies, for example, to CBDC’s legal status, if CBDC identifiers are converted\nbetween different blockchains. It must be clarified whether Norges Bank will act as issuer after a\nconversion, and whether the means of payment are to be regarded as \"genuine\" CBDC, or whether they\nare to be regarded as privately issued means of payment (\"synthetic\" CBDC). In simple terms,\n\"conversions\" in this context means that CBDC are moved or transferred between different blockchains.\nFurthermore, one must consider the possibility of CBDC being stored privately by end users (“self-\ncustody”), or the possibility for only approved providers of storage services to be able to store CBDC for\nthe end user.\n\nThe legal status and responsibility of the units that will verify the individual payments, so-called\nvalidating nodes, must also be clarified. One must take into account that this type of issue may be\nfurther regulated, e.g. by a provision of the Norges Bank Act or other financial regulatory legislation.\n\n6. Recommendations on activities and deliveries from the CBDC project Phase 5\nThe study of CBDC will be continued in Phase 5, in line with the Bank's Strategy 25 (see Norges Bank\n(2022, p. 10). It states:\n        \"Norges Bank will prepare the ground for the issue, if appropriate, of a central bank digital\n        currency (CBDC). A CBDC may be necessary to ensure that NOK will continue to be a safe,\n        efficient and attractive means of payment in the future too. During the strategy period, we will\n        analyse the possibilities afforded by, and the consequences of, introducing a CBDC and test\n        candidate solutions. To obtain knowledge and contribute to international standardisation and\n        cooperation, we will work with other central banks and international organisations.\"\n\nWe propose the following recommendations for the content of Phase 5:\n\nThe main delivery should be a report to central bank management with a decision basis for and\nrecommendation as to whether Norges Bank should introduce CBDC and, if so, the recommended type\nof design and implementation.\n\nIn this context, the project should assess both retail CBDC and wholesale CBDC. The analysis should\ninclude an assessment of how the payment system in NOK can develop, with or without the introduction\nof various forms of CBDC, respectively, and by using measures other than the introduction of CBDC. The\nanalysis should, as far as possible, be carried out in accordance with the Instructions for Official Studies,\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 16 ===\n                                                                                                           16\n\n\nthe Ministry of Finance's circular on cost-benefit analysis and the cost-benefit analysis guide drawn up\nby the Norwegian Agency for Public and Financial Management (DFØ). The report should be available so\nthat it can be considered by the Executive Board by the end of 2025.\n\nImportant part deliveries should be:\n\n•   A proposal for the potential design of a full-scale retail CBDC solution. The solution consists of a\n    technical infrastructure and a regulatory framework. This means:\n\n        •   Analyse, identify and determine fundamental requirements and characteristics of a\n            Norwegian CBDC solution.\n\n        •   Develop a requirement specification (functional, security-related and technical\n            requirements) for the infrastructure, which includes, among other things:\n                   • Core infrastructure (primarily ledger/account system, schemes for issuing and\n                        confiscating CBDC and interfaces with, inter alia, Norges Bank's settlement\n                        system).\n                   • Link to payment services from private banks and other suppliers to end\n                        customers.\n                   • At least one dedicated payment service that is independent of banks' systems.\n\n        •   Develop a regulation/framework (\"scheme\") for a full-scale CBDC solution. One should\n            develop a set of rules, guidelines, requirements for/approval of service providers (possibly a\n            requirement to have a relevant licence and be subject to supervision) and agreements\n            describing the roles of the actors involved in a CBDC solution, rights and obligations and set\n            standards for payment notifications, among other things. The framework shall provide an\n            appropriate incentive structure for the stakeholders involved and must be based on an\n            assessment of business models. The framework is also intended to give Norges Bank\n            necessary control over relevant parts of the solution and provide an appropriate division of\n            roles between Norges Bank and private financial actors. This framework comes as an\n            addition to necessary legislative amendments, see below.\n\n        •   Assess how an introduction can be implemented, both for the core infrastructure and for\n            those parts of a full-scale solution that must be facilitated by parties other than Norges\n            Bank.\n\n•   A proposal for how a potential full-scale solution for wholesale CBDC should be designed. To the\n    extent relevant, the proposal should, in the same way as for retail CBDC, describe the technical\n    infrastructure, basic requirements and characteristics of the solution and associated regulations.\n    Various forms of central bank settlement of payments with tokenised bank deposits and the money-\n    related aspect of trades in tokenised assets should be examined. The study should cover\n    opportunities and challenges related to this within Norges Bank's areas of responsibility. This\n    includes examining whether the introduction of wholesale CBDC can help support the development\n    of tokenised private bank money and assets. Such money could potentially offer attractive new\n    functionality for payments in NOK and thus reduce the precautionary approach motivating the\n    introduction of retail CBDC. At the same time, the introduction of wholesale CBDC will have\n    implications for, among other things, Norges Bank's liquidity management.\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 17 ===\n                                                                                                      17\n\n\n\n•   An assessment of whether relevant solutions for retail CBDC and wholesale CBDC raises legal issues\n    beyond those discussed in Syrstad (2023), and potentially offer suggestions as to how this can be\n    resolved. Based on Syrstad (2023) and in light of relevant solutions consider how a possible\n    requirement for CBDC as legal tender should be regulated.\n\nThe assessment of technical infrastructure for retail CBDC and wholesale CBDC should be based on\nseveral work processes:\n\n•   Experimental testing of technical solutions to gain more insight into which solutions can best\n    provide the characteristics necessary for CBDC in Norway. Norges Bank (2023c) provides a more\n    detailed description of and recommendations on how experimental testing can be further\n    developed. Experimental testing helps us to assess which solutions can best provide the necessary\n    characteristics of CBDC. There is therefore no conflict between continuing experimental testing and\n    our assessment that Norges Bank should probably not develop infrastructure in-house.\n\n•   Mapping of relevant actors who offer all or parts of CBDC solutions, and who may be engaged in an\n    introduction of CBDC in Norway.\n\n•   An assessment of whether participation in other central banks' CBDC solutions may be a relevant\n    alternative. Norges Bank would then issue CBDC in NOK using such a technical platform.\n\nThe project should cooperate/engage in dialogue with other central banks, international organisations,\nacademia and other external experts and potential actors within and users of a Norwegian CBDC\nsolution. The project should establish and operate appropriate bodies, such as reference groups, for\ncommunication with and input from stakeholders in Norway.\n\nThe project should take into account the report from the public commission that examines secure and\neasy payments for all (see Government, 2023).\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 18 ===\n                                                                                                            18\n\n\nBox: Concepts\n\n Wholesale CBDC: Tokenised central bank reserves that can be used for settlement between banks\n and certain other financial actors with an account at the central bank. Such settlement corresponds\n to today's interbank settlement in Norges Bank, but in a different technological form and potentially\n with a different setup for access.\n\n Cryptoassets: Collective term for cryptocurrencies, stablecoins and tokens (see below). Often used in\n regulatory contexts to refer to assets represented by cryptographic codes in decentralised systems.\n\n Cryptocurrency: Units on a ledger or computer system that are designed to operate in a\n decentralised manner. The ledger is often referred to as a blockchain. The units are\n accessed/disposed of through cryptographic codes. The system itself can be referred to as a\n cryptocurrency system, while the units are cryptocurrencies.\n\n Central bank reserves: Banks' and other professional financial actors’ demand deposits in the central\n bank. Banks need central bank reserves for interbank settlements.\n\n Smart contracts: A smart contract is a computer program that automates performances between\n parties according to predefined terms. Often used to refer to programs in a cryptocurrency system.\n\n Stablecoins: Cryptoassets that strive for stable value against a benchmark (e.g. USD) through a\n stabilisation mechanism. Often implemented as tokens in a smart contract in a cryptocurrency\n system. Attempts may be made to secure their stable value through external assets managed by an\n external market participant, external cryptoassets and/or algorithms that influence supply and\n demand.\n\n Tokens: Items of value in a cryptocurrency system, often issued in a smart contract. Disposed of\n through cryptographic codes. Tokens can be exchangeable (fungible) or non-exchangeable (Non-\n fungible token – NFT). The latter represents a unique value, such as a digital work of art or objects in\n a virtual game. Such non-exchangeable tokens can also represent other traditional assets, such as\n securities and real estate.\n\n Tokenisation: The process of representing assets/liabilities in a digital form, which allows them to be\n traded in cryptocurrency systems/on programmable platforms using smart contracts.\n\n\n\nReferences\nAlstadheim, R. (2023). On increased use of cryptocurrencies and risk of currency substitution in Norway,\nBankplassen blog, 10.08.2023, On increased use of cryptocurrencies and risk of currency substitution in\nNorway (norges-bank.no)\nAlstadheim, R. and Søvik, Y. (2021). Will banks' lending rates increase with the introduction of a central\nbank digital currency? Bankplassen blog, Will bank lending rates increase with the introduction of a\ncentral bank digital currency? (norges-bank.no)\n\n\n=== DOC 5 PAGE 19 ===\n                                                                                                          19\n\n\nBernhardsen, T. and Kloster, A. (2023). Drivers behind this exploration include falling cash usage, the potential of new\ntechnologies, the emergence of new money and payment systems and insights drawn from CBDC work\nin other central banks.\nThe exploration of a CBDC was included in Norges Bank’s Strategy 2023–2025, which stated that we\nwere to \"... be ready, if appropriate, to issue a central bank digital currency. A CBDC may be necessary to\nensure that NOK will continue to be a safe, efficient and attractive means of payment in the future\ntoo.\" (Norges Bank (2022), p.10).\nThe primary task of the exploration in the strategy period was to establish a basis for a decision on\nwhether Norges Bank should issue a CBDC, and if so, in what form and with which characteristics.\nMain conclusion in the exploration so far\nNorges Bank has concluded that: \"... the introduction of either a retail or wholesale CBDC is currently not\nwarranted. However, the situation and needs could change. Norges Bank will therefore be ready to\nintroduce a central bank digital currency if it becomes necessary to maintain an efficient and secure\npayment system.\" The decision was published in a news item on Norges Bank's website on 10 December\n2025. (Norges Bank (2025)).\nThe conclusion is based on the assessment that the introduction of a CBDC is probably not the most\nsuitable measure for promoting payment system innovation, strengthening preparedness or in another\nway contribute to a more efficient and secure payment system at this stage.\nThe need for issuing a CBDC in Norway may change. Technological developments in the financial\nsystem may advance rapidly, and new services and participants are emerging. The application of\nblockchain technology and tokenisation in the financial sector may provide opportunities for innovation,\nefficiency gains and reduced settlement risk. The introduction of a CBDC may be necessary to safeguard\nsocietal considerations as these potential developments unfold. At the same time, there are other risks\nand aspects of tokenisation that must be clarified, and the extent of token usage in the future is\nuncertain.\nThere is currently heightened focus on whether stablecoins may become a means of settlement in, for\nexample, tokenised securities transactions and cross-border payments. This focus could broaden,\nespecially if the volume of transactions relating to tokenised assets becomes substantially larger than\ntoday and central banks do not facilitate settlement in central bank money for these types of payments.\nNorges Bank's has carried technical and functional testing of different technologies and applications for\nCBDC solutions in a blockchain sandbox 1. The banking sector was invited to participate in the testing. To\ndate, one bank has taken part. The source code for the sandbox will shortly be made publicly available as\nopen source, enabling inter alia other central banks to draw on the work undertaken in its development.\nOver time, tokenisation and CBDC may enhance payment system efficiency and security. Realising the\nexpected benefits of a CBDC will require cooperation and coordination with external stakeholders. Norges\nBank has decided to continue its exploration of tokenisation and CBDCs, which will include collaboration\nwith other payment system participants to clarify the opportunities and implications of the new technology.\nThe possibilities for use of the Eurosystem's CBDC solutions and standards will also be explored.\n\n\n\n\n1\n    The testing is documented in a separate report (Norges Bank, 2026b).\n\n\nNorges Bank                                          Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                            1\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 3 ===\nContents\n  1. Introduction\n1.1. Background\nIn accordance with the strategy for the period 2023–2025, Norges Bank was to \"... be ready, if\nappropriate, to issue a central bank digital currency. A CBDC may be necessary to ensure that NOK will\ncontinue to be a safe, efficient and attractive means of payment in the future too.\" (Norges Bank (2022),\np.10).\n\nThe primary task of the exploration in the strategy period was to establish a basis for a decision on\nwhether Norges Bank should issue a CBDC, and if so, in what form and with which characteristics.\n\nNorges Bank has concluded that: \"... the introduction of either a retail or wholesale CBDC is currently not\nwarranted. However, the situation and needs could change. Norges Bank will therefore be ready to\nintroduce a CBDC if it becomes necessary to maintain an efficient and secure payment system\" (Norges\nBank, 2025).\n\n1.2. Norges Bank has evaluated two main forms of CBDC\nRetail CBDC can be viewed as a digital form of cash. Such money would be a universally accessible\nmeans of payment for the public, for payments at points of sale, between individuals, for online\npurchases, and for payments to and from the public sector. The introduction of a retail CBDC would entail\na substantial restructuring of the payment system and could, if widely adopted, challenge the business\nmodels of banks and other private actors as providers of payment services to the public.\n\nIn principle, retail CBDC can rely on traditional technologies, which is the approach of the European\nCentral Bank (ECB) with its digital euro 2. Alternatively, they can be based on blockchain technology,\nwhich enables the transfer of money, securities and other assets between holders without the\ninvolvement of issuers or the entities on which the assets represent a claim.\n\nWholesale CBDC is comparable to traditional central bank reserves but based on blockchain technology.\nA wholesale CBDC would only be available to banks and other financial sector entities with an account at\nthe central bank, as is the case for existing central bank reserves. A wholesale CBDC would primarily\nserve to facilitate the settlement of transactions from tokenised platforms, such as securities trading or\ntokenised bank deposits 3, in central bank money. An alternative to the introduction of wholesale CBDC is\nto adapt the central bank's existing settlement system so that it can settle transactions with tokenised\nassets.\n\nBlockchain technology allows payments to be programmed, for example by making a payment contingent\non the completion of another transaction or the occurrence of a specific event. At the same time, the\ntechnology raises complex issues, for example related to governance and control, which must be\naddressed before it can be deployed for CBDC purposes. 4 Several central banks in major global financial\ncentres, together with securities market participants are already testing alternatives for the settlement of\ntokenised transactions in central bank money.\n\n\n\n\n2\n  For more details on the digital euro, see ECB (2026a).\n3\n  Tokenised bank deposits are digital representations of regular bank deposits, issued on a blockchain, where customer ownership\nis still a claim on the bank in the same manner as a regular deposit.\n4\n  See the ECBs FAQs on the digital euro, answer to question 14: \"The digital euro is not based on distributed ledger technology\n(DLT), but it makes use of key design principles from DLT to enhance resilience and efficiency and to improve the system’s overall\nperformance and reliability.”, ECB (2026a).\n\n\nNorges Bank                                         Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                        3\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 5 ===\n2. Would the introduction of a CBDC promote an efficient and\n   secure payment system in Norway?\nNorges Bank has placed particular emphasis on whether the introduction of a CBDC could\npromote innovation in payment services and whether it would be a suitable instrument to\nstrengthen contingency arrangements in Norway's payment system. Other potential benefits of a\nCBDC have also been assessed, for example whether advantages could arise if a retail CBDC were\ndefined as legal tender. Central banks in other jurisdictions have considered whether other objectives\njustify the issuance of a CBDC. This includes the ECB, which considers the ability to withdraw cash from\nbank accounts as an important foundation for public confidence in bank deposits and singleness of\nmoney (ECB, 2026). ECB further argues that retail CBDC may promote financial inclusion, and that\nintroducing a new payment service that is independent of foreign infrastructure providers could strengthen\nEuropean strategic autonomy.\n\nThis section examines how the introduction of retail and wholesale CBDC, respectively, could support\nNorges Bank's mission to promote an efficient and secure payment system.\n\n2.1. The case for a retail CBDC\nIntroducing a retail CBDC is not an appropriate measure at this stage to promote innovation that\nenhances effective and secure payment services. Reports and speeches from the Bank for\nInternational Settlements (BIS), International Monetary Fund (IMF) and the World Bank, among others,\nexpress expectations that the adoption of blockchain technology in the payment system could provide a\nbasis for new and innovative payment services. 5 Such expected benefits include in particular payment\nand settlement programmability, which could enable further automation of transaction processing. So far,\nblockchain technology has not been applied to any significant extent in traditional finance. However, if the\nexpectations about this technology are fulfilled, this could unlock significant innovation potential.\n\nUnder the current division of responsibility in the payment system, banks and other private entities\ninterface with customers directly. They develop and provide payment services to meet customer needs\nand are responsible for carrying out control measures to combat money laundering and financing of\nterrorism etc. Norges Bank’s roles in the payment system are to carry out interbank settlement, to\nsupervise interbank systems and to issue cash, but without direct contact with the public (apart from the\nredemption of withdrawn banknotes).\n\nNorges Bank does not interface directly with the public in the payment system. Consequently, the new\npayment services, including any that may be based on blockchain technology, should be developed and\noffered by banks and other private providers. The means of payment in such innovative payment services\nneed not be a CBDC. Tokenised bank deposits may serve as a better alternative. The development of\nsuch new payment services is therefore not necessarily dependent on the introduction of a retail CBDC.\nHowever, adjustments to interbank settlement may be necessary to ensure that payments made using\ntokenised bank deposits can be settled in central bank money in Norges Bank’s Real Time Gross\nSettlement (RTGS) system. These issues are addressed further below in the assessment of a possible\nintroduction of a wholesale CBDC.\n\nPayment solutions using retail CBDCs could enhance the payment system's contingency\narrangements, but contingency considerations do not at present provide a decisive argument for\nthe introduction of a retail CBDC. A new payment system based on a retail CBDC would be partly\nindependent of existing payment solutions. A new and independent payment infrastructure would\n\n5\n    See eg BIS (2025a), BIS & CPMI (2024) and BIS (2023).\n\n\nNorges Bank                                        Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                4\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 6 ===\nstrengthen contingency preparedness thereby enabling CBDC payments if, for example, payment card\nnetworks were disrupted. For a retail CBDC to work well in a contingency situation, it would need to be in\nregular everyday use and accepted at relevant points of sale. Furthermore, establishing a new payment\ninfrastructure as CBDCs would be complex and costly.\n\nIntroducing a retail CBDC primarily to strengthen contingency arrangements therefore appears to be a\nrelatively costly and uncertain option compared with the cost of enhancing existing electronic contingency\nsolutions. A working group appointed by the Ministry of Finance has highlighted opportunities to improve\npayment resilience by building on current contingency arrangements (see Ministry of Finance (2025)).\nThese proposed measures are likely less costly than introducing a CBDC. Strengthening payment\ncontingency is therefore not a decisive argument for introducing a retail CBDC in Norway now, not least\ngiven the costs, complexity and uncertainties associated with a CBDC introduction.\n\nCBDC as legal tender is not a decisive argument for introduction at this stage. 6 According to\nSection 3-5 (1) of the Central Bank Act, banknotes and coins issued by Norges Bank are legal tender in\nNorway. There have been compelling reasons in favour of legal tender. One important reason has been\nthe provision ensuring the possibility of discharging a payment obligation in a legally certain way if parties\nhave not agreed on the means of payment and later disagree on the means of settlement. The provision\ncan therefore promote efficiency by helping to prevent conflicts regarding the form of payment. This effect\nis reduced if the use of and access to cash weakens further. Furthermore, the provision has been eroded\nbecause, in many cases, specific rules have been established for how different types of payments must\nbe carried out without the use of cash. According to the preparatory works to the current Central Bank\nAct, financial inclusion has also been one of the purposes of the legal tender provision.\n\nThe current provisions on legal tender were formulated based on the fact that banknotes and coins are\nused as a means of payment, but cash plays a smaller role now. If cash usage were to decline further or\ncease altogether, it may become necessary to reassess the need for legal tender and which means of\npayment should be granted such status.\n\nAlthough retail CBDCs could be designed to incorporate some of the characteristics required for\nfunctioning as legal tender, other characteristics do not appear to be satisfied, for example that such a\nmeans of payment must be readily available to all. A retail CBDC would therefore likely not replicate all\nthe characteristics that explain why cash is legal tender. It may therefore then seem natural to assume\nthat a CBDC is not well-suited for fulfilling this purpose, at least given the current situation.\n\nFor the time being, the need for legal tender is best met by strengthening access to and the option to use\ncash, in the same way as legislation has also been adjusted in recent years through provisions in the\nFinancial Contracts Act and the Financial Institutions Act.\n\nNorges Bank has not concluded on whether a retail CBDC should become legal tender. This is an\nassessment that must be made by for example a dedicated legislative committee.\n\nIntroducing a retail CBDC is not a suitable measure for financial inclusion in Norway. The most\ncommon reasons for financial exclusion in Norway include limited digital literacy and identification\nproblems, which make it difficult to open a bank account and therefore gain access to payment services.\nThis is noted, for example, in the report from the Payment Commission. 7\n\n\n\n\n6\n  The need for legal tender was also analysed in the second phase of the CBDC project. See Section 2.5 in the report from this\nphase: Norges Bank (2019).\n7\n  Official Norwegian Reports (NOU 2024:21) (In Norwegian only).\n\n\nNorges Bank                                         Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                    5\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 7 ===\nPotential CBDC-based payment solutions would probably have user interfaces that are broadly similar to\ncurrent digital payment services. The introduction of a retail CBDC is therefore not a particularly suitable\nmeasure for promoting financial inclusion for those that have difficulties using current payment services.\nThis would likely be achieved more easily and at lower cost by setting design requirements for existing\nservices.\n\nLack of access to banking and payment services is a relatively limited problem in Norway and is unlikely\nto be resolved by defining a potential CBDC as legal tender. Other instruments, including those proposed\nby the Payment Commission, will probably be more appropriate.\n\nPublic confidence in bank deposits does not depend on the existence of a retail CBDC. Some\ncentral banks argue that the ability to withdraw cash from bank deposit accounts is important for public\nconfidence in bank deposits and support singleness of money with a one-to-one conversion rate between\ndifferent types of money denominated in national currency. This would imply that an electronic alternative\nfrom the central bank should be available if cash becomes marginalised or disappears entirely. In Norges\nBank's assessment, the deposit guarantee scheme, public regulation and supervision of banks, and\ninterbank settlement in central bank money are fundamental to confidence in bank deposits and the\nsingleness of money. Ensuring confidence in bank deposits is therefore not a compelling argument for\nintroducing a retail CBDC.\n\nIntroducing a retail CBDC could become relevant if necessary national control over the payment\nsystem is at risk, but this is not currently the case. Societal considerations require that a country’s\npayment system must be subject to sufficient national governance and control. The Eurosystem has\nemphasised a similar argument for the introduction of a digital euro.\n\nIf global entities achieve too dominant positions in the domestic payment system, and they are not subject\nto sufficient regulation by Norwegian authorities, there is a risk that societal considerations will not be\nsafeguarded. Such entities include international card companies and big techs. Big Techs are big\ncompanies primarily engaged in digital services such as social media, search engines, ecommerce and\nIT/telecommunications equipment. Their business models centre on the collection, analysis and\ncommercial use of user data. If these companies were to introduce new private forms of money or\npayment services that fail to safeguard key societal considerations, the introduction of a retail CBDC\ncould become an appropriate corrective measure.\n\nHowever, a central bank may face substantial challenges in seeking to launch a payment alternative\nwhich is more attractive than alternatives offered by large international actors with extensive experience\nof payments.\n\nThe market for CBDC systems is under-developed, and introducing a CBDC now would be\ndisproportionately costly and risky. Were Norges Bank to introduce a CBDC solution now, it would\nlargely have to rely on procuring an IT system developed by a commercial provider. During 2022 and\n2023, another central bank conducted a comprehensive market survey of commercial suppliers of such\nsystems. The survey found that the market was still immature, with no available system that could be\ndeployed without comprehensive customisation. Such customisation could be costly and would normally\nentail undesirable risk, partly because the result could be non-standardised solutions. The market for\nCBDC infrastructures has still not developed substantially further.\n\nCollaboration with other central banks on retail CBDC infrastructure may be relevant, but so far no\nrelevant alternatives are in operation. No central banks in countries comparable to Norway have\nintroduced a CBDC. Therefore, there are, as yet, no other central banks with which Norges Bank could\nshare an operating solution. If the ECB / Eurosystem decides to introduce a digital euro that facilitates\n\n\nNorges Bank                                Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                           6\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 8 ===\nmulti-currency handling, making use of their infrastructure may be relevant for a Norwegian retail CBDC.\nIt may also be relevant if the need for retail CBDC in Norway increases. Such collaboration would likely\nbe dependent on Norwegian participation in T2 8. Whether Norway will participate in T2 has yet to be\ndecided.\n\nA CBDC solution introduced in Norway now, based on, for example, external IT development, would\nentail high costs and would risk that the system does not satisfy relevant common standards that are\nintroduced at a later stage. Regardless of the assessment of whether a retail CBDC should be introduced,\nthe situation in respect of providers and standardisation suggests that it would be sensible to postpone\nany introduction until the market is more mature and appropriate standards are in place. If Norges Bank\nwere to introduce a CBDC before other comparable central banks, it would entail considerable risk that\nthe introduced system would not be compatible with what later becomes the market standard for such\nsystems. It is better to be a fast follower than a first mover.\n\n2.2. Wholesale CBDC – assessment of needs, characteristics and\nissues related to future development\nWholesale CBDC would be like traditional central bank reserves but represented on a blockchain.\nWholesale CBDC could perform the same core functions as traditional reserves, while its tokenised\nrepresentation in a blockchain would enable new functionalities. The most important function of central\nbank reserves in the payment system is to serve as a secure means of interbank settlement in payments.\nIf central bank reserves are represented as tokens in a blockchain, this allows for reserves or other\nassets (such as tokenised ownership rights to securities) to be moved between blockchains such that a\ntransfer of ownership to money or other assets is made on the same blockchain in an integrated, instant\nand reciprocally dependant process called atomic settlement. Counterparty risk can thus be eliminated\nand it may be possible to reduce the number of participants involved in e.g. securities trading and\nsettlement.\n\nIn securities settlement in Norway, settlement risk is managed at the interbank level. A reduction of\nsettlement risk is therefore not a decisive argument for introducing a wholesale CBDC. However,\ntokenisation may enable the streamlining of transaction chains, allowing the number of participants and\nfunctions involved in e.g. securities trades to be organised more efficiently. Such use of blockchain\ntechnology can provide gains in the form of automation and lower costs.\n\nWholesale CBDC can ensure risk-free settlement for tokenised transactions. Without access to\nsettlement in central bank reserves, financial infrastructures may be introduced that use e.g. stablecoins\nas a means of settlement. This would carry higher risk for participants and for financial stability compared\nwith settlement in central bank money. This use of wholesale CBDC now seems like the most relevant\nargument for introducing a CBDC in Norway at a later date. An alternative to wholesale CBDC is to adapt\nthe existing settlement system so that it can settle transactions from tokenised platforms. A number of\ncentral banks are currently exploring and testing such adaptations.\n\nIn Norway, introduction of tokenisation has so far been fairly limited but could accelerate quickly\nonce it gains momentum. There are currently no known specific plans in Norway for the tokenisation of\nsecurities or other assets where settlement should be in central bank money. Internationally, progress\nhas been more pronounced, particularly in financial centres such as London, Singapore, New York and\nSwitzerland, where different forms of such tokenised solutions are being tested. However, the\ndevelopment of tokenisation in the Norwegian financial sector may make rapid progress once it is started.\nThis may require that Norges Bank, at a later stage, facilitates central bank settlement of tokenised\n\n8\n    T2 is the ECB/Eurosystem's settlement system for payments in euro, see ECB (2026b).\n\n\nNorges Bank                                         Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                              7\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 9 ===\ntransactions at relatively short notice.
