Norges Bank will therefore continue efforts in this area and should\nbe ready to introduce an appropriate form of settlement in central bank money of transactions from\ntokenised platforms should it later become necessary to ensure an efficient and secure payment system\nand financial stability.\n\nThe advantages of tokenisation can be realised by registering multiple assets on the same\nblockchain, but this also entails complexity in terms of management and control. The experimental\ntesting that has been performed as a part of the exploration has shown that many of the benefits\nassociated with blockchain technology can only be achieved if all assets in a settlement are registered in\nthe same blockchain. At the same time, such a structure raises complex and unresolved issues related to\nresponsibility, management and control. This is a topic being discussed internationally, with no viable\nsolutions so far. Should Norges Bank conclude that a form of wholesale CBDC is needed, management\nand control issues must be addressed first. These issues are discussed in more detail in Section 3 with a\nsummary from the experimental testing.\n\nShould wholesale CBDC be introduced in Norway, it must not undermine the possibilities for\nliquidity management or the conduct of monetary policy. Banks would gain access to wholesale\nCBDC by converting from their holdings of traditional reserves. Should it become possible to make such\nconversions rapidly and efficiently in both directions, and both Norges Bank and commercial banks\nreceive access to a comprehensive updated overview of the holdings of traditional reserves and\nwholesale CBDC, liquidity management could also be conducted after the introduction of tokenised\nreserves. There are strong arguments why banks' holdings of wholesale CBDC for settlement should be\nincluded in banks' deposit quotas and that they should bear the same interest rate as traditional reserves.\nThis is discussed in more detail in Section 6 on the effects on liquidity management of introducing a\nwholesale CBDC.\n\nShould it become relevant to introduce wholesale CBDC or another form of settlement of tokenised\ntransactions in central bank money in Norway, Norges Bank should consider collaborating on\ninfrastructure with other central banks. Among other things, conditions related to costs, expertise and\nrisk indicate that such opportunities should be investigated. In that case, the use of the Eurosystem's\nsolutions may be particularly relevant, but that likely requires an agreement between Norges Bank and\nthe Eurosystem to use T2 as the settlement system for NOK.\n\n2.3. Overall assessment of the need for a Norwegian CBDC in the\nnear future\nSo far, there are no sufficient arguments that Norges Bank should introduce either a retail or a\nwholesale CBDC in the near future. However, the use of blockchain technology may give long-term\nbenefits in the payment and financial systems. In that case, the division of labour and responsibility in the\npayment system indicates that it is banks and other payment system service providers that develop and\noffer retail payment services based on this technology.\n\nThe introduction of a retail CBDC may be relevant should new payment services or new types of\nmoney based on this technology be launched, such that societal needs are not met. It may also\nbecome relevant should global operators be expected to take a dominant position in the Norwegian\npayment system and sufficient national governance and control of the payment system is not possible.\nOther instruments, such as influence through dialogue, speeches, reports and other publications, along\nwith regulation and other measures by political authorities, may, however, be more relevant, preferably to\nprevent developments from moving too far in an undesirable direction.\n\n\n\n\nNorges Bank                                Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                            8\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 10 ===\nThere is currently no basis to introduce a wholesale CBDC. Tokenised central bank reserves may play a\nkey role in securing interbank settlement of tokenised transactions. But the introduction of tokenisation in\nthe Norwegian financial sector has not made much progress. Should tokenisation develop rapidly, it\nwould be relevant for Norges Bank to introduce a wholesale CBDC or another form of interbank\nsettlement in central bank money of tokenised transactions. It may be relevant to ensure that\ndevelopments evolve in the desired direction and to prevent infrastructures being established where the\nmeans of settlement, is less stable money such as stablecoins. Systemically important settlement should\nbe made with central bank money as a means of settlement. However, it is considered neither necessary\nnor appropriate for Norges Bank to introduce such solutions for the time being. The ECBs planned\nsolutions in this area may be appropriate at a later date should Norges Bank enter into an agreement with\nthe ECB to participate in T2.\n\nThe assessment of the need to introduce a CBDC is made based on the current state of the\nNorwegian payment system. If the payment system situation had warranted the introduction of a CBDC,\nit would have still been difficult for a relatively small central bank to be among the first to introduce a\nCBDC. There are good reasons why Norges Bank should rather be a fast follower should the need arise,\nappropriate solutions are available and standards are established.\n\nTo introduce a CBDC now would require considerable resources. At the same time, not introducing a\nCBDC entails a certain risk that Norway is left behind should needs rapidly change. This risk is, however,\ngiven the current situation considered to be relatively limited and does not warrant the introduction of a\nCBDC as a precautionary measure at this stage.\n\nExploration will continue into the benefits and opportunities of tokenisation and the consequences of\nCBDCs and other new forms of money. Collaboration with other stakeholders in the Norwegian financial\nsector will be important.\n\n\n3. Summary from experimental testing 9\n3.1. General information on the test setup\nThe overall aim of the technical testing has been to gain insight into the characteristics of the blockchain\ntechnology relevant to the assessment of whether to introduce CBDC into the current Norwegian payment\nsystem. Specifically, this has consisted of running test cases to identify gains, issues and drawbacks of\nusing blockchain technology in various payment system processes in which central bank money plays a\nrole.\n\nTested payments and transactions are similar, but not identical to, payment transactions in the current\npayment system. At the same time, the testing has been of an exploratory nature and has not aimed to\ncopy all details in existing transaction chains.\n\nNorges Bank has also not tested how the technology's capacity to process large transaction volumes over\na short period of time or how transactions in a ledger react to unforeseen or abnormal events.\n\nEarly in this exploration phase, Norges Bank entered into framework agreements with two external IT\ncompanies for the development of technology sandboxes 10 to house experimental testing. Both the\nsandbox development and necessary further development to execute the specific test cases were\n\n\n\n9\n    The testing setup and findings from the testing are described in greater detail in a separate report (Norges Bank, 2026b).\n10\n     Sandbox: An isolated test environment to enable technology experiments without impacting production systems.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                            Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                 9\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 11 ===\nhandled as call-offs under the framework agreements. A total of six test cases were executed during this\nexploration phase. One bank participated in parts of the testing.\n\nA test system with two sandboxes enables the testing of transaction characteristics in which both\nretail and wholesale CBDCs are included. The test system consists of two sandboxes based on\ndifferent blockchain or distributed ledger technologies (DLT 11). CBDC transactions and holdings will be\nrecorded on these ledgers. The establishment of the two ledgers are described under Test Case 1 and\nTest Case 2 below.\n\nThe aim was to use one ledger for testing retail CBDC characteristics and the other for testing wholesale\nCBDC characteristics. Together, the sandboxes could therefore be used to test payment transactions\ninvolving both retail and wholesale CBDCs. The two sandboxes can also be used to test financial\ntransactions, for example securities trading with settlement in tokenised central bank reserves.\n\nTransfer of tokens between the sandboxes was enabled by a bridge 12 which facilitates communication\nbetween them. The bridge can be used to transfer tokens (such as CBDC, securities or tokenised bank\ndeposits) between the two ledgers. This is described in more detail under Test Case 3 below. This\nmethod is used to test tokenised bank deposits on both multi-ledger 13 and single-ledger 14 architecture,\nsee description of Test Case 4.\n\nWe have also tested how CBDC can be used as a means of settlement in tokenised securities trading,\nand how smart contracts 15 and blockchain technology can automate parts of government debt\nmanagement, in particular in connection with the issuance of such securities, see Test Cases 5 and 6\nbelow.\n\n3.2. The six test cases\nTest Case 1: Characteristics of the Hyperledger Besu technology in retail\nCBDC projects.\nRetail CBDC based on the Hyperledger Besu technology can provide the central bank with\nsufficient control over the central bank money in the ledger without gaining access to personal\ndata. The purpose of this test case was to explore the characteristics of retail CBDC with robust access\ncontrol and governance in a two-tier distribution model, while providing the central bank with sufficient\ncontrol but without access to personal data.\n\nFor a central bank that issues CBDC a decisive criterion is that no unauthorised parties can issue CBDC,\nwhich would constitute digital counterfeiting. At the same time, partly for personal data protection\nconsiderations, it may be important that the central bank cannot access data related to individual\ncustomers' payments or CBDC holdings. Should the central bank have access to such data, it could also\ntrigger Anti-Money Laundering (AML) and Countering the Financing of Terrorism (CFT)monitoring duties.\n\n\n\n11\n   Ledger/DLT: A digital ledger where all transactions are stored as a shared, verifiable log, distributed across multiple nodes,\nproviding all authorised participants with the same up-to-date version without depending on one central authority.\n12\n   Bridge: A system for transferring messages and/or assets between two or more DLT networks. The bridge verifies an event on the\nsource chain (for example that assets are locked) and reflects this in the target chain (for example by issuing a representation of the\nassets). Bridges are similar to SWIFT as a messaging layer.\n13\n   Multi-ledger system: Several individual/independent DLTs that are connected to each other. Unlike one shared ledger for\neverything, a multi-ledger system allocates responsibilities and states across domains (jurisdictions, currencies, market participant\ngroups etc.).\n14\n   Single ledger-based architecture has one shared DLT.\n15\n   These are digital algorithms that link one or more conditions in order to automate transactions. Delivery versus payment (DvP)\nand atomicity mentioned below are examples of applications.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                          Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                      10\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 12 ===\nThis would also be contrary to the principle that banks should have direct customer contact in a CBDC\nsolution, as is the case in the current payment system.\n\nThe testing included transaction flow that is practically end-to-end ie from the establishment of a customer\nrelationship to the execution of a payment to another retail customer. The solution was hosted on a\ntechnological platform where the roles of the central bank, banks and end-customers are handled in\nsmart contracts. Several log-in mechanisms (including digital keys and traditional authentication) were\ntested.\n\nThe solution is designed so that customer information remains with banks, while the ledger only contains\nwhat was necessary to execute transfers of retail CBDC. However, personal data protection cannot be\nresolved by technical measures alone but must be assessed together with regulations and governance\nmodels.\n\nIn the solution, personal data are protected through pseudonymity 16, whereby end-users are represented\nby anonymised addresses in the ledger without a direct link to their identity. This reduces personal data\nexposure during normal operations, but there is still a risk.\n\nIf the ledger address of an end-user can be linked to an identity – either through banks, court orders,\ntechnical leaks or data collocation – any participant with read access to the ledger would be able to view\nthe entire transaction history related to this address. This applies to both historical and future transactions\nand applies regardless of whether access is through the central bank, participating banks or others with\nread access to the ledger. Pseudonymity alone cannot prevent full retrospective access once the identity\nlink has been established.\n\nTest Case 2: Characteristics of the Hyperledger Fabric technology in\nwholesale CBDC projects\nThe Hyperledger Fabric technology provides for both an account-based and a token-based\nsolution for wholesale CBDC. The purpose of this test case was to establish a technical sandbox\nprimarily for the handling of wholesale CBDC but based on a different technology than the one in the Test\nCase 1 sandbox.\n\nThe test case was intended to identify characteristics when wholesale CBDC is used as a means of\ninterbank settlement, including functions such as issuance, distribution, and redemption of wholesale\nCBDC, as well as various types of messages between banks in connection with interbank transactions.\nThe test also aimed to look into whether the technology was suitable for meeting the needs for\ngovernance limits and operational traceability. The ledger was established using the Hyperledger Fabric\ntechnology, which is designed to limit access to information between participants and provides the\nopportunity to test account-based 17 and UTXO-based 18 wholesale CBDC.\n\nThe test also showed that this technology enables access control for both transactions and holdings, and\nthat the central bank can have sufficient control in the ledger without breaching interbank confidentiality\nrequirements. One important finding from the testing is that operational complexity increases with the\nnumber of participants and channels with access to the ledger.\n\n\n16\n   Pseudonymity means that the identity is not explicitly visible in the system but can be derived if identifiers are linked to external\ninformation. Once such a link has been established, pseudonymity provides no technical access limitation.\n17\n   An account-based model is a method for recording assets on a blockchain where each address has a running balance that is\nupdated for each transaction, similar to a bank account.\n18\n   UTXO (Unspent Transaction Output) is a model for representing ownership in individual blockchains, where each transaction\n\"spends\" complete former outputs and creates new ones, and where a user's balance is the sum of the UTXOs that the user can\nspend.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                            Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                           11\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 13 ===\nTest Case 3: Establishing a bridge for token transfer between two ledgers\nIf bridges are used for CBDC transfers across ledgers, some of the characteristics of blockchain\ntechnology will not be fully exploited. The purpose of this test case was to explore whether the\ncharacteristics of a CBDC is weakened if the means of payment is transferred from the ledger controlled\nby the issuer to external ledgers across a bridge. This is important, partly to ensure that no-one but the\ncentral bank can issue CBDC in a scenario where tokenised asset issuers use separate ledgers. Such\nbridges connect different ledgers, enabling, for example, wholesale CBDC to be used for payment\nsettlement using tokenised bank deposits on a ledger that is not under the central bank's direct control.\nShould CBDC be \"transferred\" to other ledgers using such bridges, it will be important to ensure that\ncentral bank control over the issuance and deletion of CBDC is not weakened. Furthermore, one of the\nobjectives of the testing was to identify opportunities provided by such an arrangement for ensuring\natomicity – ie payment against delivery with immediate effect.\n\nThe test showed that Delivery versus Payment (DvP) 19 is also possible in a bridge solution. The transfer\nof asset ownership (for example a security) and the payment can be linked logically but do not take place\nsimultaneously on the two ledgers (ie not atomically). As transfers are simultaneous, this increases the\nrisk of unforeseen events when there are errors or outages in the ledgers or in the bridges connecting\nthem.\n\nOn the other hand, a bridge – given that security is ensured – can make CBDC available on external\nledgers where other participants can use it for settlement in innovative payment services based on the\nnew technology. The bridge structure does, however, create a larger attack surface for malicious actors\nand it increases security management requirements, including key management, access control and\ncontinuous monitoring.\n\nTest Case 4: Characteristics of settlement of payments using tokenised\nbank deposits on multiple ledgers\nCross-ledger transactions weaken some of the key characteristics of blockchain technology The\npurpose of this test case was to establish and test the characteristics of payments using tokenised bank\ndeposits between customers with deposits in different banks and using wholesale CBDC as a means of\nsettlement between the participating banks. The test aimed to explore advantages and disadvantages of\na structure where participants operate on multiple ledgers, including how the need for governance and\ncontrol over the issuance of the different assets could be safeguarded. The test supplemented the\nfindings from Test Case 3 described above, but in this case, the means of payment was tokenised bank\ndeposits and not retail CBDCs.\n\nThe test showed that parity (equal value) between tokenised bank deposits and CBDCs can be\nmaintained throughout the transaction process and that real-time exchange is feasible. At the same time,\nthe limitations of a multi-ledger architecture were clearly apparent. Smart contracts on one ledger cannot\ndirectly control operations on a different ledger, and bridges or other mechanisms can ensure asset\ntransfer between ledgers.\n\nThe test showed that not all settlements in such multi-ledger structures take place immediately, and\natomicity is therefore not achieved. This means that discrepancies can arise in the settlement process,\nwhich increases the need for standardised messaging formats, clear lines of responsibility and error-\nhandling procedures. In such a setup, CBDC has limited added value beyond what the existing settlement\n\n\n19\n  Delivery versus Payment (DvP) is a settlement principle whereby securities/assets or money change owner and the two legs of\nthe transaction are mutually conditional ie such that counterparty risk is eliminated.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                       Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                     12\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 14 ===\nsystem can offer. However, such a solution could strengthen contingency preparedness by providing an\nalternative to the current payment infrastructure.\n\nTest Case 5: Settlement of tokenised securities\nSimultaneous transfer of ownership of multiple assets on the same ledger ensures simultaneity\nand reduces settlement and counterparty risk but also creates new issues. The purpose of the test\ncase was to simulate trading in and settlement of tokenised securities and bank deposits on one shared\nledger and explore whether such an architecture provides better opportunities to realise gains that were\nnot achieved in the multi-ledger structure tests.\n\nIn this solution, the central bank, banks, brokers, the central securities depository and the end-investor all\noperate on the same ledger. In this ledger, CBDC, tokenised bank deposits and securities are\nrepresented as tokens. The entire value chain, from order to settlement, is executed as one smart\ncontract-controlled transaction. This provides atomicity, with multiple changes taking place together in\none operation – in other words, either all elements of the transaction are executed or none. This also\nprovides lower counterparty risk, real-time settlement and a high degree of traceability.\n\nPersonal data protection is ensured by the central bank having sole access to aggregated data, while\ndetailed customer data remains protected by role-based access control. The opportunity to simulate\nnetting effects in a gross system was demonstrated, with a potential for lower liquidity needs.\n\nWith one shared ledger, bridges or other forms of cross-ledger token transfer are not necessary (unlike in\nthe multi-ledger structure), reducing technical and operational complexity. On the other hand, the single-\nledger structure raises issues related to governance, control and responsibility. This is described in more\ndetail in the summary below.\n\nTest Case 6: Government bond issuance using blockchain technology\nTokenisation can make a number of functions in the life cycle of a government bond more\nefficient, but the inherent transparency of the technology makes it difficult to satisfy\nconfidentiality requirements. The purpose of this test was to determine whether the entire government\nbond life cycle can be tokenised and recorded on a shared ledger and be executed using smart contracts.\nThe entire transaction chain from issuance via an auction, trading in the secondary market, coupon\npayments, buybacks and redemptions at maturity was handled through smart contracts. Government\nbonds, tokenised bank deposits and wholesale CBDC were represented as separate tokens. Settlement\nwas to be atomic – so that either the settlement of both money and securities would be simultaneous or\nno asset transfers would be made. This mechanism eliminates counterparty risk and provides real-time\nsettlement and a high degree of traceability for the issuer and market participants. Although the auction\nprocess for government bonds may deviate from the issuance of corporate bonds, the findings from the\ntest are likely transferable to other such issuances.\n\nThe test also identified a basic conflict between the use of blockchain technology and confidentiality\nrequirements. A blockchain is generally transparent: If a bid is made in plain language on the ledger,\neveryone with ledger access can see both bid prices and volumes. To conduct sealed-bid auctions, bids\nwould have to be encrypted, and the actual opening, ranking and allotment conducted outside the ledger\nbefore the result is recorded on the ledger with signed proofs of intent. In the same manner, identification\nof actual bond holders in connection with, for example, coupon payments and redemptions must be\nperformed outside the ledger, both to safeguard confidentiality and technical feasibility. Even with\nencryption and role-based access control, activity patterns and metadata (who participates, when, how\nmuch activity) will still be visible on the ledger.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                        13\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 15 ===\nIt is again proved that a comprehensive solution requires a deliberate division of labour between\nprocesses that take place on the ledger (on-chain) and processes that take place off the ledger (off-\nchain). The ledger is well suited for processes requiring strong guarantees for integrity, simultaneity and\naccountability, such as registration of ownership, settlement (DvP) and enforcement of roles and rights.\nMore complex, calculation-intensive or confidential processes, such as auction awards and owner\nidentification, can be handled more practically off-chain, but with connections into the ledger.\n\nThe test showed that tokenised government bonds on a single-ledger platform provide issuers and\nmarket participants with substantial efficiency, risk reduction and transparency gains, but also revealed\nthat the inherent transparency of blockchain technology limits which functions can be performed on-chain\nwhen there are mandatory confidentiality requirements.\n\n3.3. Summary of insights from the tests\nCBDC on a single-ledger structure provides greater gains than CBDC on a multi-ledger structure\nbut then has governance and control issues. If a shared ledger is used for the execution and\nsettlement of transactions, there are clear advantages compared with coordinated settlement across\nmultiple ledgers. At the same time, there are issues related to roles and responsibilities for the shared\nledger. Overall, the lesson from the tests indicates that a single ledger CBDC structure, tokenised bank\ndeposits and tokenised securities are the best solution for realising the desired gains. These gains\ninclude simultaneous and final settlement of payments, reduced counterparty risk, fewer sources of error\nand easier compliance and traceability.\n\nBridges between ledgers increase complexity related to governance and control, which expands\nthe attack surface for fraud actors and others with malicious intent. Using bridges to connect\ntransactions across multiple ledgers in a multi-ledger structure requires mechanisms that increase\ntechnical and operational complexity and expands the attack surface and full atomicity is then not\nachieved. 20\n\nUsing bridges raises particular governance issues for solutions requiring cross-system interaction. A\nbridge must be configured for privileged access to both environments and thereby concentrates risk and\nresponsibility. This requires strict key and access management (including two-person control and\nseparation of duties), detailed logging and real-time monitoring, effective error handling and rollback\nprocedures and precise rules to define when final settlement is made. Standardised protocols and audit\ntrails are necessary to reduce system and governance risk. However, even these measures will not\nprovide settlement via a bridge with full atomicity. The use of bridges should therefore be limited to\nsituations where interoperability and simultaneity of settlement cannot be otherwise achieved.\n\nA single-ledger solution entails issues related to control, governance and allocation of\nresponsibility on the ledger. A single ledger requires clear roles and a structure that ensures that all\nparticipants with responsibility for assets on the ledger can perform their roles and maintain sufficient\ncontrol of \"their assets on the ledger. For example, the central bank must be in full control of issuance and\ndestruction of CBDCs, associated smart contracts and the main principles of secure cash settlement. In\nturn, banks, brokers and the central securities depository must be able to manage their own tokens and\nservices vis-a-vis customers and the market. On the other hand, the operation of a shared ledger and\nbasic infrastructure may be a candidate for a public-private partnership in the form of an infrastructure\n\n\n20\n   Atomicity means that all relevant state changes in a settlement, for example transfers of money and securities, are carried out as\none indivisible and simultaneous operation, typically on one ledger or within the same system. The transaction will have just one\nfinal state: completed or not completed. There are no interim states where only parts of the settlement/transfer of ownership has\nbeen completed.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                          Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                      14\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 16 ===\ncompany with shared change management, version control for smart contracts and clear decision-making\nprocesses.\n\nBlockchain technology is based on transparency, while payment system data must be subject to\naccess limitations. Personal data protection must be ensured by technical measures, as ledger data are\ngenerally accessible to all nodes in the system. Personal data or other sensitive information related to\nholdings or payments should therefore be kept off-chain or protected through appropriate encryption, at\nthe same time as access control is governed through roles on the ledger. Such information protection\nincreases complexity.\n\n4. Relevant design features of a future Norwegian CBDC\n   solution\nThe purpose of introducing such a currency influences the characteristics of a CBDC solution. As Norges\nBank has concluded that introducing a CBDC is currently not warranted, there is no basis for describing\nthe design of a possible future CBDC solution. The list of design features below must therefore be\nconsidered a rough draft that will have to be made more detailed should issuing a CBDC become\nrelevant.\n\n4.1. Relevant design features in a retail CBDC solution\nA CBDC will likely be distributed through a two-tier model. 21 It is natural to assume that the allocation\nof responsibilities between banks and Norges Bank will remain similar to the current situation. If so,\nCBDC distribution to end-customers will resemble the arrangement in place for cash. A bank wishing to\nincrease its CBDC holdings for further distribution to its customers will receive a transfer of CBDC to its\nwallet against a corresponding debit from the bank's central bank reserves. CBDCs are then transferred\nto end-customers' wallets from the bank's holdings against a corresponding debit from their bank\naccounts. 22\n\nNorges Bank should not have information about individuals' CBDC holdings or payments. Based\non the current corresponding division of roles between banks and central banks, in a CBDC solution,\nbanks will also have the direct customer contact, while Norges Bank will neither receive nor need detailed\ninformation about the public's holdings of means of payment or which payments they make. Likewise, it\nshould not be possible for the central bank to monitor or control the CBDC holdings of individual account\nholders or CBDC payments.\n\nKnow Your Customer (KYC), Countering the Financing of Terrorism (CTF) and Anti Money\nLaundering (AML) requirements for CBDC would be the same as for other payment services. KYC\nand AML checks of CBDC users would have to be performed by banks or other market participants that\noffer wallets for CBDC storage and transactions to their customers. Bank customers would not have a\ncustomer relationship with Norges Bank, and Norges Bank would therefore not be in a position to, or have\na duty to perform, customer compliance checks.\n\nAcquiring CBDC must be straightforward. It must be straightforward for the public to acquire retail\nCBDC through online and mobile banking services and ATMs by debiting their own bank accounts.\n\n\n21\n   See also Section 4.2 in the final report for Phase 2: Norges Bank (2019).\n22\n   CBDC holdings and transfers are different from cash holdings and cash payments, as the ledger where each individual owner's\nCBDCs are recorded is held by the central bank, while a wallet, in simple terms, contains a code or key that provides access to the\nwallet owner's CBDC holdings – unlike cash, which actually exists in a physical wallet. The two-tier model means that the central\nbank does not have direct customer contact, information about the identity of individual CBDC account holders or conducts CBDC\ntransactions in the ledger.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                         Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                     15\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 17 ===\nCBDCs will be claims on Norges Bank. 23 Retail CBDC can be described as a form of digital cash and\nmust like other central bank money be a claim on Norges Bank.\n\nRetail CBDC should not be interest-bearing. In previous phases of internationally published CBDC\nexploration, it was argued that CBDC should be interest-bearing, and that changes in CBDC interest rates\ncould be used to influence households' CBDC holdings. 24 For example, the interest rate could be reduced\nif the public chose to make larger-than-desired withdrawals from their bank accounts to acquire retail\nCBDC, which in turn could create problems for banks' funding and in extreme cases pose a threat to\nfinancial stability.\n\nThere now appears to be a relatively broad consensus in the ECB, central banks which have issued\nCBDC and other central banks considering whether to issue CBDC that retail CBDC should not be\ninterest-bearing. 25 This is partly a result of retail CBDC being intended for transaction purposes, not\ninvestment purposes. Retail CBDC would then also be comparable to cash, which is not interest-bearing.\n\nThe ledger for CBDC holdings and transactions should probably be based on blockchain\ntechnology. A presumed purpose of introducing CBDC was that it would boost innovation in the use of\nblockchain technology. This exploration has therefore been based on the assumption that a Norwegian\nCBDC solution will be based on blockchain technology, which enables the use of smart contracts to\nautomate payments. The use of this technology for CBDC solutions is, however, in an early phase, at\nleast in traditional finance.\n\nHowever, some central banks, including the ECB, have stated that it will become possible in time to use\nblockchain technology and smart contracts for new and efficient payment services, even if the means of\npayment is a CBDC that is not based on blockchain technology. This aspect will probably become clearer\nas the technology develops.\n\nCBDC should not be programmable money, and programmability, if implemented, should be at the\npayment service level. It must be possible to use central bank money for payments in various settings.\nThis is a basic principle and applies regardless of conditions relating to other events and terms. At the\nsame time, there should not be different varieties of the same money as this could contravene the parity\nprinciple (ie a 1:1 ratio between all forms of money denominated in NOK).\n\nOne of the main purposes of programmability in a payment situation is to link the execution of a payment\nto other events. A common example is the link between the transfer of securities ownership and cash\nsettlement in connection with securities trading. This is an additional argument in favour of linking smart\ncontracts to payment services and not to the money in a CBDC solution. However, technical issues\nsuggest that this is not straightforward to achieve in practice.\n\nThere should probably be an upper limit on how much CBDC each individual entity can hold.\nHolding limits reduce the possibility to convert bank deposits to CBDC, which will limit the consequences\nof CBDC for financial stability and banks' deposit funding. The ECB has indicated that it will set a digital\neuro holding limit of EUR 3000 (see eg ECB (2026a)). The Bank of England has indicated a limit of GBP\n10 000–20 000 (see eg Bank of England (2025) or Bank of England and HM Treasury (2023)). The\nintroduction of retail CBDC in Norway should be followed by the introduction of a holding limit. The limit\nshould be low enough to prevent the public from converting bank deposits to CBDC on a scale that would\nsignificantly impact financial stability and banks' deposit funding. However, the limit should not so low so\n\n\n23\n   See also Section 3.1 in the final report for Phase 3; Norges Bank (2020).\n24\n   See eg Section 3.15 in the final report for Phase 3: ibid.\n25\n   Some research reports discuss whether banks' holdings of retail CBDC should bear interest. This could motivate banks to hold a\ncertain volume of retail CBDC for further distribution to their customers.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                        Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                    16\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 18 ===\nthat it prevents the use of CBDC for intended payment purposes. Some central banks have considered\nother measures to limit the withdrawal of bank deposits during market turbulence, eg (negative) interest\non CBDC holdings.\n\nIt is too soon to conclude on where the holding limit should be set, partly because this will depend on the\nintended purpose of a CBDC. However, a calculation of the effect on banks' overall deposit funding if the\nbanks should lose deposits equal to NOK 30 000 26 for all citizens over the age of 16 shows that the\nconsequences could be substantial. Banks would then have to increase their central bank funding\nsignificantly, at worst by more than the liquidity they now have access to through central bank deposits\nand available credit facilities. In order for banks to increase their borrowing from the central bank to\ncompensate for the loss of deposit funding, they will have to substantially increase their holdings of\nsecurities approved as collateral in Norges Bank. The impact on banks' costs and therefore lending rates\nis uncertain, depending partly on which securities Norges Bank would be willing to accept as collateral for\nthe increased borrowing. The calculation has been described in more detail in Section 5.\n\n4.2. Relevant design features for wholesale CBDC\nThe introduction of wholesale CBDC so that settlement of tokenised transactions in a secure means of\nsettlement now seems like the most relevant argument for introducing a CBDC in Norway at a later date.\nWithout access to settlement in central bank reserves, financial infrastructures may be introduced that\nuse eg stablecoins as a means of settlement. This would carry higher risk for participants and for financial\nstability compared with settlement in central bank money. An alternative to wholesale CBDC is to adapt\nthe existing settlement system so that it can settle transactions from tokenised platforms.\n\nThe design of a wholesale CBDC depends on the intended areas of application and the conditions that\nwill be set for this usage. Listed below are first some potentially non-negotiable design features or\ncharacteristics of a Norwegian wholesale CBDC solution. Then follows some design features that will\nhave to be clarified in connection with a decision on whether to introduce a wholesale CBDC.\n\nReserves in the form of wholesale CBDC must bear interest at the same rate as traditional\nreserves. Wholesale CBDC, like the traditional central bank deposits banks have in Norges Bank, will be\na form of central bank reserves. The overnight interest rate on banks' wholesale CBDC holdings must be\nthe same as the interest rate on traditional reserves. Diverging interest rates would disrupt liquidity\nmanagement.\n\nConversion between traditional reserves and wholesale CBDC must be seamless. It must be\nstraightforward for banks to convert traditional reserves into and out of wholesale CBDC. Likewise, both\nbanks and Norges Bank must have a user-friendly and up-to-date overview of individual banks' wholesale\nCBDC holdings. In addition, Norges Bank must be able to continuously monitor the total volume of\nwholesale CBDCs.\n\nA wholesale CBDC solution will be based on blockchain technology. Digital central bank money for\ninterbank settlement already exists in the form of banks' reserves in Norges Bank. The introduction of a\nnew, separate system for interbank settlement, based on reserves using technology similar to the current\nNorges Bank settlement system, would not result in the desired gains. This suggests that if Norges Bank\nintroduces wholesale CBDC as a new function, the solution should be based on blockchain technology.\n\n\n\n\n26\n   The amount of NOK 30 000 is derived from indications from the ECB to the effect that EUR 3000 may become the holding limit for\ndigital euro. Norges Bank has not decided what the holding limit for a Norwegian retail CBDC should be. This limit should be set\nfollowing further clarification of purpose and areas of application.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                        Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                  17\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 19 ===\nA decision must be made whether to record wholesale CBDC on a public or a private ledger. A\npublic ledger will in principle be open to anyone who wants to connect to it through a node – which can be\nconsidered part of the ledger's network. Connecting to the ledger will provide access to the information on\nthe ledger, including transactions and holdings, but the name or other personal data of the CBDC account\nholder will not be shown. The node’s task in the network may differ between different ledgers. On the\nother hand, connecting to a node with a private ledger requires advance approval by the owner of or the\norganisation operating the ledger.\n\nShould Norges Bank issue a CBDC solution on a ledger, the Bank must decide whether this ledger\nshould be public or private. A public ledger may facilitate more innovation than a private ledger. On the\nother hand, a public ledger involves complex security issues that will likely entail unacceptable risk, at\nleast until the technology has been further developed with sufficient security solutions. This suggests that\nthe ledger for a Norwegian CBDC, at least initially, should be private and with better mechanisms so that\nNorges Bank can control access.\n\nThe responsibility of the central bank will likely be limited to cash and the cash leg of settlement.\nThe responsibility of the central bank should be limited to the ownership and management of the CBDC\ntoken, including issuance, redemption, access control and interest rate setting. This enables banks and\nother payment service providers, as well as ownership registration entities (eg securities depositories,\nproperty registers etc) to develop their own tokens, smart contracts and application logic within standards\nand approved frameworks without changing settlement functionality.\n\n5. Calculation of potential effects of a holding limit for retail\n   CBDC\nA holding limit may be necessary to limit adverse effects on banks' deposit funding and financial\nstability. A limit on CBDC holdings may be necessary to prevent CBDC withdrawals exceeding banks'\nability to handle deposit withdrawals without suffering liquidity problems. Regardless of what the limit will\nbe, Norges Bank must be able to supply the banking system with sufficient reserves to avoid liquidity\nproblems due to the introduction of CBDC, also in times of financial turbulence. This may be ensured if\nNorges Bank is prepared to provide necessary F-loans to supply central bank reserves lost by the banks\nwhen customers exchange deposits for CBDCs. Banks' collateral for loans in Norges Bank must also be\nsufficient to take out these loans.\n\nNorges Bank has calculated the potential effect of a holding limit of NOK 30 000 per person on both\nbanks' funding and Norges Bank's balance sheet. This is just under the limit of EUR 3 000 per person,\nwhich the ECB has suggested as a digital euro holding limit.\n\nBanks may be exposed to liquidity risk even with a low holding limit. It is uncertain how a holding\nlimit of NOK 30 000 would affect banks' funding costs and thus lending rates. A simple calculation shows\nthat banks' overall annual funding costs could increase by anything from NOK 1bn to NOK 4bn, assuming\nthat all adults in Norway exchange their bank deposits for CBDCs equivalent to the holding limit, and that\nNorges Bank continues to provide collateralised loans. Exchange on this scale would reduce total\nhousehold bank deposits by 7-8%. This could increase lending rates by an average of 2-6 basis points. In\naddition, it would entail a cost for the banks it they have to replace customer deposits with F-loans from\nthe central bank (Norges Bank uses market operations, called “F-loans” and “F-deposits”, to manage the\nquantity of reserves in the banking system) 27. Should Norges Bank become willing to bear a higher risk\non its balance sheet by providing reserves though the purchase of securities or by expanding the range of\nsecurities accepted as collateral, costs would likely be lower. Such measures may deviate considerably\n\n27\n     For more, see F-loans and F-deposits.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                  Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                      18\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 20 ===\nfrom the current liquidity management scheme and rules on the pledging of collateral and must be\ninvestigated in more detail if this becomes relevant.\n\nThe introduction of a CBDC could also necessitate changes to the banks' funding structure as a stable\nfunding source is then reduced or becomes less stable. Therefore, banks may aim to increase their share\nof long-term wholesale funding to reduce their liquidity risk. This could significantly affect banks' funding\nand lending if demand is high and the funding structure is changed abruptly. Furthermore, this is the main\nargument given by the Bank of England for its holding limit for a digital pound. 28\n\n\n6. The introduction of wholesale CBDC and the importance\n   for Norges Bank's liquidity management and conduct of\n   monetary policy\nThe introduction of a wholesale CBDC may affect Norges Bank's liquidity management. Norges\nBank's liquidity management is based on a quota system where central bank reserves in the banking\nsystem are managed to ensure that the shortest money market rates are kept close to the policy rate. 29\nWith the introduction of a wholesale CBDC, two types of central bank money will exist for settlement. Both\nare liabilities on the central bank's liability side, but with different technological characteristics. The\ntraditional reserves are the means of settlement in the central bank's settlement systems (RTGS - Real\nTime Gross Settlement), while the wholesale CBDC (tokenised reserves) would be the means of\nsettlement for tokenised payments that are registered in a blockchain-based registry.\n\nA wholesale CBDC can assume various forms. However, there is broad consensus among central banks\nabout some fundamental principles:\n\n     •    Full interoperability/transferability between traditional central bank reserves and wholesale\n          CBDCs without limitations or frictions when conversion takes place from one to the other.\n     •    A wholesale CBDC and traditional central bank reserves must have identical interest terms and\n          the same overnight rate for calculating interest. 30\n     •    Wholesale CBDC is not to be an independent instrument in the conduct of monetary policy (this\n          partly follows from the two preceding principles). 31\n\nThese principles are also the basis for some of the design features described in Section 4. Adhering to\nthe principles supports the effective settlement of tokenised transactions in tokenised reserves (wholesale\nCBDC). 32\n\nWholesale CBDC must be designed to not interfere with Norges Bank's ability to manage liquidity.\nIf a wholesale CBDC is introduced, banks' total holdings of traditional reserves and wholesale CBDC will\ncomprise the basis for interest calculations, the central bank's market operations and banks' need to\nredistribute reserves/wholesale CBDCs among themselves overnight.\n\n\n\n28\n   For calculations of the possible effects on banks' funding costs, see Norges Bank (2021).\n29\n   Deposits in excess of the quota are remunerated at the reserve rate, one percentage point lower than the policy rate, while\nnegative account balances are remunerated at the D-loan rate, one percentage point higher than the policy rate (referred to as the\ncentral bank's overnight standing facilities). Banks have an incentive to redistribute central bank reserves among themselves\novernight to avoid having to use the standing facilities at unfavourable interest rates. For further discussion of the liquidity\nmanagement system in Norway, see Liquidity management.\n30\n   Assuming that CBDCs are to have the same interest rate terms as traditional forms of money: Like cash, retail CBDC is to be\nremunerated at a zero interest rate and wholesale CBDC to be the same as traditional means of settlement between banks.\n31\n   There is broad consensus among central banks that retail CBDC is also not a suitable monetary policy instrument.\n32\n   For a more in-depth discussion, see Norges Bank (2023c).\n\n\nNorges Bank                                         Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                    19\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 21 ===\nA wholesale CBDC may pose challenges to Norges Bank's liquidity management, particularly if reserves\nin the banking system are low. With low reserves, changes in banks' wholesale CBDC holdings\nimmediately before end-of-day in the Real Time Gross Settlement (RTGS) systems may generate an\nunintended requirement for banks to redistribute reserves and CBDCs among themselves overnight. A\npossible solution may be to limit tokenised transactions for a short timeframe before end-of-day. If\ntokenised transactions are settled in traditional reserves in the RTGS system (ie a wholesale CBDC\ncannot be introduced), deferred settlement in the central bank may be an alternative. Both solutions have\nsome disadvantages and will, in that case, have to be studied further. The consequences of the\nintroduction of a wholesale CBDC for central banks' liquidity management is discussed in more detail in a\nseparate paper. 33\n\n\n7. Legislative amendments required for the introduction of\n   CBDCs\nIf CBDCs are introduced, they will be a new means of payment that naturally enough is not yet regulated.\nA retail CBDC will affect several aspects of financial legislation, including the Central Bank Act, and\nrequires relatively extensive legislative work. The need for legislative amendments in some areas has\nalready been assessed by Norges Bank and presented in a separate paper. 34 The need for other\nlegislative amendments will depend on as yet unclarified details related to a potential CBDC solution. This\nsection first describes necessary legislative amendments for the introduction of a retail CBDC, before\noutlining the amendments for a wholesale CBDC.\n\n7.1. Legislative amendments needed for the introduction of a retail\nCBDC 35\nFirst, the introduction of a retail CBDC will require several amendments to the Central Bank Act including\nto Section 3-4 on issuance of banknotes and coins. This provision can also include statutory authority to\nregulate CBDC interest rates. If CBDCs are to be given legal tender status, this must be clarified in\nSection 3-5 on legal tender. Norges Bank's task to issue CBDCs should also be included in Section 1-3\non central banking activities. With respect to the need for legal tender status, this is further discussed in\nSection 2.1.\n\nSecond, a well-functioning solution for a retail CBDC requires clarifications in the Financial Contracts\nAct's provisions on means of settlement (Section 2-1) and time and place of payment (Section 2-2). The\nFinancial Contracts Act may also specify that CBDC payments shall be deemed final and that the\npayments have legal protection against payers' creditors.\n\nThird, it may be appropriate to amend Section 16-4 on the obligation of banks to receive cash and to\nmake deposits available for their customers in the form of cash so that these provisions are also extended\nto comprise CBDCs.\n\nFourth, various provisions in the special legislation on public benefits and tax and duty claims should be\nadapted to CBDCs becoming a new generally accepted means of payment. If CBDCs are to be used for\npublic receipts and payments, regulatory adjustments must be made.\n\n\n\n\n33\n   A Norges Bank Staff Memo is published together with this Report, see Norges Bank (2026c).\n34\n   See Section 3 in Norges Bank (2023b).\n35\n   Ibid, Section 3.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                       Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                  20\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 22 ===\nFifth, it must be clarified who is to be responsible for transaction monitoring under the Anti-Money\nLaundering Act with respect to CBDCs.\n\nSixth, if there are to be interest rates on CBDCs, this will raise some special issues that should be\nresolved in both the Central Bank Act and special legislation.\n\nSeventh, the technical design of CBDCs could require distinct legislative changes. Special attention has\nbeen paid in both Norges Bank's exploration project and internationally to a solution where CBDCs are\nissued and distributed on blockchain-based solutions. Compared with account-based solutions,\nblockchain-based solutions raise several issues. For example, this applies to the legal status of CBDCs if\nCBDC identifiers are transferred or converted between different blockchains, including if Norges Bank is\nthe issuer after a conversion. In simplified terms, conversion in this context means that CBDCs are moved\nor transferred between different blockchains by using bridges. (The technical solution for such bridges is\ndescribed in Section 3 above.) It must also be determined whether end-users can hold CBDCs in their\nown software (often referred to as self-custody) or whether only approved custodians will be able to hold\nCBDCs on behalf of end-users. The legal status of CBDCs and responsibility for the entities that will verify\nindividual payments, ie validating nodes, must also be clarified. Consideration must be given to the fact\nthat this type of issue can be further regulated, eg by a statutory authority in the Central Bank Act or other\nfinancial regulation.\n\n7.2. Legislative amendments needed for the introduction of a\nwholesale CBDC\nIn legal terms, the introduction of a wholesale CBDC is considerably simpler than a retail CBDC. The first\ntype of CBDC entails that some bank reserves are converted into tokens to be able to settle securities\ntrades on a tokenised platform. Under Section 3-1, Sub-section 1 of the Central Bank Act, Norges Bank is\nauthorised to \"accept deposits from ... banks and other financial sector undertakings\" (cf litra a), and\n\"issue and deal in own financial instruments\" (cf litra c). In principle, there is no doubt that Norges Bank is\nalready authorised to issue a wholesale CBDC.\n\nNevertheless, there is legal uncertainty attached to how digital tokens should be characterised. As many\ntokens have a technical structure, the value is embedded in the token itself. It is then natural to consider a\ntoken a bearer instrument subject to property law. For example, a bearer instrument would not be subject\nto limitation under the Limitation Act 36, and furthermore a bearer instrument could be subject to a\nrestitution claim (vindication). 37\n\nOn the other hand, a bank deposit is a simple monetary claim subject to obligation law. The value\nconsists of a claim against the issuer and is not represented in the token. A simple monetary claim can be\nlimited under the Limitation Act and there is no item that may be claimed for restitution.\n\nIf Norges Bank introduces a wholesale CBDC, it should be clarified in Section 3-1, Subsection 1 of the\nCentral Bank Act that tokenised reserves are to continue to be deemed deposits and, a contrario, not as\nfinancial instruments. Alternatively, a statutory authority can be introduced whereby Norges Bank is\nauthorised to decide the matter.\n\nIt has been stated that uncertainty as to how a digital token should be legally considered is a general\nproblem. For example, it is unclear whether tokenised bank deposits should still be deemed to be simple\n\n\n36\n  LOV-1979-05-18-18.\n37\n  A claim issued in the form of a bearer instrument would be subject to limitation against the issuer (cf Section 1, Subsection 1 of\nthe Limitation Act on the Act applies to \"[c]laims on money or other benefits\"). But the instrument itself is deemed to be an item\nsubject to ownership rights and is not subject to limitation. The relationship can be compared to withdrawn banknotes.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                          Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                         21\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 23 ===\nmonetary claims or bearer certificates. This ambiguity means that using tokenised bank deposits as a\nmeans of payment between customers of different banks may be problematic. Resolving this uncertainty\nmust be regarded as a matter for the legislator.\n\nIt has also been discussed whether Norges Bank instead of issuing a wholesale CBDC, should facilitate\nspecial settlement of tokenised transactions but without the tokenisation of central bank reserves\nthemselves. Pursuant to Section 3-3, Subsection 2 of the Central Bank Act \"Norges Bank [shall] facilitate\na stable and efficient system for payment, clearing and settlement between undertakings that hold\naccounts with the Bank\", and pursuant to Section 1-3, Subsection 3 Norges Bank shall \"facilitate the\ncentral settlement system and oversee the payment system.\" In Norges Bank's assessment, facilitating\nNorges Bank's settlement system (NBO) for the settlement of tokenised trades lies clearly within the\ncentral bank's current mandate, and it cannot be ruled out that such arrangements would require\nlegislative amendments.\n\n\n8. Can regulated stablecoins satisfy a need for a tokenised\n   money alternative?\nStablecoins are cryptoassets designed to maintain a stable value against a reference asset, such as\nnational currencies. For the stablecoins issued so far, the reference asset is generally the US dollar.\nMarket forces determine the value of stablecoins, and establishing mechanisms that maintain full value\nstability is challenging.\n\nStablecoins are bearer instruments where there is no final settlement in central bank money, neither for\nindividual payments using stablecoins nor when stablecoins are exchanged. There is therefore no\ninteroperability as with bank deposits, which means their values can differ. A significant increase in the\nscope of stablecoins undermines the principle of singleness of money.\n\nCompetition between different stablecoins does not necessary result in the public choosing the most\nstable stablecoins. On the other hand, competition may be based on other characteristics, such as the\npossibility of return and being able to make anonymous payments. Furthermore, economies of scale may\nmake some stablecoins dominant even though their stability characteristics are not optimal.\n\nStablecoins may be a source of systematic risk, partly because a loss of confidence may lead to the rapid\nredemption of eg the securities where the issuer has invested the reserves. A considerable reduction in\nthe market value of these securities could then arise, which in turn can have contagion effects to the\nwider financial system if the volume of stablecoins becomes substantial.\n\nRegulation may enhance stablecoin stability and reduce the risk stablecoins pose to financial stability.\nSuch regulation consists of eg requirements for hedging, redemption and transparency, and are\nimplemented in the EU/EEA through the Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCAR). However,\nregulation can work against their intended purpose, and at worst impede the conduct of monetary policy.\nThis may occur if the regulation enhances stablecoin safety in a way that displaces bank deposits and\nweakens the role of banks in the transmission mechanism of monetary policy. The amount of issued\nstablecoins is also less flexible / elastic than the amount of bank deposits, which can disrupt credit\nintermediation. Banks have the flexibility to create money by extending credit, while stablecoins must be\nsecured before they can be issued.\n\nStablecoins therefore have limitations preventing them from meeting users' and society's need for money\nin the same way as bank deposits. Improved regulation may address some of the problems with\nstablecoins. At the same time, tokenised bank deposits may be a better alternative as a tokenised means\n\n\nNorges Bank                                Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                         22\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 24 ===\nof payment than stablecoins. However, it cannot be assumed that the characteristics of tokenised bank\ndeposits will be automatically the same as those of existing deposit money or that these deposits will out-\ncompete stablecoins. As stablecoins are bearer instruments, they could be used by everyone with a\nwallet in which such money can be held, independent of the currency of the country the payer and payee\nare in. This feature can be very difficult to accommodate in tokenised bank deposits as the payer must\nhave access to the relevant payment currency and the payee must have an account that is able to\nreceive that same currency.\n\nIt is important to facilitate payment service competition, but systemically important settlements should be\nmade in central bank money. Introducing wholesale CBDC may therefore be a suitable measure for more\nefficient settlement of tokenised bank deposits, while still preserving key characteristics of bank deposit\npayments such as interoperability.\n\n\n9. Exploration and introduction of CBDCs in other countries\n9.1. International CBDC exploration focus\nMany central banks are researching CBDCs, with a shift towards wholesale CBDCs. In 2025, the\nBIS published a survey conducted in 2024 that showed around 91% of respondent central banks were\nresearching CBDC in some form. 38 A key motivation for central banks' CBDC exploration is the need to\npreserve the role of central bank money while cash use is falling and prospects of tokenising traditional\nassets are increasing. Retail CBDC is also regarded as an instrument for financial inclusion. While earlier\nexploration was mostly focused on retail CBDCs, the focus has now shifted more to wholesale CBDCs, at\nleast for central banks in advanced economies. Many central banks attribute the shift to developments in\nstablecoins and other cryptoassets. The survey also shows that, over the next six years, more central\nbanks consider it likely that they will introduce a wholesale CBDC than consider it likely that they will\nintroduce a retail CBDC.\n\nFew central banks have introduced a retail CBDC, and none of these are located near Norway. So\nfar, only central banks in the Bahamas, Jamaica and Nigeria have introduced a CBDC, all of which are\nretail CBDCs. In addition, the People’s Republic of China (PRoC) has carried out a pilot project with a\nconsiderable number of registered users. However, like the other CBDC solutions, the Chinese solution is\nrelatively little used (see Thomson (2026)). 39\n\nWith the exception of the ECB / Eurosystem, many other peer jurisdictions have stated that they do not\ncurrently see a need for a retail CBDC. In that respect, Norges Bank's view on retail CBDC is in line with\nmost other comparable central banks.\n\nGreater focus on wholesale CBDC. So far, no central banks have introduced a wholesale CBDC as a\npermanent solution. However, a number of central banks are participating in testing and pilots (ie with real\ntransactions and assets) to explore different solutions for central bank settlement of tokenised\ntransactions. This applies particularly in regional and global financial centres, but also elsewhere.\nTherefore, there appears to be increased attention on the need for a wholesale CBDC to help realise the\nbenefits of tokenised solutions, especially in securities.\n\nAn overview is provided below of the status as of March 2026 with regard to the introduction of and\napproach to CBDCs in selected relevant countries/currency areas.\n\n\n BIS (2025b).\n38\n39\n  Thomson (2026) reports in Central Banking that China has recently launched a new e-CNY design (e-CNY 2.0) where the e-CNY\nmoves from the digital cash concept to digital deposit money.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                     Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                                23\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 25 ===\n9.2. Status in the ECB\nOf the jurisdictions with which it is natural to compare Norway, the ECB is probably the central bank that\nhas come the furthest in its exploration CBDCs.\n\nThe ECB is working purposefully towards introducing a digital euro. 40 Since 2019, the\nECB/Eurosystem has been exploring the need for a digital euro. The ECB has a somewhat different\nreasoning for the introduction of a digital euro compared with the reasoning underlying Norges Bank's\nexploration. Among the most important differences is that the ECB emphasises that access to central\nbank money can be considered a collective good and that a digital euro is therefore a natural extension of\nphysical cash since cash is used less and less for payments. Furthermore, the introduction of a digital\neuro is justified by the argument that it would provide access to claims on the central bank/government,\nand that the ability to withdraw CBDC from bank accounts is important for confidence in bank deposits\nand for underpinning the singleness of money. Another objective of the introduction of a digital euro is\nthat it must promote financial inclusion. The ECB further emphasises, as a strategic measure, that the\nintroduction of a digital euro must reduce dependency on foreign payment solutions and thereby\nstrengthen European autonomy.\n\nThe ECB is working on a rulebook that will describe terms and responsibilities among stakeholders in a\ndigital euro infrastructure. Furthermore, the ECB/Eurosystem has entered into contracts with private\nservice providers for the development of five different components of digital euro infrastructure, including\nan offline solution that should function in emergency situations such as loss of access to the internet.\n\nA proposal to regulate the digital euro is currently being deliberated in the EU’s legislative bodies, but it is\nuncertain if and when these rules will be adopted by the EU. The most recent signals suggest that the Act\nwill be adopted in 2026 H2. The ECB will decide whether to introduce a digital euro once the legislation is\nin place. A pilot project is in the pipeline for H2 2027, with a possible launch in 2029 (see (ECB, 2026e)).\n\nCentral bank settlement of tokenised transactions/wholesale CBDC: The ECB/Eurosystem has also\ninvestigated and tested settlement solutions in central bank money for transactions and trades of\ntokenised assets. In this context, the ECB has published a plan 41 where the first stage is to adapt the\nordinary RTGS system for euro (T2) to enable the settlement of tokenised asset transactions in central\nbank money in this system. According to the current plan, such an opportunity will only become available\nat the end of 2026 Q3. 42\n\nIn the longer term, the ECB is investigating the benefits and challenges associated with establishing its\nown DLT-based platform for the settlement of tokenised transactions in central bank money. It is more\nuncertain if and when such a solution will be introduced. The goal of the project is not to establish a\ntechnical solution. 43\n\nLarge European banks and other stakeholders in the financial sector have been involved in testing of\nearlier versions of these solutions.\n\n9.3. Status in Sweden\nSveriges Riksbank was among the first to start testing and issuing statements on the need for an e-krona,\nand has recently signalled renewed focus on CBDCs.\n\n\n\n40\n   See inter alia ECB (2025b), (2026a) and (2026e)\n41\n   See ECB (2025a).\n42\n   For more on the work with Pontes, see ECB (2026c).\n43\n   For more on the work with Appia, see ECB (2026d).\n\n\nNorges Bank                                       Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                   24\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 26 ===\n            “Between 2017 and 2023, the Riksbank investigated economic, legal and technical issues related\n            to a digital version of the Swedish krona – an e-krona. In 2023, the government Payment Inquiry,\n            which examined the role of the state in the payment market, concluded that there were not\n            sufficient societal needs for the Riksbank to issue an e-krona. However, the report emphasised\n            that developments are rapid and that economic, political and technological changes may require\n            a reassessment. Since 2023, the Riksbank’s focus has therefore been on analysing and\n            monitoring international work on central bank digital currencies – in particular the digital euro.”\n            (Payments Report (2026), p. 25)\n\nIn a speech at the Center for Business and Policy Studies on 4 December 2025, the Riksbank's first\nDeputy Governor, Aino Bunge, stated:\n\n            \"One way to view the ECB's digital euro development activities is that many countries – and many\n            countries' central banks – are coming together to build a common system. This reduces both\n            costs and risks for each individual country. Sweden could benefit from it in the same way as we\n            have already done with the TARGET systems. We will therefore explore the opportunities and\n            possible challenges of sharing the digital euro platform with a potential Swedish e-krona in the\n            future. But developing an e-krona would take time. If and when the ECB after political approval\n            takes the decision to develop and issue a digital euro, we as a country should know what we want\n            and be ready to make suggestions on how to proceed. We should therefore likely already next\n            year start discussing how to approach the issue of an e-krona if the digital euro is introduced.\"\n            (Sveriges Riksbank (2025), p.9).\n\nFinally, Governor Erik Thedéen of Sveriges Riksbank stated in a speech published in the Sweden Herald\non 9 December 2025: “I have not said that we should have a digital crown, but I have said that it is good\nto investigate and possibly have some legislative work to be prepared.” 44\n\n9.4. Status in Denmark\nDanmarks Nationalbank is sceptical about whether a retail CBDC is needed but is monitoring\ndevelopments in both retail and wholesale CBDC closely and participates actively in international\nexploration in the field.\n\nIn a speech on 19 February 2024, Central Bank Governor Signe Krogstrup of Danmarks Nationalbank\nstated:\n\n            \" …(I)n Denmark we currently do not see the possibilities for a CBDC to address them given the\n            specific circumstances we have in Denmark. …(W)ith the well-functioning digital payments\n            infrastructure already in place in Denmark, it is not clear how a CBDC would offer added value\n            and compete on trust and convenience, and hence be adopted in Denmark. …(W)e follow the\n            development closely and reassess the arguments for and against our own CBDC on an ongoing\n            basis \". (Danmarks Nationalbank (2024), p.9).\n\nIn a speech on 21 January 2026, Krogstrup stated:\n\n            “Issuing tokenised central bank money can help foster resilience. This resilience is one of the key\n            motivations of our joint work with the ECB on developing the infrastructure for wholesale CBDC.\n            The purpose is to ensure access to a central bank-issued or -linked settlement asset for\n            systemically important transactions, and enable lender-of-last-resort functions in a potential\n\n\n\n44\n     Sweden Herald (2025).\n\n\nNorges Bank                                   Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                      25\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 27 ===\n         tokenised financial system. This work may also support the use of tokenised bank deposits as a\n         complementary element in the future payments system.\n         The ECB’s work on a retail CBDC, the digital euro, is also seen as important for the future\n         ecosystem of payments and resilience. While we do not, at this point, see a need for a retail\n         CBDC in Denmark, we follow the ECB’s work closely.” (Danmarks Nationalbank (2026), s.8)\n\n9.5. Status update of the three countries that have introduced a retail\nCBDC\nThe countries that have introduced a retail CBDC have been particularly motivated by financial\ninclusion and reducing the costs related to the use of cash. Central banks in Nigeria, Jamaica and\nthe Bahamas have introduced retail CBDC as a permanent arrangement. Financial inclusion has been a\nkey goal for each of these three countries, along with a desire to reduce the costs and risk related to cash\nhandling and distribution.\n\n     -   In 2020, the Central Bank of Bahamas introduced its retail CBDC solution, the Sand Dollar. The\n         issued volume and use of the Sand Dollar remain relatively modest and mostly concentrated in\n         the peripheral areas of the island nation. The central bank also provides substantial incentives to\n         promote the use of Sand Dollars. Nevertheless, only a small share of transactions across the\n         country are in Sand Dollars. 45\n     -   In 2021, the Central Bank of Nigeria launched the retail CBDC solution, eNaira. However, less\n         than 1% of the population has so far adopted this CBDC. Both technical problems, poor and\n         insufficient communication and access to other means of payment affect the extent to which the\n         eNaira has been used. 46 To increase usage, work underway to allow eNaira use for public\n         receipts and payments, and to strengthen communication to the public on the advantages of\n         eNaira use.\n     -   In 2022, the Bank of Jamaica introduced the retail CBDC solution, JAM-DEX. 47 The solution is\n         based on a dynamic QR code that can be used in payment terminals. The use of JAM-DEX has\n         been relatively limited so far, partly due to the public's preference for cash. In addition, the\n         payment solution has had technical issues. A further challenge is that use of JAM-DEX requires\n         merchants to install separate payment terminals.\n\n\n10. Implications for Norway of a potential introduction of a\n  digital euro 48\nA digital euro may become popular among tourists at major tourist destinations, but usage in\nNorway is unlikely to notably affect the Norwegian payment system or otherwise weaken Norges\nBank's ability to perform its mission. Danmarks Nationalbank and Sveriges Riksbank published\npapers in 2023 and 2024, respectively 49 including analyses of how a digital euro would affect key\nconditions in the payment systems of Denmark and Sweden and their conduct of monetary policy.\nDanmark's Nationalbank's paper concludes that a digital euro with low holding limits would not have a\nsignificant impact on Denmark's fixed exchange rate policy and that a digital euro would only be used to a\nlimited extent in Denmark. Similarly, the paper from Sveriges Riksbank concludes that a digital euro,\n\n\n\n45\n   Central Bank of Bahamas (2025).\n46\n   Mahachi (2025).\n47\n   Bank of Jamaica (2022).\n48\n   For a more detailed assessment, see Norges Bank (2026a).\n49\n   Danmarks Nationalbank (2023) and Sveriges Riksbank (2024b).\n\n\nNorges Bank                                     Norges Bank Papers 1/2026                                 26\n\n\n=== DOC 6 PAGE 28 ===\nprovided that it can be used in Sweden, would have a limited impact, but that it could strengthen payment\npreparedness and competition.\n\nNorges Bank has conducted an equivalent assessment for Norway, focusing on the implications for\nfinancial stability, efficiency in the payment system and the possibility to conduct liquidity management\nand monetary policy should the ECB decide to introduce a digital euro and should its use be permitted in\nNorway.\n\nThe analysis concludes that a potential digital euro in Norway would require broad assessments and\nadjustments but usage is unlikely to be of such an extent that it would affect Norges Bank's mission.\n\nIn accordance with the draft regulation of the digital euro, the digital euro can be used in countries outside\nthe euro area but inside the EEA once the relevant central banks have signed an agreement with the\nECB regarding such use. Assuming that some amendments are made to Norwegian law and Norges\nBank signs the aforementioned agreement, the digital euro could be used in Norway under the same\nconditions as in the euro area.\n\nThe impact of a digital euro on the efficiency and security of the Norwegian payment system, financial\nstability or the conduct of liquidity management and monetary policy in Norway will depend on the extent\nof domestic use. Norges Bank's analyses show that there is no reason to expect that a digital euro will be\nused to the extent that it affects the payment system in general. The introduction of a digital euro is not\nlikely to lead to currency substitution to an extent that threatens financial stability or affects the conduct of\nliquidity management or monetary policy.\n\nHowever, in areas and among businesses with a considerable influx of foreign customers, it can be\nenvisaged that a non-negligible share of future payments could be made with digital euros, for example at\ntypical tourist destinations. In that case, it would also provide Norwegian holders of digital euros with an\nalternative means of payment. In isolation, this would lead to stronger competition for payment services at\nthe relevant points of sale and strengthen preparedness at these locations. However, this would probably\nhave a limited effect in specific areas with many tourists 50, and is unlikely to have any material impact on\nthe efficiency and security of the Norwegian payment system overall.\n\n\n11.
