A CBDC may be necessary to\nensure that NOK will continue to be a safe, efficient and attractive means of payment in the\nfuture too.” (Norges Bank, 2022)\nAs a follow-up to this strategic objective, Norges Bank established a project to explore the\nneed for introducing a central bank digital currency (CBDC1) and, if so, how a CBDC\nsolution should be designed2. The project assumed that the solution would be based on\nblockchain technology3 so that the benefits of this technology could be utilised. As part of\nthe investigation, the project conducted technical and functional testing in two\ntechnological sandboxes. The aim of the testing was to gain insight into how blockchain\ntechnology can be used to promote a safe, efficient and attractive payment system, and to\nhighlight the benefits, challenges, and drawbacks of different technical alternatives.\n\nThe two sandboxes are based on two different ledger4 platforms, connected via a bridge\nthat enables the transfer of tokens5 (CBDC, tokenised bank deposits (TBD), and tokenised\nsecurities) between them. The test programme in this investigation phase includes various\nforms of payments with retail CBDC between individuals, the use of wholesale CBDC for\nsettlement of payments between banks, payments with TBD with central bank settlement\nin tokenised reserves, and the use of ledger technology to automate and streamline\ngovernment debt management.\n\nThe test cases were modelled on key processes in the payment and financial system, but\ncarried out in a simplified and adapted manner to test the characteristics, benefits, and\nchallenges of various alternative technological designs. The test programme did not\ninclude capacity tests, stress tests, or the technology’s ability to handle abnormal events\nand attacks from malicious actors.\n\nThe main findings can be summarised in three points. Firstly, a single-ledger6 architecture,\nwhere CBDC, TBD, and tokenized securities are registered on the same ledger, offers\n\n\n1\n  Central bank digital currency (CBDC) is a digital form of central bank money issued by the central bank and represents a direct claim on\nthe central bank.\n2\n  See Norges Bank (2026).\n3\n  Blockchain technology: A type of distributed ledger (DLT) in which transactions are stored in ‘blocks’ that are cryptographically linked\ntogether in a chain and updated/synchronised between multiple participants without a single shared central database. ‘A blockchain is a\ndecentralised and distributed digital “ledger” that makes it possible to record, track, and visualise all digital transactions’ (Store norske\nleksikon, 2026).\n4\n  The term “ledger” is used in the report as a collective term for a shared, distributed, and cryptographically secured register (DLT), and\nincludes both blockchains and other variants of distributed ledgers/registers.\n5\n  Token/tokenisation: Tokenisation involves representing money, securities, or other rights as digital tokens on a ledger, so that ownership\nand transfers can be handled in a programmable way.\n6\n  Single-ledger: An architecture where all digital assets are tokenised and managed on a single DLT/ledger. Transfers and settlements\ntake place within the same ledger, without the need for multiple DLTs or bridges between them.\nNorges Bank                                             Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                           2\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 3 ===\nadvantages. Specifically, the architecture enables atomic7 settlement across assets,\neliminates counterparty risk, provides a high degree of traceability, and simplifies technical\ncompliance with certain regulatory requirements. Secondly, multi-ledger8 architectures —\nwhere different assets such as CBDC, TBD, and securities are registered on different\nledgers and moved between them using bridges9 — increase technical and operational\ncomplexity and present a larger attack surface. Delivery versus Payment (DvP)10 can be\nachieved, but when values are settled across different ledgers, this does not occur\nsimultaneously. This means that atomic settlement cannot be achieved with such a\ndesign. This increases the need for governance, monitoring, and standardisation. Thirdly,\nthe most significant benefits of CBDC and ledger technology will only be realised if actors\nother than the central bank itself can register their own assets on the ledger and develop\nsolutions that use CBDC as a means of settlement.\n\nThis report documents the technical design, implementation, and findings from the testing\nin the sandboxes. The report is a technical elaboration and supporting report to the\nproject's final report11, and does not contain recommendations on the introduction of\nCBDC.\n\nThe source code that forms the basis for the test systems in the sandboxes will be\npublished as open source and can be found in our github repository12.\n\n\n\n\n7\n  Atomicity means that a compound transaction is either executed in its entirety or not executed at all. All relevant state changes, such as\nthe transfer of money and securities, are treated as a single indivisible operation. There are no partial or intermediate states where only\nparts of the transaction are completed. Atomicity thus eliminates the risk of one party delivering without the consideration being fulfilled\nsimultaneously.\n8\n  Multi-ledger: A multi-ledger architecture means that different assets or actors use separate ledgers, and that settlement between them\nrequires coordination or bridges.\n9\n  Bridges: A technical mechanism that connects two (or more) separate DLTs so that tokens/values and relevant messages can be\ntransferred between them, typically by locking/burning on one ledger and issuance/disbursement on another.\n10\n   Delivery versus Payment (DvP) is a settlement principle whereby the delivery of an asset and payment occur simultaneously and are\nconditional on each other, so that neither party is exposed to counterparty risk in the settlement. In traditional systems, DvP is achieved\nthrough coordination between separate securities and payment systems. In DLT-based solutions, DvP can either be implemented as a\nsingle technical atomic transaction when money and assets are represented on the same ledger (single-ledger), or as an orchestrated\nprocess across multiple ledgers, where simultaneity and finality are not achieved through a single technical operation.\n11\n   See Norges Bank (2026).\n12\n     See Github (2026)\nNorges Bank                                            Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                          3\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 4 ===\nTable of Contents\n  Introduction if\nappropriate, of a central bank digital currency (CBDC). A CBDC may be necessary to\nensure that NOK will continue to be a safe, efficient and attractive means of payment in the\nfuture too.” (Norges Bank, 2022, p.10)\n\nAs a follow-up to this strategic objective, Norges Bank established a project to explore the\nneed for introducing central bank digital currency (CBDC) and, if so, how such a CBDC\nsolution should be designed.13 The project was based on the premise that a CBDC solution\nshould be based on blockchain technology so that the benefits of this technology could be\nutilised. As part of the exploration, the project carried out technical and functional testing\nof the technology for payment and other financial services in two technological\nsandboxes14.\n\nThis report describes the technological choices underlying the establishment of the\nsandboxes. It also explains how the sandboxes were established and how the technical\nand functional testing was carried out. Finally, the main findings from the testing are\nsummarised.\n\nThe overall objective of the technical testing has been to obtain information relevant to\nassessing whether the introduction of CBDC based on blockchain technology will\ncontribute to an efficient and secure payment system. The testing was intended to provide\ninsight into the benefits, challenges, and disadvantages of various technical choices,\nincluding how money, securities, and other claims can be represented in tokenised form on\na ledger, how values can be transferred between ledgers, how settlements can be\nautomated using smart contracts15, and how roles and responsibilities can be technically\nenforced on a common technological platform.\n\nThe technological sandboxes are established as isolated test environments without\nconnection to production systems. The sandboxes were developed in collaboration with\ntwo external IT vendors. They are based on two different ledger platforms and are\nconnected by a technological bridge enabling the ‘movement’ of tokens between the\n\n\n\n13\n   See Norges Bank (2026)\n14\n   A technological sandbox is a confined test environment where technology and functionality can be tried out safely, without affecting\nreal systems or data.\n15\n   Smart contract: programme code that runs on a ledger and automatically enforces rules, rights, and transactions when predefined\nconditions are met.\n\nNorges Bank                                            Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                          5\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 6 ===\nledgers by locking value on one side and issuing a representation of the value on the other\nledger16.\n\n\n     DLT, blockchain, and ledger\n     In this report, the term ‘ledger’ is used to refer to a blockchain for recording ownership\n     and transactions based on technology that falls under DLT (Distributed Ledger\n     Technology). DLT is a shared, distributed, and cryptographically secured register, and\n     also includes other variants of distributed ledger besides blockchains. The ledger\n     solutions tested in the project have not necessarily been shared or distributed between\n     independent actors. The blockchain may just as well be centralised or partially\n     distributed within a controlled and permissioned setup.\n\n\nThe sandboxes were used to experiment with different architectures and mechanisms,\nsetting aside the full complexity of today's infrastructure. Payments, settlement and\nsecurities trading, and government securities lifecycle management are modelled to\nresemble key elements of current systems and processes, but have been deliberately\nsimplified and generalised. Consequently, the tests do not cover all details of existing\nprocesses, regulatory requirements, or interfaces with external systems.\n\nThe testing did not include load and stress tests or the technology's ability to handle error\nevents or attacks from malicious actors. The focus has been on expected use and\noperation under normal conditions (‘happy path’), architecture choices, rights and role\nframeworks, and the interaction between ledgers, smart contracts, and adjacent system\ncomponents, such as bridges.\n\nThe testing approach has been twofold. Firstly, we wanted to explore the applicability of the\ntechnology in realistic use cases based on practical needs and existing processes in the\npayment and financial system. Secondly, we wanted to use different solutions and\narchitectures in each scenario to identify the strengths and weaknesses of different\napproaches and different applications of the technology17.\n\nThe testing explored the characteristics of two types of CBDC, each serving a different\npurpose18:\n\n\n\n\n16\n   In practice, the money never moves, but is locked on one ledger in order to be represented and used for payments on another ledger.\nTherefore, descriptions such as ‘moving’ and ‘bridges’ are only metaphors for what actually happens in a DLT context.\n17\n   The testing was limited to a selection of ledger technologies, including Hyperledger Besu and Hyperledger Fabric, and is intended to\nhighlight architectural and design choices rather than provide a comprehensive technology comparison.\n18\n   For a more detailed description of the purpose of introducing CBDC, see Norges Bank (2026)\nNorges Bank                                            Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                          6\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 7 ===\nRetail CBDC are central bank digital currency available to the public for use in payments at\npoints of sale, between individuals, in online commerce, and for receipts and\ndisbursements to and from the public sector. The introduction of retail CBDC will entail a\nsignificant restructuring of the payment system and, if widely adopted, may affect the roles\nand business models of banks and other private actors in the payment system.\n\nWholesale CBDC is comparable to traditional central bank reserves, but on a new\ntechnological platform based on ledger technology. Wholesale CBDC is only available to\nbanks and other financial actors with accounts at the central bank, and is used for\nsettlement between banks, including in connection with trading in tokenised securities and\nTBD.\n\nThe majority of the testing has focused on wholesale CBDC between banks. With the\nexception of test case 1, which focuses on retail CBDC, the tests deal with the use of CBDC\nas a means of settlement in interbank settlements and in interaction with tokenised\nsecurities and TBD.\n\nA total of six test cases were carried out during the project period:\n\n    •   Test case 1: Establishment of a ledger for retail CBDC and exploration of the\n        characteristics of such a ledger.\n\n    •   Test case 2: Establishment of a ledger for wholesale CBDC and exploration of the\n        characteristics of such a ledger.\n\n    •   Test case 3: Establishment of a bridge for transfers of tokens/values between\n        ledgers and exploration of the characteristics of such a bridge.\n\n    •   Test case 4: Characteristics of tokenised bank deposits in a multi-ledger\n        architecture.\n\n    •   Test case 5: Trading and settlement of tokenised securities on a single shared\n        ledger.\n\n    •   Test case 6: Issuance and life cycle of government bonds on a single shared ledger.\n\nSome of the tests were conducted in collaboration with DNB as a participating bank in the\nsandboxes. DNB Carnegie has also contributed to the design of certain test scenarios\ninvolving securities trading. This has made it possible to test technical solutions against\nrelevant needs and transactions among actors in the financial sector.\n\nEach test case is described in separate sections with the following structure: first, the\npurpose of the test case is described, followed by technical design. Finally, the results,\n\nNorges Bank                           Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                               7\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 8 ===\ninsights, and findings of each test case are summarised. At the very end of the report,\nlessons learned from all test cases are summarised.\n\nTest case 1 — Establishment of a ledger for retail CBDC and exploration\nof the characteristics of such a ledger\nThe purpose of the test case\nThe purpose of the test case was to explore how retail CBDC can be designed in a two-tier\ndistribution model, where the central bank has technically enforceable control19 over\nframeworks, rules, and the issuance and redemption of central bank money, while banks\nand regulated third parties have responsibility for customer relationships and customer\ndata. The test was based on the premise that the central bank must ensure that no other\nactors can issue and destroy CBDC, while at the same time the central bank has no access\nto information about individual customers’ payments with or holdings of CBDC.\n\nA key objective was to explore how roles, rights, and governance capabilities can be\ntechnically enforced in a DLT-based solution. Ledger technology does not include\nembedded access control, and the test case was therefore designed to illustrate how the\nnecessary mechanisms for access control and division of responsibilities can be defined\nexplicitly, both on the ledger and in adjacent systems. This included exploring how the\nsolution can support the principle that banks have direct customer contact, in line with the\ncurrent division of responsibilities in the payment system.\n\nFurthermore, the aim was to explore how the central bank can obtain real-time, aggregated\noverviews of circulation, and holdings in the system without access to detailed customer\ndata. The test case thus provided insight into how access control at the ledger level can be\nused to balance the central bank’s need for governance and control of issuance and\nredemption of central bank money with consideration for privacy, division of\nresponsibilities, and the role of banks in a retail CBDC solution.\n\n\n\n\n19\n  In this report, the term ‘the central bank’s control’ is used to describe the central bank’s technically enforceable rights and\nresponsibilities in the tested architecture, particularly in relation to the issuance and destruction of CBDC, the setting of overall rules,\nand access to aggregated overview data.\nThe term does not imply full operational control over all components, ongoing operation of all ledgers, or access to detailed information\nabout transactions or customer relationships at banks and other actors. Operational responsibility, data access, and technical operation\nmay be distributed among several actors in line with the architecture and role framework.\nNorges Bank                                            Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                           8\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 9 ===\nTechnical design\nThe sandbox solution in this test case is based on Hyperledger Besu, which is a\npermissioned ledger compatible with the Ethereum Virtual Machine (EVM)20.\n\nThe solution follows a two-tier distribution model as illustrated in Figure 1. The central bank\nconstitutes the top layer and is the only actor with the right to issue and redeem retail\nCBDC. Banks and other approved, regulated third parties constitute the distribution layer\nand act as intermediaries between the central bank and the end customers. End\ncustomers do not participate directly in the ledger’s governance structure, but use services\nprovided by banks and other third-party actors.\n\n\n\n\n        Figure 1: Two-tier distribution model\n\n\n\nThe distribution of responsibilities and access is technically enforced through role-based\nsmart contracts on the ledger. Banks are responsible for customer onboarding and for\nlinking customers to ledger addresses that are registered in the banks’ own account\nregisters. Only addresses approved by banks can hold and use retail CBDC. End customers\ncan manage their own holdings and make payments, but cannot create addresses or\n\n20\n  Ethereum Virtual Machine (EVM) is a standardised execution model for smart contracts used by Ethereum and a number of compatible\nledger platforms, including Hyperledger Besu. EVM defines how contract code is executed deterministically across nodes, how accounts\nand contracts store state, and how transactions are validated and recorded in a shared ledger.\nNorges Bank                                         Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                      9\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 10 ===\nchange the rights themselves. The central bank does not have access to customer data\nand does not have responsibility for AML/KYC/CTF, but through its own roles it has access\nto aggregated overviews of circulation, holdings, and transaction activity on the ledger.\n\n\n\n\nFigure 2: High-level architecture sketch of the retail CBDC sandbox\n\n\n\n\nNorges Bank                                     Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                   10\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 11 ===\nFigure 2 shows the solution design at a high level. A common application layer with user\ninterfaces and API21s handles authentication, authorisation, and interaction with the\nledger.\n\nVarious login mechanisms have been tested, including traditional login via external ID\nsolutions and the use of digital wallets. The ledger itself contains only what is necessary to\nrepresent ownership and transfers of retail CBDC, while customer information and identity\ndata remain at the banks and outside the ledger. The design has been deliberately chosen\nto test how technical mechanisms can ensure appropriate division of responsibilities and\ncontrol over issuance and redemption of central bank money while limiting access to\ncustomer data.\n\n\nResults and findings\nTesting in the retail sandbox shows that role-based access control can be technically\nenforced in a DLT-based solution, allowing the distribution of responsibilities and rights\nbetween the central bank, banks, and end customers to be implemented directly on the\nledger using smart contracts. Various login mechanisms were tested. Digital wallets\nprovide a high degree of user control but entail a risk of key loss. Logging in via external ID\nsolutions offers better user-friendliness and the possibility of recovery, but involves an\nadditional intermediary.\n\nThe testing was not intended to provide a basis for a comprehensive assessment of privacy\nin a legal sense, but to highlight technical features that are relevant to privacy matters. The\nsolution is designed so that customer information remains with the banks, while the ledger\nonly contains what is necessary to carry out transfers of retail CBDC. The solution is based\non pseudonymity22, which reduces the exposure of personal data during normal operation.\nHowever, this creates a structural privacy risk if ledger addresses can be linked to identity,\nas the full transaction history will then be visible. The choice of ledger model and address\nstructure therefore has significant implications for how privacy requirements can be met.\nAccess control at the application level is not sufficient to handle traceability at the ledger\nlevel.\n\n\n\n\n21\n   An Application Programming Interface (API) is a set of rules and protocols that enables different software systems to communicate\nwith each other and exchange data.\n22\n   Pseudonymity is a feature of a system where actions and data are linked to an identifier (a pseudonym) rather than directly to a real\nidentity. The pseudonym acts as a substitute for the person or actor, making it possible to track activity over time without necessarily\nknowing who is behind it. However, if the link between the pseudonym and the identity is established, past and future actions can be\nlinked to the same identity.\nNorges Bank                                             Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                          11\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 12 ===\nThe test also showed that updating and further developing smart contracts is demanding,\nas contracts are immutable after publication and changes require new contracts and\nmanaging dependencies.\n\nTest case 2 — Establishment of a ledger for wholesale CBDC and\nexploration of the characteristics of such a ledger\nThe purpose of the test case\nThe purpose of the test case was to establish a technical sandbox for CBDC used for\nsettlement between banks (wholesale CBDC) and to explore whether Hyperledger Fabric23\ncan support such a solution in a secure and controllable manner. The test case was\nintended to provide practical experience with the basic functions of an interbank\nsettlement system, where CBDC is used as a means of settlement between banks on a\nshared ledger.\n\nA key objective was to explore how the rights, access to information, and confidentiality\ncan be technically enforced in such a system. This requires an unambiguous definition of\nwhat actions different actors are entitled to perform on the ledger, and that the rights can\nbe technically enforced. Simultaneously, the solution must ensure confidentiality between\nbanks, so that banks can settle payments with each other without individual banks having\naccess to confidential information about other banks’ payments and so forth.\n\nFurthermore, the aim was to explore how the central bank can have access to sufficient\ninformation to ensure that the settlement system functions as intended. This includes,\namong other things, an overview of the total amount and use of CBDC in circulation,\nwithout the central bank having access to detailed data on transactions between banks.\n\nTechnical design\nIn test case 2, Hyperledger Fabric is used as a DLT platform to explore the properties of\nwholesale CBDC in an interbank settlement environment. The solution is established as a\njoint settlement platform where the central bank and all participating banks are part of the\nsame network, but with decentralised operation and differentiated access to information\nand information sharing.\n\nDecentralised operation means that each bank operates and hosts its own node on the\nledger and is thus responsible for its own technical infrastructure. Figure 3 visualises how\nthe ‘bank peer node’ is handled by each bank and connects to the ledger.\n\n\n23\n  Hyperledger Fabric is an open source platform for shared ledgers (DLT) developed under the Linux Foundation, designed for\nbusinesses that need controlled access and the ability to segregate data between participants.\nNorges Bank                                          Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                12\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 13 ===\nIn the tested solution, governance of access to information and information sharing are\nimplemented using private data collections (PDC). PDC enables settlements to be carried\nout on a single shared ledger, while selected parts of the transaction data are only shared\nbetween authorised parties.\n\nThe test scenario has been further expanded to explore a UTXO-based model for\nrepresenting wholesale CBDC, in contrast to the retail sandbox which was designed as an\naccount-based model.\n\n Token models\n The UTXO model can be understood as digital ‘banknotes’ or ‘value bits’ with a specific\n value. When a payment is made, one or more ‘value bits’ must be used to cover the total\n amount of the payment. For example, if NOK 100 is paid using a digital ‘NOK 200 note’,\n the original value bit is consumed and two new ones are created: one that is transferred\n to the recipient (NOK 100) and one that is returned to the payer as ‘change’ (NOK 100).\n The remaining value is an example of an unspent transaction output (UTXO).\n In the UTXO model, ownership is not linked to an account with a balance, but to each\n individual value bit. Ownership is defined by who is authorised to use the value bit as\n input in a new transaction. Each value bit has its own history from issuance to\n destruction, which makes it technically easier to verify that the same value bit cannot be\n used twice and to track values through subsequent transactions. This provides high\n traceability at the token level and may be well suited for parallel processing of\n transactions.\n\n The account-based model is similar to how banks currently manage bank deposits.\n Each participant has an account that shows a total balance at any given time. When a\n payment of NOK 100 is made, the payer’s balance is reduced by NOK 100 and the\n recipient’s balance increases accordingly. In this model, the focus is on the balance per\n account, not on the specific ‘value bits’ that make up the balance. Traceability is more\n closely linked to the transaction log and changes in account balances over time: it is\n possible to identify who has sent and received values and how balances change, but the\n value does not have its own ‘banknote history’. The account-based model often provides\n simpler balance management and more intuitive access control for data visibility at the\n account level.\n\n Both models can be used for digital settlements, but they represent different technical\n approaches with different strengths and weaknesses in terms of traceability, complexity,\n and access management.\n\n\nNorges Bank                         Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                               13\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 14 ===\nFigure 3: Architectural sketch of the CBDC sandbox for settlement. 1) Shows the components used by the central bank\nthat are part of the central bank’s infrastructure and operational responsibility. 2) shows the components used by each\nparticipating bank and which are part of each bank’s infrastructure and operational responsibility. 3) shows the\nHyperledger Fabric network, ie the ledger itself. Note that each peer node is located on the ledger. This is also where the\nwNOK (wholesale CBDC) token is located.\n\nThe solution has two separate user interfaces, one for the central bank and one for banks,\nboth of which are tailored to their respective roles. The central bank’s view provides an\noverview of CBDC in circulation, the central bank’s total holdings of CBDC, transaction\nactivity over selected time periods, and total amounts per bank. The banks’ user interface\ncan be used for payment orders and transfers of CBDC to other banks or back to the\ncentral bank, as well as to obtain information about the bank’s own balance and\ntransactions.\n\nThe test case includes three participating banks (here referred to as Bank A, Bank B, and\nBank C in Figure 3). This allowed us to test features such as access to the ledger, access to\ninformation, and shielding of information. With more participating banks, it becomes easier\nto assess how the solution behaves when more participants are included in the same\ninfrastructure.\nNorges Bank                                       Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                   14\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 15 ===\nFigure 4: Overview of how wholesale CBDC is issued (mint), destroyed (burn), and moved between participants in the\nsystem. In the figure, wNOK = wholesale CBDC.\n\nThe central bank issues wholesale CBDC and the newly created tokens are registered at\nthe central bank’s address on the ledger. From there, wholesale CBDC is distributed to\nparticipating banks (illustrated here as Bank A and Bank B). The banks can then use\nwholesale CBDC freely to settle payments between themselves through transfers of tokens\non the ledger. When a bank wishes to convert all or part of its holdings of wholesale CBDC\ninto traditional reserves, the wholesale CBDC is sent back to the central bank, which\ndestroys the tokens and hence takes them out of circulation. As an integrated part of the\nprocess, the holdings of traditional reserves will be increased accordingly. However, this\npart of the transaction has not been included in the test.\n\n\nResults and findings\nThis test case demonstrates that Hyperledger Fabric may be a relevant technology choice\nfor CBDC used for settlement between banks, particularly in situations where there are\ndifferent needs for access to information and where the information must be shielded. The\ntests show that this flexibility entails costs and challenges, including more demanding\nsetup, operation, and management than solutions based on a single fully shared ledger\nwith equal access to information for all.\n\nNorges Bank                                    Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                             15\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 16 ===\nThe test also shows that Hyperledger Fabric makes it technically possible to handle\nwholesale CBDC in both an account-based model and a UTXO-based model. Both models\nsupport the basic functions required in an interbank setting, including issuance, transfers\nbetween banks, and redemption. The UTXO-model also provides traceability at the token\nlevel, which may be relevant for verification, troubleshooting, and reconstruction of events.\n\nThe test shows that the use of PDC enables control over which transaction data different\nactors have technical read access to, even if the settlements are carried out on a common\nledger. In the tested solution, the central bank can monitor the interbank settlement,\nincluding amounts, counterparties, and timing, as well as access aggregated overviews of\ncirculation. Detailed bilateral transaction data and information about underlying customer\nrelationships are shielded and therefore not available to the central bank. PDC is used to\nensure that banks only have access to information about the settlements in which they\nthemselves are a part of, and not about other banks’ transactions.\n\nThe tests indicate that Hyperledger Fabric can be used as the technology for a CBDC\nsolution for settlement with technical feasibility for multiple token models, precise access\ncontrol for data visibility, and access to aggregated overview data. The primary problem\nidentified by the test is that the structure of this solution becomes relatively complex. As\nmore banks connect and more information boundaries need to be enforced, the\nrequirements for standardisation, governance, and operational robustness increase\ncompared to solutions based on a fully shared single-ledger.\n\n\n\nTest case 3 — Establishment of a bridge for transfers of tokens/values\nbetween ledgers and exploration of the characteristics of such a bridge\nThe purpose of the test case\nThe purpose of the test case was to explore the consequences of using wholesale CBDC as\na means of settlement in an infrastructure where CBDC and other values are registered on\ndifferent ledgers.\n\nThe test case was based on the central bank needing to control the use of CBDC on a\nseparate, closed ledger, while other actors wanted to develop payment solutions using\nsmart contracts on their own external ledgers. Through the test, we sought to clarify how\nwholesale CBDC could be used as a means of settlement across systems, how governance\nand role-based responsibility could be ensured, and what characteristics such a structure\nwould have.\n\nNorges Bank                          Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                             16\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 17 ===\nIn the test case, wholesale CBDC was registered on a central bank-controlled ledger, while\nsecurities and application logic were located on an external ledger outside of the central\nbank’s control. A bridge was used as a technical mechanism to enable interaction between\nthe two ledgers in such a multi-ledger structure.\n\n\n\nTechnical design\nThe bridge in test case 3 is built as a centralised link between two separate blockchain\nenvironments: a core ledger for wholesale CBDC based on Hyperledger Fabric, and an\nexternal ledger based on Hyperledger Besu. The purpose is to enable the use of CBDC in\napplications on the external ledger without actually moving CBDC out of the core ledger\nand without relinquishing control over central bank money in circulation.\n\nFigure 5 shows the main components and the money flow between them. On the left is the\ncore ledger with the wholesale CBDC contract. On the right-hand side is the external Besu\nledger, which contains, among other things, a wrapped24 wholesale CBDC contract\n(wNOK*) and a simple zero-coupon bond contract. Between those ledgers is the bridge\ncomponent, which has system access to both ledgers.\nWholesale CBDC can only be issued and destroyed by the central bank. In the bridge\nsetup, a custody account on the core ledger is used for temporary locking of CBDC when\nthe funds are made available on an external ledger. This ensures that the same CBDC value\ncannot be used in parallel on multiple platforms/systems.\nOn the external ledger, wholesale CBDC is represented by a smart contract where only the\nbridge has the rights to issue and destroy tokens. The bridge is implemented as an external\ncomponent connected to both ledgers and is responsible for coordinating transfers of\nCBDC.\nThe specific transfer flow is illustrated in the figure below.\n\n\n\n\n24\n  A wrapped token is a representation of a value that is locked on another ledger and can be used as a means of payment or a means of\nsettlement on the target ledger.\n\nNorges Bank                                          Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                      17\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 18 ===\nFigure 5: Overview of how money is moved between ledgers. On the core ledger, wholesale CBDC is listed as ‘wNOK’. On\nthe external ledger, the wrapped wholesale CBDC is listed as ‘wNOK*’\n\nWhen a bank wishes to use CBDC in an application on the external ledger, a bridge\noperation is initiated. In a typical scenario, a bank will log into a web interface connected to\nthe core ledger, select the amount and destination address on the external ledger, and\nsend a request to the bridge to ‘move’ the CBDC to the specified address. The bridge then\nstarts a process that broadly follows these steps:\n    1. The bridge first creates an asset reference on the core ledger and locks the relevant\n        wholesale CBDC amount in its custody account. After this operation, the amount\n        remains on the core ledger but is no longer available for normal transactions.\n    2. Once the locking is confirmed on the core ledger, the bridge sends a transaction to\n        the wrapped wholesale CBDC contract on the external ledger, which issues a\n        corresponding wrapped wholesale CBDC amount to the recipient’s address on the\n        external ledger.\n    3. The recipient can now use wrapped wholesale CBDC in applications on the external\n        ledger, for example to purchase zero-coupon bond tokens. The zero-coupon\n        contract receives wrapped wholesale CBDC from the buyer, issues bond tokens,\n        and upon maturity, the contract repays the face value of the bonds to the\n        bondholder’s address in wrapped wholesale CBDC.\nWhen the actor on the external ledger wants to transfer the funds back to the core ledger, a\nreverse transaction flow is executed. The user (or an application on its behalf) sends\nwrapped wholesale CBDC back to the bridge’s address on Besu (a so-called ‘bridge back’\noperation). The bridge validates that the amount received is correct and then performs two\nsteps:\n    1. The bridge calls the wrapped CBDC contract and destroys the received wrapped\n        CBDC amount. After this, the wrapped wholesale CBDC no longer exists on the\n        external ledger.\n\nNorges Bank                                   Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                            18\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 19 ===\n    2. The bridge sends a transaction to the core ledger that unlocks the corresponding\n       wholesale CBDC in the custody account and transfers it back to the recipient’s\n       account on the core ledger.\n\nThe process is built as a coordinated and sequential flow, where the locking of CBDC on\nthe core ledger must be confirmed before wrapped wholesale CBDC can be issued on the\nexternal ledger. If locking on the core ledger is unsuccessful, the process is interrupted and\nno wrapped CBDC is issued. Upon reversal, the wrapped CBDC is destroyed on the\nexternal ledger before the funds are unlocked on the core ledger. If destruction is\nsuccessful but the unlock operation on the core ledger fails, the value remains locked and\nunavailable until the bridge either succeeds on a retry or reconstructs a consistent state\nbased on previous confirmations. Wrapped CBDC cannot remain in circulation without a\nconfirmed lock on the core ledger.\nAs long as the wholesale CBDC amount remains locked on the core ledger, the total\namount of CBDC remains under central bank control, even if the funds are temporarily\nmade available for use on an external ledger in the form of wrapped wholesale CBDC. The\nlocking and issuance mechanism ensures that there is always an unambiguous allocation\nof the amount: either as available CBDC on the core ledger or as locked CBDC on the core\nledger with a corresponding wrapped CBDC on the external ledger. There is therefore no\ndouble-spending or deviation in the total amount of CBDC.\nSince the bridge is a central component with rights to lock funds on the core ledger and\nissue/burn tokens on the external ledger, the technical design is based on a clear division\nof responsibilities and limited rights. On the core ledger, the bridge only has the rights\nneeded to move funds in and out of its own custody account, not to be an issuer of new\nCBDC. On the external ledger, the bridge’s account is set as the owner/administrator of the\nwrapped CBDC contract, with exclusive access to the issuance and destruction functions.\nIt is not possible, except for the bridge, to change the total amount of wrapped wholesale\nCBDC .\nThe bridge’s function means that the central bank does not need to operate or govern the\ninfrastructure on the external ledger. It is sufficient for the bridge to have an account on the\nexternal ledger and access to a node endpoint to send and read transactions. This provides\nflexibility to use CBDC for payments on external ledgers without taking operational\nresponsibility for the entire network of ledgers. This architecture also requires that the\nexternal ledger has a governance and control regime that prevents the contract\nrepresenting wrapped wholesale CBDC from being changed, replaced, or circumvented in\na way that weakens the bridge’s function. Consequently, the technical and operational\n\nNorges Bank                           Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                               19\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 20 ===\ncontrol of the external infrastructure must be assessed before CBDC can be used as a\nmeans of settlement on such a platform.\nThe technical design also entails certain limitations. In this test, the bridge can only\ntransfer wholesale CBDC between the ledgers, not other types of tokens or data. Nor does\nit allow smart contracts to be called directly across the ledgers.\nThe zero-coupon bond contract is an example of a pure application that occurs on the\nexternal ledger and only uses wrapped wholesale CBDC as a means of payment; the logic\nof this contract does not directly affect the core ledger. This is an important distinction\nbetween a bridge used solely for liquidity transfer and more advanced interoperability\nsolutions where contracts on one ledger can trigger contracts on another.\nThe design used in test case 3 is based on the bridge being understandable and\ncontrollable from a business and regulatory perspective. Each step in the flow between the\nledgers can be explained as simple operations: lock, mint (issue), transfer, burn(destroy),\nand unlock. Figure 5 visualises how these steps are connected, making it possible to trace\nthe flow of tokens throughout the entire process, both for technical and non-technical\nreaders. This provides a framework in which the use of CBDC in external applications can\nbe gradually expanded without changing the basic principles of control of the issuance and\nredemption of central bank money.\n\n\n\nResults and findings\nThe tests show that it is possible to maintain unambiguous control over one and the same\nCBDC value even if the funds are made available on an external ledger via a bridge. The\nsolution ensures that the same money is never available in two places at the same time, by\nlocking the CBDC value on the core ledger while issuing a representation of the value on\nthe external ledger, as long as the bridge is functioning correctly.\nThe settlement process in the bridge-based setup worked as intended within the test\nconditions, but did not have the same degree of simultaneity and finality as settlement\nwithin a single shared ledger. When conducting a settlement via a bridge, the process\nconsists of several coordinated steps across ledgers, which can result in temporary\nintermediate states in the event of delays or technical interruptions. In the test, this was\nhandled in a controlled technical environment. Nevertheless, in a real operating\nenvironment, such settlement processes would require continuous operational monitoring\nand active error handling.\n\n\n\nNorges Bank                          Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                20\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 21 ===\nThe bridge solution requires one actor to have privileged access to both systems in order to\nunlock, issue, and destroy CBDC. In this test, this role was assigned to the central bank.\nAlthough the technical operation of the bridge can in theory be carried out by a third party,\nthe solution requires that the central bank has control over and responsibility for the\nfunctions that affect the total amount of central bank money in circulation at all times. The\nissuance and destruction of CBDC must therefore be technically and organisationally\nsubject to the central bank, regardless of who operates the underlying infrastructure. Such\ncentralisation provides clear and technically enforceable control over central bank money\nin circulation. However, it also entails increased responsibility and stricter requirements for\nthe operation, security, and monitoring of the entity managing the bridge.\n\nIn architectures with multiple independent ledgers, interoperability will require some form\nof bridge or equivalent interoperability protocol between the ledgers. When CBDC is mainly\nused as a means of settlement for processes carried out on external ledgers, few of the key\nbenefits of DLT are realised, such as close integration between money and business logic,\natomic interaction, and reduced need for external orchestration. The tests indicate that the\nfull benefits of a blockchain-based CBDC solution require external actors to be able to use\nCBDC directly in their own solutions, so that money, logic, and processes can interact on\nthe same technological platform.\n\n\nTest case 4 — Characteristics of tokenised bank deposits in a multi-\nledger architecture\nThe purpose of the test case\nThe purpose of this test case was to explore the characteristics, advantages, and\nchallenges of a blockchain-based infrastructure where TBD25 are used as a means of\npayment, and where wholesale CBDC was used as the final means of settlement between\nthe banks involved. The test was intended to demonstrate how banks could issue and\nmanage TBD on their own ledger technology, to explore whether TBD could be converted\nsecurely and seamlessly to retail CBDC issued by the central bank, and to highlight the\nadvantages and disadvantages of a structure in which the actors operate on multiple\nledgers. The test case builds on the experiences from test case 3, but with a slightly\ndifferent focus: here, TBD are the means of payment, while wholesale CBDC is used\nexclusively for the interbank settlement.\n\n\n25\n  TBD (tokenised bank deposits): A digital bank deposit issued by a commercial bank as a token on a DLT/ledger, where the token\nrepresents a claim on the bank with the same value as the deposit (typically 1:1)\nNorges Bank                                          Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                    21\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 22 ===\nA key motivation was to explore characteristics in a scenario where the payment system is\nnot built on a single shared ledger for all banks and participants, but where each bank has\ngreater freedom to choose its own technology platform and pace of innovation. This is\nconsistent both with recent international descriptions of ‘unified ledgers’26 and ‘regulated\nliability networks’27, and with a market in which national and international participants\nmust interact across system and jurisdictional boundaries. The test also had an innovation\nobjective: to explore whether bank deposits in tokenised form could enable new processes\nor use cases, including whether banks could offer customers fast real-time conversion\nbetween TBD and retail CBDC across technologies in a secure and controlled manner, and\nhow interbank settlements could be handled when both the central bank’s CBDC and the\nbanks’ TBD systems were part of the same testing environment.\n\nTechnical design\nThe test setup includes mechanisms for issuance and management of TBD at individual\nbanks, conversion between TBD and retail CBDC, and settlement between banks in\nwholesale CBDC. The solution builds on the architecture from test case 3, where the core\nledger for wholesale CBDC and the retail CBDC ledger are connected via a bridge\ncontrolled by the central bank. In test case 4, separate ledgers have also been established\nfor TBD at each commercial bank. The overall architecture is referred to as a ‘Unified\nLedger Sandbox’ and is illustrated in Figure 6.\n\n\n\n\n26\n   BIS ‘Unified Ledger’: Concept for a common, programmable platform where tokenised money (central bank money and bank deposits)\nand tokenised assets can interact, allowing transactions to be automated and settled more seamlessly — preferably with final\nsettlement in central bank money.\n27\n   Regulated Liability Network (RLN): Concept for a regulated (often permissioned) financial infrastructure with a shared ledger that\nrecords, transfers, and settles tokenised liabilities from regulated issuers — typically central bank money, TBD, and e-money — with the\naim of settlement at par and better interaction between institutions.\nNorges Bank                                           Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                       22\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 23 ===\nFigure 6: High-level overview of the implemented solution. Retail CBDC and wholesale CBDC on two different ledgers are\nconnected via a bridge controlled by the central bank. TBD are found on ledgers at commercial banks. Transactions with\nTBD are settled in wholesale CBDC.\n\nFigure 6 shows the core elements of the solution: The wholesale CBDC and retail CBDC are\nlocated on separate ledgers, connected by a bridge that can lock the wholesale CBDC and\nissue the retail CBDC, and vice versa. In addition, each bank has its own TBD ledger where\nthe bank’s customers’ deposits are represented as TBD tokens. Transactions in TBD\nbetween banks are settled in CBDC on the wholesale CBDC ledger. The TBD ledgers are\nimplemented as separate channels in the same distributed network used for wholesale\nCBDC, but each channel is logically separate and controlled by the individual bank; these\nchannels could therefore be considered separate ledgers, see textbox below.\n\n\n\n\nNorges Bank                                    Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                            23\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 24 ===\n Channels in Hyperledger Fabric\n In Hyperledger Fabric, a channel is a logically delimited subnetwork that functions as a\n separate ledger, with its own data, smart contracts, and access rules. Only actors who\n are members of a channel have technical access to information about transactions and\n holdings on the channel.\n\n An organisation can be a member of several channels at the same time. This allows\n several logically separate ledgers to run on a single shared technology platform and to\n segregate sensitive information between actors.\n\n The actor who creates and owns a channel is also responsible for the operational\n management of the channel, including access control, membership, smart contracts,\n and the operation of the associated infrastructure.\n\n In this solution, each bank’s TBD ledger is implemented as a separate channel,\n controlled and operated by the individual bank. The channels are therefore treated\n architecturally as separate ledgers, even though they are technically part of the same\n Fabric\nEach  TBDnetwork.\n           ledger runs its own smart contract that defines how TBD can be issued,\ndestroyed, and transferred between customer accounts. The ledgers are account-based:\neach customer has an identifiable account with a balance in TBD. Only the bank has the\nright to issue and destroy TBD on its own ledger, while transfers between customer\naccounts are made according to rules defined in the smart contract. The central bank is a\nmember at channel level in the sandbox in order to be able to read aggregated data (e.g.,\ntotal outstanding TBD), but has no control rights over the TBD contracts.\n\nThe goal of test case 4 has been to use this setup to explore the characteristics of DLT-\nbased versions of two financial processes:\n\n    •   Conversion from TBD to retail CBDC (comparable to a cash withdrawal from a bank\n        account), and\n\n    •   The interbank settlement for TBD (the settlement in wholesale CBDC between\n        banks, as a result of transfers between customers in different banks).\n\nBoth processes have been implemented so that, technically speaking, they can function\nindependently of which ledger the individual bank has chosen for its TBD ledger. In the\nsandbox, TBD is built on Hyperledger Fabric channels, but the logic for settlement and\nconversion is placed on the wholesale CBDC ledger and in a separate orchestration\ncomponent, so that, in principle, other types of TBD ledgers could be used as long as they\ncan be connected to these components.\n\nNorges Bank                          Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                               24\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 25 ===\nBanks therefore have complete freedom to choose the ledger for their TBD. In doing so, we\nhave deliberately chosen one of the most complex approaches to issuing TBD.\n\n Orchestration of processes in a multi-ledger setup\n In the tested multi-ledger setup, composite processes such as TBD conversion and\n interbank settlement cannot be executed as a single atomic transaction on a single\n ledger. The connection between necessary operations is therefore enforced through\n explicit orchestration in the application layer, outside the ledgers. The orchestration is\n implemented as state-based process logic (finite state machines) and controls the\n order of operations, locking of funds to prevent double spending, bridge calls where\n applicable, and handling of errors and interruptions.\n\n It is important to distinguish the orchestration from the bridge itself.\n\n A bridge is a dedicated technical component that enables the transfer or representation\n of values between ledgers, typically by locking a value on one ledger and issuing a\n corresponding representation on another. The bridge has no business logic and no\n comprehensive understanding of the process it is part of.\n\n Orchestration, on the other hand, is responsible for the overall process and determines\n when and whether irreversible steps should be performed. Settlement in a multi-ledger\n setup is therefore not achieved through the bridge alone, but through a controlled\n orchestrated process in which irreversible operations are only performed when the\n necessary prior confirmations are in place. If errors occur before a defined point in the\n process, the entire process can be interrupted and reversed.\n\n In principle, bridges can be used as part of an orchestration logic. In the tested solution,\n however, the bridge is only used as a value transfer mechanism, while the actual\n orchestration — including sequence governance, locking, and error handling — is\n explicitly located in the application layer outside the bridge.\n\n\nConversion from TBD to retail CBDC\n\nFigure 7 illustrates the conversion from TBD to retail CBDC. The process can be seen as the\nDLT-based parallel to a customer making a cash withdrawal from their bank account.\n\n\n\n\nNorges Bank                           Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                 25\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 26 ===\nFigure 7: Conversion from TBD to retail CBDC\n\nTechnically, the following occurs:\n   1. The customer initiates a withdrawal by asking the bank to convert a given amount of\n      TBD to retail CBDC.\n   2. The bank checks that the customer has a sufficient TBD balance and then locks the\n      relevant TBD amount on the bank’s TBD ledger. This is done using the chaincode on\n      the TBD channel, which changes the customer’s balance so that the amount cannot\n      be used in other transactions while the withdrawal process is ongoing.\n   3. The bank then initiates a settlement transaction on the wholesale CBDC ledger. The\n      bank reduces its reserves in the wholesale CBDC by the same amount that the\n      customer is to receive in the retail CBDC. This reduction is made through a\n      transaction from the bank’s account to a bridge-controlled account, which is part of\n      the bridge mechanism of the retail CBDC ledger.\n   4. The bridge between the wholesale CBDC and retail CBDC ledger locks the\n      wholesale CBDC (wNOK) and issues CBDC on the retail CBDC ledger. The retail\n      CBDC is first credited to the bank’s account on the retail CBDC ledger.\n   5. The bank then transfers the relevant amount in retail CBDC to the customer’s\n      address on the retail CBDC ledger. After this, the customer has a direct claim on the\n      central bank in the form of retail CBDC, and the corresponding TBD amount is\n      permanently reduced on the bank’s TBD ledger.\n\n\n\nNorges Bank                                    Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                 26\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 27 ===\nAt the balance sheet level, this means, as stated in Figure 7, that the bank’s deposits (TBD)\nare reduced and the bank’s reserves (wholesale CBDC) are reduced accordingly. On the\ncentral bank’s balance sheet, the bank’s reserves are reduced, while the holdings of retail\nCBDC increase. This solution ensures that there is always a one-to-one relationship\nbetween the reduction in TBD and the increase in retail CBDC, and that no new money is\ncreated in the process.\n\nInterbank settlement for TBD\nFigure 8 shows how the interbank settlement for TBD is modelled in the sandbox. The\nscenario is that the bank customers transfer TBD between one another across banks, for\nexample from a customer in Bank B to a customer in Bank A. Today, a corresponding\nsettlement takes place by updating the banks’ deposits (customer accounts) in their\nrespective internal ledgers, while the banks’ mutual positions are settled in central bank\nreserves. In test case 4, the same transfers are made as an integrated process.\n\n\n\n\nFigure 8: The interbank settlement for TBD\n\nTechnically, such a credit transfer consists of three main operations on three different\nledgers:\n   1. On Bank B’s TBD ledger: Bank B reduces the customer’s TBD balance by the\n       amount to be sent. In the sandbox implementation, this is done by destroying or\n       locking TBD tokens from the customer’s account, with a reference to a unique\n       settlement ID.\nNorges Bank                                  Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                       27\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 28 ===\n     2. On the TBD ledger of Bank A: Bank A increases the recipient’s TBD balance by the\n        corresponding amount. This can be implemented by issuing new TBD tokens to the\n        recipient’s account, linked to the same settlement ID.\n     3. On the wholesale CBDC ledger: A settlement is carried out in the wholesale CBDC\n        between Bank A and Bank B. Bank B’s reserves in wholesale CBDC are reduced,\n        while Bank A’s reserves are increased by the same amount in an integrated\n        operation.\n\nThese three operations are linked through the settlement process, or in the form of an\norchestrated protocol on the wholesale CBDC ledger. The protocol ensures that there is a\nsingle source of truth28 for the settlement itself in the wholesale CBDC, by storing a\nsettlement transaction with references to the two banks’ TBD transactions (for example, via\nunique transaction IDs). If something goes wrong before the actual CBDC transaction on\nthe wholesale CBDC ledger is completed, the process is not executed. If the CBDC\ntransaction has been completed, the settlement is considered final, and banks must\nensure that their TBD ledgers reflect this.\nIn Figure 8, this is also expressed in balance sheet form: Bank B’s deposits (TBD) are\nreduced, with a corresponding reduction in its wholesale CBDC reserves. Bank A’s deposits\n(TBD) are increased, with a corresponding increase in its wholesale CBDC reserves. On the\ncentral bank’s balance sheet, reserves are transferred from Bank B to Bank A, while the\ntotal amount of wholesale CBDC and TBD in the system as a whole remains unchanged.\nThe solution is deliberately designed so that the logic for the interbank settlement lies on\nthe wholesale CBDC ledger, not in each individual TBD ledger. This means that the\nsettlement process itself can be standardised and controlled centrally, while banks can\nhave a high degree of freedom to choose the technology and structure for their own TBD\nledgers, as long as they can refer to a unique settlement ID and perform\nissuance/destruction or similar operations in accordance with the settlement result.\n\nResults and findings\nIn a multi-ledger setup, the settlement takes place across several independent systems.\nThis means that transactions must be coordinated outside the ledgers themselves, which\ncan result in intermediate states and a lower degree of simultaneity than atomic settlement\nin a single shared ledger. Such solutions therefore require additional mechanisms for\ncoordination and error handling. When involved actors use separate ledgers, the smart\n\n28\n  Single source of truth: In an IT context, this is known as the single source of truth (SSOT) and refers to a single authoritative source that\ndetermines the final truth of information. In this setup, the wholesale CBDC settlement on the wholesale CBDC ledger serves as the\nsingle source of truth.\n\nNorges Bank                                              Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                           28\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 29 ===\ncontracts cannot directly control cross-ledger operations. Coordination must take place\nthrough external mechanisms, such as orchestration or bridges, which increase technical\nand operational complexity compared to a single-ledger architecture.\n\nThe test shows that when each actor has its own ledger, several of the key benefits of\nblockchain technology are reduced. This applies in particular to the possibilities for\ncoherent automation, atomic settlement, and close integration between money and\nbusiness logic, as such features largely require that assets and logic are handled on the\nsame ledger. In multi-ledger setups, these connections must be established through\nexternal orchestration and messaging. This can be compared to how such processes are\nhandled in today’s payment systems, which raises the question of whether improvements\nto existing systems may be a better alternative than implementing a CBDC based on ledger\ntechnology.\n\n\n\nTest case 5 — Settlement of tokenised securities on a single shared\nledger\nThe purpose of the test case\nThe purpose of the test case was to explore whether trading and settlement of tokenised\nsecurities provided significant benefits when all relevant assets and actors operate on a\nsingle shared ledger, compared with the multi-ledger solutions tested. The test was based\non experience from previous test cases, which showed that cross-ledger transactions\nreduce the ability to reap the full advantages of ledger technology and CBDC, particularly in\nterms of atomicity, automation, and effective risk reduction.\n\nThe test case is relevant because the simultaneous transfer of ownership of multiple\nassets on the same ledger both provides atomicity and reduces settlement and\ncounterparty risk. Unlike bridge and multi-ledger solutions, where the settlements take\nplace as coordinated sequential processes, a single shared ledger allows all changes to\ntake place in a single transaction. This enables real-time settlement, high traceability, and\na single source of truth for both money and securities.\n\nThe test also aimed to highlight the new opportunities for automation and efficiency that\narise when financial processes, including order processing, settlement, role validation, and\ncompliance are implemented directly as smart contracts on a single ledger. This includes\nthe ability to mimic netting-like effects in a gross settlement system, reduce liquidity\nrequirements and eliminate the need for bridges and external orchestration between\nseparate systems.\nNorges Bank                          Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                              29\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 30 ===\nAt the same time, the test case was intended to highlight the new challenges posed by a\nsingle-ledger model. When all actors were required to use a single shared ledger, this could\nbe perceived as more intrusive than solutions where each actor had their own ledger.\nIssues related to governance, change management, and division of responsibilities were\ntherefore an important part of the basis for assessment.\n\nTechnical design\n\n\n\n\nFigure 9: Overview of actors and transaction flow on the single ledger. Wholesale CBDC is listed as wNOK, TBD are listed\nas TBD, and securities are listed as SEC.\n\nIn this test, the central bank, banks, brokers, the marketplace, and the investors operate on\na single shared digital ledger. The central bank issues wholesale CBDC, which is used as a\nmeans of settlement between banks, while each bank issues TBD to its own customers.\nWhen two investors trade a security through their brokers, orders are recorded in a shared\norder book on the ledger.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                     Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                              30\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 31 ===\nWhen a buy and sell order are matched in the shared order book, the settlement is initiated\nautomatically. The buyer’s bank debits the customer’s TBD and simultaneously settles the\ntrade in wholesale CBDC with the seller’s bank, which issues new TBD to the seller. At the\nsame time, the ownership of the security is transferred from the seller to the buyer. The\ncustomer does not have direct access to the wholesale CBDC; the settlement in central\nbank money takes place exclusively between the banks. The settlement is implemented as\nan atomic DvP process, where the cash and securities legs are either executed together\nwith final effect, or not executed at all if one step in the process fails. The security therefore\nonly changes ownership if the payment is completed at the same time.\n\n About atomicity\n Atomicity exists when all relevant state changes in a settlement, such as the transfer of\n money and securities, are executed as a single, technically indivisible, simultaneous\n operation, typically on a single ledger or within the same system. The transaction has\n only one final state: completed or not completed. There are no intermediate states\n where only parts of the settlement have been completed.\n\n In coordinated settlements, such as in multi-ledger architectures or when using bridges,\n the settlement is executed as a sequence of dependent operations across systems.\n Such solutions may be functionally interconnected, but do not achieve the same\n technical simultaneity as in atomicity, and require explicit orchestration and error\n handling.\n\n In this report, the term atomicity is used primarily in the former case.\nAccess to the system is restricted to approved actors, and addresses and rights are\nvalidated against a common register. The architecture thus facilitates trading, payment,\nand record-keeping within a single technical platform, with consistent traceability and\nwithout the need for external reconciliation at the moment of settlement.\n\n\n\n\nNorges Bank                            Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                31\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 32 ===\nFigure 10: Overview of how the smart contracts that constitute the system for trading tokenised securities interact.\n\nThe test setup includes a Global Registry as a single source of truth for contract addresses\nand whitelists (approved banks, brokers, and customers). An investor places an order via\nthe Broker contract, which first checks that the customer is approved and that the broker is\nauthorised. The order is forwarded to the Order Book contract, which matches purchases\nand sales and, if there is a match, initiates the settlement by calling the DvP contract.\n\nThe DvP contract is used to enforce an atomic settlement in which both legs are executed\nsimultaneously:\n\n    •    The security leg: The security (issued by the Security Creator Factory and\n         represented in the Security contract) is locked with the seller and prepared for a\n         transfer.\n\n\nNorges Bank                                      Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                             32\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 33 ===\n    •   The cash leg: The buyer’s bank settles the trade in wholesale CBDC with the seller’s\n        bank. The buyer’s bank’s outlay is covered by reducing the customer’s TBD balance,\n        so that the customer’s claim on the bank is cancelled and the corresponding TBD is\n        issued at the receiving bank. The customer never receives wholesale CBDC; the\n        settlement in central bank money takes place exclusively between the banks.\n\nThe settlement in both legs is completed atomically in the same transaction; if a check fails\n(e.g. due to lack of authorisation, insufficient balance, an invalid counterparty, or incorrect\nversion in the Registry), everything is stopped, so that there is no transfer of ownership of\nsecurities and no payment. If all checks pass, the DvP contract calls transferFrom() in the\nSecurity contract so that the ownership is registered to the buyer, and the settlement is\ncompleted.\n\nThe ‘Factory’ pattern is used to issue new securities in a controlled manner (deterministic\naddressing via the Registry and built-in whitelists), mirroring what a production process\nmight look like. Although full AML/KYC/CTF is outside the scope, the test enforces a simple\nvariant: unknown customers or unauthorised brokers are stopped on-chain before orders\nare accepted.\n\nNetting-like effects in a smart contract-based settlement model\n\nIn traditional securities and payment systems, netting is achieved by collecting multiple\ntrades over a period of time in batch settlements and settling only net positions between\nthe participants at fixed times. This reduces liquidity needs, but can also involve\nintermediate counterparty risk, operational complexity, and the need for clearing and\nguarantee mechanisms.\n\nIn the smart contract-based model implemented here, there is no such deferred\nsettlement step. All trades are settled immediately and atomically through the DvP\ncontract. At the same time, the model can provide netting-like effects, particularly in the\nform of reduced liquidity needs, through the way in which multiple transactions and\noperations can be linked together and executed collectively in a single atomic process.\n\nThis is made possible by the fact that the DvP settlement can be surrounded by pre- and\npost-smart contract calls (pre- and post-hooks), which are executed as part of the same\ntransaction. These surrounding calls are shown below in Figure 11. Such calls can, for\nexample, be used to obtain the necessary liquidity, execute related trades, or clean up and\nconsolidate positions after the settlement. Multiple trades, payments, and any liquidity\nsteps can thus be chained together so that only the total net effect materialises on the\nparticipant’s balance, without funds having to be provided gross in advance. The result is\nthat economic effects traditionally associated with netting, particularly reduced liquidity\nNorges Bank                          Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                33\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 34 ===\ncommitment, can be achieved without deferred settlement, batch processing, or\ncounterparty risk. It is therefore more accurate to refer to this as netting-like effects rather\nthan netting in the traditional sense. The settlement remains immediate and final, but the\nsettlement process itself may internally consist of several coordinated steps that either\nsucceed collectively or are not completed at all.\n\n\n\n\nFigure 11: Enhanced DvP contract, where four external smart contract functions — preBuyHook(), preSellHook(),\npostBuyHook(), and postSellHook() — are called as part of the same transaction. The function calls surround the actual\nDvP process, including the transfer of securities and TBD (including CBDC)\n\n\n\n\nResults and findings\nThe testing shows that when tokenized money and securities are handled on a single\nshared ledger, the settlement can be carried out atomically: either both the money and\nsecurities legs are executed simultaneously and with final effect, or the entire transaction\nis cancelled if one part fails. The tested single-ledger architecture thus meets key\nrequirements for coherent and secure settlement, technical enforcement of roles and\naccess, as well as traceability and compliance. Compared to multi-ledger solutions, the\nneed for intermediaries such as bridges and messaging mechanisms is reduced, and the\ntechnical complexity is lower. However, experience from the test shows that several issues\nneed to be clarified. Before such a solution can be implemented, further testing is required\n\nNorges Bank                                     Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                                34\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 35 ===\nin terms of robustness under real load conditions, cybersecurity, contingency planning and\nrecovery in the event of failure, as well as interoperability with foreign and multinational\nsystems. The requirement that all actors must use a single shared ledger also raises\nquestions about flexibility, incentives, and willingness to adopt, especially for actors with\nspecific regulatory or international needs.\n\nThe test also shows that the use of smart contracts on a shared ledger enables the\nenforcement of settlement rules, roles, and access directly in the infrastructure, without\nthe need for external orchestration or manual control steps. Responsibility for the\ninfrastructure points to a shared model, where the central bank is responsible for the\nCBDC and principles for secure settlement, while banks and other actors manage tokens\nrepresenting bank deposits, securities, and other assets. The actual operation of the ledger\nand the core infrastructure may be suitable for joint organisation, ie through a joint venture.\n\nTest case 5 has not had the objective of investigating how privacy considerations are taken\ninto account. However, the test has highlighted structural limitations in single-ledger\narchitectures based on EVM-compatible platforms, such as Hyperledger Besu. Privacy is\nmainly based on pseudonymity, which does not provide adequate protection if\nunauthorised parties can link addresses to identities. Role and access control in smart\ncontracts limits the actions that actors can perform, but does not protect against analysis\nof transaction patterns at the ledger level. Experience from other test cases indicates that\nalternative DLT platforms, such as Hyperledger Fabric, have a better technical basis for\nconfidentiality through more granular access control for data visibility. Additional privacy\nmeasures, such as anonymisation layers or layered architectures, have not been explored\nin this test.\n\n\n\nTest case 6 — Issuance of government bonds on a single shared ledger\nThe purpose of the test case\nThe purpose of this test was to determine whether the entire life cycle of government\nbonds could be tokenised and recorded on a single shared ledger and executed using\nsmart contracts. The entire transaction chain, from issuance via an auction, trading on the\nsecondary market, coupon payments, buybacks, and redemption at maturity, was handled\nthrough smart contracts. Government bonds, TBD, and wholesale CBDC were represented\nas separate tokens. The settlement was to be carried out atomically — so that either both\nthe settlement of money and securities was carried out simultaneously, or none of the\ntransfers of ownership are carried out.\n\nNorges Bank                           Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                              35\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 36 ===\nTokenised securities are often highlighted as a use case with great potential. The idea\nbehind the test was to explore:\n\n    1. Whether it would be possible to easily expand the ledger to handle the issuance and\n       life cycle of government bonds.\n    2. What advantages and drawbacks did tokenised securities offer issuers and traders.\n\n\n\nTechnical design\nThe solution is designed as a hybrid model where parts of the process are handled on\nledgers (on-chain) using smart contracts, while certain operations are carried out off-chain\nfor practical and regulatory reasons. This applies in particular to bid opening and allocation\nof securities in the auction process, which is not fully automated on the ledger.\n\n\n\n\nFigure 12: Overview of smart contract architecture for tokenised government bonds\n\nFigure 12 shows an overview of how the smart contract system for tokenised government\nbonds is implemented in the sandbox. The entire system is controlled by a central\nNorges Bank                                    Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                    36\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 37 ===\ncomponent, ‘BondManager’, which handles the initiation of new auctions, final deadline\nfor submitting bids, allocation of securities, and finally the settlement of the money and\nsecurity leg. This contract calls on other, more specialised contracts, which handle\nsettlement, for example.\n\nThe overview in Figure 12 shows four main processes in the auction cycle for government\nbonds. These include the establishment of the auction, the submission and registration of\nbids, the closing and resolution of the auction (including the decision on which bids are\naccepted), and the execution of allocation, issuance of bond tokens, and the associated\nsettlement. In addition, there is a mechanism for extending an existing loan (ISIN), but this\nis not shown in the figure.\n\nThe auction process begins with the central bank creating a new auction through the API\ninterface. This part is initiated outside the ledger. The API interface then activates the\nrelevant smart contracts, which create both new bond tokens and the auction itself on the\nledger. Once this is in place, the auction is opened for bids from the primary dealers.\n\nDuring the bidding phase, the primary dealers seal their bids using both the central bank’s\nand their own encryption keys. This takes place outside the ledger to ensure confidentiality,\nwhile allowing the bids to be verified later. The sealed bids are then submitted to the\nauction on the ledger.\n\nWhen the bid deadline has been met, the central bank closes the auction through the API\ninterface. The API then triggers the ‘BondAuction’ smart contract, which closes the auction\non the ledger and prevents new bids from being received. After the auction is closed, the\ncentral bank opens the sealed bids through the API interface and verifies their content. This\npart of the process also takes place outside the ledger.\n\nIn the final phase, the central bank assesses whether the bids should be accepted. Via the\nAPI interface, the auction can either be completed or cancelled if the bids are not to be\naccepted. The result of the auction is published as part of this process.
