When the auction\nis completed, the API interface activates the BondManager smart contract, which initiates\nthe settlement and allocation of the bond tokens through the underlying smart contracts\non the ledger.\n\nFinally, the API interface creates a time-triggered workflow outside the ledger. This\nworkflow is automatically activated when it is time for coupon payment on the bond.\n\n\n\nExtension of an existing bond:\n\n\nNorges Bank                          Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                               37\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 38 ===\nIf the central bank wishes to add volume to an existing loan (ISIN), almost the same routine\nas when creating an auction is followed. The extension is posted as an auction and follows\nthe auction process mentioned above. An important difference is that the ISIN is retained,\nand the coupon rate is unchanged.\n\n\n\n\nFigure 13 Process diagram for the issuance and auction of tokenised bonds\n\n\n\n\nNorges Bank                                    Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                   38\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 39 ===\nCoupon payments\n\nIn order to execute coupon payments and redemptions at maturity, the smart contracts\nmust have access to externally generated overviews of the bondholders. This is because\nthe contracts themselves do not identify all the addresses that are entitled to\ndisbursement at any given time. These ownership lists are therefore produced outside the\nledger and used as the basis for the operations that are then performed on the ledger.\n\nThe model in Figure 14 is a simplified representation of the current bond cycle. Coupon\npayments are repeated at fixed intervals until the bond reaches its maturity date.\n\nWhen the time for a coupon payment arrives, the process begins with the central bank\ncalling the BondAPI for the relevant bond, identified by its ISIN. The API then activates the\nsmart contract function payCoupon() in BondManager. At the same time, the API ensures\nthat there is an up-to-date overview of who owns the bond at this point in time, so that the\ndisbursement goes to the correct recipients.\n\nThe BondToken contains key information about the bond, including the maturity date, the\ninterval between coupon payments, and a counter showing how many coupons have\nalready been paid. BondManager retrieves this information and then calculates how large\nthe coupon should be per unit. A unit is linked to a nominal amount, for example NOK\n1,000, and the coupon rate is the one set at the original interest rate auction.\n\nBondManager then reviews all registered bondholders. For each holder, the holdings in\nBondToken are checked and the individual coupon payment is calculated based on how\nmany units the holder owns. Once the amount has been determined, a transfer is\ninstructed from a dedicated ‘gov’ account (represented by TBD) to the relevant bondholder.\nIn this way, each owner receives the coupon payment corresponding to their own holdings.\n\nThe entire coupon payment is defined as atomic. This means that either all disbursements\nare made collectively, or the entire operation is reversed if something goes wrong. This\nreduces the risk of partial or inconsistent disbursements.\n\nOnce all owners have received their coupon payments, the BondManager checks against\nthe BondToken to ensure that the total coupon paid out matches the total bond holdings in\nthe system. The counter in BondToken is then updated so that the system registers that the\ncurrent coupon period has been completed. If this was the last coupon payment according\nto the bond structure, BondManager calls a function in BondToken that marks the bond as\nmatured.\n\n\nNorges Bank                          Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                  39\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 40 ===\nFinally, the API sends a status message back to the central bank. This confirms where the\nbond is in its term and whether it has now reached maturity.\n\nRedemption\n\nWhen the bond has reached maturity and all coupons have been paid, the central bank\nstarts the redemption phase, cf. Figure 14. The central bank calls the BondAPI against a\nseparate endpoint for redemption, which in turn activates the BondManager function\nredeem(). BondToken maintains a status (isMature) that shows that the bond has reached\nmaturity, and this status is used by BondManager as the basis for redemption.\n\nThe BondManager then retrieves relevant information from the BondToken, including what\nis needed to determine what the bondholders will receive upon redemption. In theory, the\namount can be calculated based on the remaining payments on the bond. When the bond\nhas matured, the principal shall be repaid, possibly together with the last coupon payment.\n\nFurthermore, the BondManager reviews all remaining bondholders and verifies each\nindividual holding in BondToken. The redemption settlement itself takes place via the\nBondDvP component, so that payment and cancellation of the bond tokens take place\nsimultaneously. The BondManager instructs BondDvP to carry out the settlement,\nwhereupon BondDvP calls BondToken to redeem the owner’s holdings through\nredeemFor(holder, balance). In practice, this means that the bond tokens are removed\nfrom the owner’s address. At the same time, the settlement amount is transferred from the\n‘gov’ account (TBD) to the same owner.\n\nThe redemption flow is atomic. Once the redemption flow is completed, the BondManager\nverifies that the bond tokens are destroyed. The bond is then fully redeemed and closed in\nthe system. The BondAPI then sends a status report to the central bank confirming that the\nredemption has been completed.\n\n\n\n\nNorges Bank                         Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                               40\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 41 ===\nFigure 14: Process diagram for coupon payments to owners of tokenised government bonds\n\n\n\n\nResults and findings\nThe test shows that a ledger can be used to manage large parts of the life cycle of\ngovernment bonds, from issuance and trading to coupon payments, buybacks, and\nredemption at maturity. Important state changes and settlements can be automated\nthrough smart contracts, which provides good traceability, control, and efficiency. At the\nsame time, the test shows that certain key processes still need to be handled outside the\nledger, in particular the opening and allocation of bids in auctions, as well as the\nidentification of current bondholders prior to coupon payments and redemption.\n\nAtomic DvP has been implemented in line with its purpose. This means that ownership of\nsecurities and payments are settled simultaneously, and that the entire transaction is\nNorges Bank                                  Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                       41\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 42 ===\ncancelled if one part fails. This reduces the counterparty risk and appears to be a strength\nof the solution. The auction process is conducted with encrypted bids (‘sealed bids’),\nwhere bids can only be read outside the ledger, while the allocation decision is recorded on\nthe ledger with a signed cryptographic proof. This provides good verifiability, but does not\nprovide complete assurance that the allocation is automatically correct in relation to all\nbids.\n\nThe solution could strengthen the integrity, control, and rights management in auction\nimplementation, but some processes in the test are not fully automated and need to be\nsupported by external routines (off-chain). Confidentiality and privacy on the ledger have\nbeen improved through encrypted bids and strict access control, but certain information,\nsuch as activity patterns, timing, and number of bids, can still be observed on the ledger.\nThe findings thus illustrate the need for a balance between what should be handled on the\nledger and what should still be resolved outside it.\n\n\n\nSummary of findings\nThe technical testing has provided practical insight into how CBDC based on ledger\ntechnology can be used in payment and settlement processes, and how architecture\nchoices largely determine the benefits, limitations, and role of CBDC. Across the test\ncases, architecture emerges as the most important factor determining which capabilities\ncan be realised with this technology.\n\nA key finding concerns the distinct characteristics of single-ledger and multi-ledger\narchitectures. When money, assets, and business logic are handled on a single shared\nledger, settlements can be executed simultaneously and atomically. This enables\nintegrated DvP, reduces counterparty risk, and makes it possible to enforce rules, roles,\nand rights directly in the infrastructure using smart contracts. In such setups, CBDC serves\nas a functionally integrated part of the processes.\n\nIn multi-ledger architectures, where assets and applications are distributed across\nseparate ledgers, the link between transactions must instead be established through\nbridges, messaging, or external orchestration. The tests show that this leads to greater\ntechnical and operational complexity, reduced simultaneity, and increased settlement risk.\nAt the same time, the tests indicate that several key benefits of ledger technology,\nparticularly those related to close integration between money and business logic, are not\nrealised to the same extent as in single-ledger architectures.\n\n\n\nNorges Bank                         Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                             42\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 43 ===\nThe testing also shows that when settlement and associated logic are consolidated on a\nsingle ledger, multiple operations can form part of a single atomic process. This can, for\nexample, yield efficiency and liquidity gains, including netting-like effects, without deferred\nsettlement or intermediate counterparty risk. At the same time, the tests indicate that\ncertain functions and processes are not suitable for execution directly on the ledger, and\nthat an appropriate architecture will therefore involve a deliberate combination of on-chain\nand off-chain components.\n\nThe tests have mainly focused on technical feasibility and conceptual properties. Issues\nsuch as performance under load, handling of errors and deviations outside the ‘happy\npath’, operational management over time, security, contingency, and interaction among a\nlarger number of actors have received limited attention. Further work should also address\nhow requirements for anonymity and privacy can be better met, for example through\nlayered architectures or the use of secondary layers (layer 2) above a core ledger. These\nconditions should therefore be a focus for further testing, as well as specific applications\nwhere CBDC and ledger technology can provide added value beyond replicating current\nstructures. This could include testing interaction in broader ecosystems, assessing\nincentives and governance models, and exploring architectures that enable genuine\nintegration of money, assets, and processes.\n\nPublishing source code\nAvailability of Source Code and Use of the Sandbox Solutions\n\nThe source code underlying the sandbox solutions and test cases described in this report\nwill be made publicly available as open source. The purpose is to promote transparency,\nreproducibility, and knowledge sharing, and to enable other stakeholders to use and\nexplore the source code for their own testing.\n\nThe code has been developed to support the specific test cases and experiments\nconducted in the project and is tailored to simplified processes and controlled sandbox\nenvironments. The repositories can be forked and run locally in dedicated development\nenvironments, allowing interested parties to set up sandbox solutions and test\nfunctionality and architectural choices on their own machines. The code may also serve as\na starting point for further experimentation or for comparison with alternative technical\nsolutions. The source code will be made available in Norges Banks Github repository29.\n\n\n\n\n29\n     See Github (2026).\nNorges Bank                           Norges Bank Papers 3/2026                                43\n\n\n=== DOC 7 PAGE 44 ===\nLimitations and disclaimers\n\nThe code is not intended for use in production environments and must not be used as\nthe basis for real-world payment, settlement, or securities systems. Comprehensive\nassessments of security, robustness, performance, privacy, or regulatory compliance have\nnot been carried out, and deliberate simplifications have been made in both architecture\nand implementation to enable efficient experimental testing.\n\nAll use of the code is at the user’s own risk. Any further use or development requires\nindependent assessments of technical, security, legal, and regulatory aspects.\n\n\n\nReferences\nBIS (2025) Annual Economic Report 2025, Chapter III.
